    The Browns also moved on from veteran running back Jerome Ford, the third member of last season's rotation. Their only additions at the position were undrafted rookies. Ford's departure should primarily benefit Sampson, though it could also create more receiving opportunities for Judkins.

    Judkins' rookie season ended early because of a dislocated ankle and fractured fibula. He participated in voluntary minicamp in April and OTAs in June, with all signs pointing toward him playing in Week 1. There is at least some question about whether he can perform to his usual standard early in the season.

    If he returns to form, the better offensive line and expanded workload could position Judkins for a top-20 fantasy season. If the Browns‘ offense and offensive line outperform expectations, Judkins has top-10 upside, though that is unlikely this year. As long as he stays healthy, he should see enough volume to be worth starting in fantasy lineups each week.

    42. WR Tee Higgins, Cincinnati Bengals

    Higgins has been a significant part of the Bengals' passing attack in each of the past six seasons. His PFF receiving grade has hovered between 77.9 and 83.8 in most seasons, but his fantasy value has benefited from catching passes from Joe Burrow and from a poor defense that led Cincinnati to throw more than most teams.

    His targets per route tied a career low at 18.7% last season, though that was partially due to Burrow's injury. With Burrow at quarterback, that figure was 21.3%, around league average. Higgins’ role also shifted last season toward more of a deep threat, regardless of who was throwing. His average depth of target hit a career-high 13.9 yards, and his 23.5% deep-target rate was his highest since his rookie season.

    The Bengals kept the same offensive coaching staff from last year, and nearly all of their offensive players return, aside from a few Day 3 draft picks who will be backups. That is remarkable continuity, which narrows Higgins’ range of outcomes relative to most wide receivers.

    He ranked 25th in receiving yards and tied for second in touchdowns, which led to the 15th-most fantasy points. Six of his touchdowns came on plays of 25 yards or more, four of them from Joe Flacco. That kind of long-touchdown production tends to regress toward the mean from one season to the next.

    Two seasons ago, Higgins was also among the league leaders in receiving touchdowns, but most of those came on passes from within 5 yards of the end zone. That year, Cincinnati's wide receivers caught a ridiculous 14 touchdowns from that range, a total that fell to four last season.

    Burrow, the Bengals‘ offense and his talent should still produce a good number of touchdowns, but expecting double digits again is unrealistic. That touchdown regression, combined with his injury history, suggests he shouldn't be drafted at his current ADP of WR15, which is where he finished last season in fantasy points.

    43. WR Terry McLaurin, Washington Commanders

    McLaurin has spent the last seven seasons as the Commanders' lead wide receiver. He typically finished between WR16 and WR29 during his first five seasons. The addition of Jayden Daniels, along with a ridiculous contested catch rate and a high-end deep-target rate, led him to finish as the overall WR7 in 2024.

    Expectations weren't as high for McLaurin heading into 2025. Touchdown regression was inevitable, and Washington added Deebo Samuel, giving McLaurin his most significant competition for targets during his time in Washington. The season went much worse than expected, as both McLaurin and Daniels dealt with injuries throughout the year. McLaurin missed seven games and finished 37th in fantasy points per game among wide receivers.

    Expectations weren't as high for McLaurin heading into 2025. Touchdown regression was inevitable, and Washington added Deebo Samuel, giving McLaurin his most significant competition for targets during his time in Washington. The season went much worse than expected, as both McLaurin and Daniels dealt with injuries throughout the year. McLaurin missed seven games and finished 37th in fantasy points per game among wide receivers.

    The one interesting wrinkle is that Washington has a new offensive coordinator in David Blough. There are reports that McLaurin could move around the formation more often and not play so strictly as an X receiver. He has the third-most routes lined up on the outside on the line of scrimmage since 2019, and the 60th-most on the outside off the line. The Commanders are also the favorite to land Brandon Aiyuk once he becomes available, and Aiyuk has also historically lined up as an X receiver. More snaps on the move could lead to easier targets for some free fantasy points, but the addition of Aiyuk could pull McLaurin away from some of the targets that make him special.

    This makes McLaurin a little more of a high-risk, high-reward option compared to past years, when he was one of the safer fantasy starting options.

    44. RB Cam Skattebo, New York Giants

    Skattebo started his career third on the Giants' depth chart, but was playing over 50% of snaps by Week 2. A Week 3 Tyrone Tracy injury allowed Skattebo to start in Week 4. He averaged 19.2 fantasy points per game over his next four starts on 19 carries per game, even with Tracy returning two weeks later. He suffered a season-ending injury in Week 8, which included a dislocated ankle, a fractured fibula and a ruptured deltoid ligament.

    He was one of several rookie running backs to struggle because of offensive line play. His 1.0 yards before contact per attempt was tied for eighth-lowest among 49 running backs with at least 100 attempts. He averaged a first down or touchdown on 26.7% of his carries, ranking ninth-best, despite the line. His 0.22 avoided tackles per carry also ranked among the top 10 running backs.

    New York underwent several offensive changes, led by the addition of head coach John Harbaugh. They spent a first-round pick on Francis Mauigoa, who will ideally help the offensive line. The good news is New York didn't add anyone to the backfield outside of an undrafted rookie, despite having reported interest in Jeremiah Love. This means Skattebo should be the clear leader of the backfield, as long as he's healthy. Both Harbaugh and offensive coordinator Matt Nagy have a long history of using committees in the backfield, so we shouldn't expect Skattebo to dominate playing time.

    The major concern is his injury recovery. He was able to take some work in team drills during OTAs, and all signs point to him playing in Week 1. However, just because he can physically play football doesn't necessarily mean he will be able to play at the same level he did last season. This makes Skattebo a high-risk, high-reward RB2. He could return to form and see usage similar to Derrick Henry, which could make him more of an RB1, but he might also not be the same until 2027, in which case he would lose touches to Tyrone Tracy Jr.

    45. RB Bucky Irving, Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    Irving has quietly established himself as one of the NFL's most productive running backs over the past two seasons. His 87.0 PFF overall grade ranks 10th among running backs, and he joins Bijan Robinson, Josh Jacobs and Jahmyr Gibbs as the only backs to earn both PFF rushing and receiving grades above 85.0 during that span. He also averaged 20.3 touches per game last season, the fourth-highest mark among running backs.

    Normally, that profile would place a player firmly in the first or second round of fantasy drafts, but a few concerns are suppressing Irving’s ADP.

    The most legitimate is Tampa Bay's addition of Kenneth Gainwell. He received a stronger free-agent contract than several notable backs, including Tyler Allgeier, Rico Dowdle, Chris Rodriguez Jr., Keaton Mitchell and Rachaad White. New offensive coordinator Zac Robinson has also spoken positively about Gainwell's projected role, making it clear he will be involved in Tampa Bay’s offense.

    Still, Gainwell has earned just a 69.3 PFF grade over the past two seasons. His presence is enough to cap Irving's ceiling. He impressed coaches throughout OTAs, which has lifted his ADP while hurting Irving's. Even so, all indications are that a healthy Irving remains the team's top back.

    There are also some durability concerns after Irving battled foot and shoulder injuries last season. Encouragingly, recent reports suggest he should be healthy by training camp. Compared to other running backs in a similar ADP range, such as Quinshon Judkins and Cam Skattebo, Irving's health outlook appears less concerning.

    Robinson's arrival could further boost Irving's fantasy outlook. Falcons running backs combined for 1,435 receiving yards over the past two seasons under Robinson, suggesting both Irving and Gainwell should remain active in the passing game. Last season, Sean Tucker frequently handled Tampa Bay's goal-line work, limiting Irving's touchdown upside. Robinson handled those situations differently in Atlanta, splitting snaps at the 1-yard line evenly between Bijan Robinson and Tyler Allgeier despite Allgeier's larger frame. That approach should create more goal-line opportunities for Irving in 2026, even if touches near the end zone remain shared.

    If Irving stays healthy, he has the talent and workload potential to remain a reliable fantasy starter, even while sharing part of the backfield with Gainwell.

    46. RB D'Andre Swift, Chicago Bears

    Swift has spent the last two seasons as the Bears' starting running back. He was a low-end RB2 in 2024 at RB21, averaging 12.6 fantasy points per game. He accomplished this with high volume and low efficiency. Chicago invested heavily in the offensive line in the offseason. That, combined with Ben Johnson's offense, helped him to 1.9 yards before contact per attempt, ranking fourth-best among running backs. That room helped set him up to accomplish more after contact, leading to a solid 3.0 yards after contact per attempt.

    His fantasy value only moved up to 17th in fantasy points per game at 14.3. The problem is he received more competition for snaps from seventh-round rookie Kyle Monangai. Swift averaged 66.1% of offensive snaps in 2024, but had only two games above that mark last season: Week 1 and Week 4. His average dropped to 57.8% of snaps.

    The most important parts of the Bears' offense remain intact, but a few changes could affect Swift. Center Drew Dalman retired after being the Bears' second-highest-graded player in run blocking with a 74.0 grade. His replacement is Garrett Bradbury, who posted a 55.9 grade last season and has a career-high 70.5 run block grade. Chances are the quality of run blocking in front of Swift will decline in the short term. Last season, Swift, Monangai and Caleb Williams accounted for 92.9% of the Bears' rushing attempts. The additions of third-round rookie Zavion Thomas and veteran Kalif Raymond could lead to more wide receiver carries, leaving fewer for the running backs.

    Swift has more wear and tear on him than the typical starting running back, and he's at an age where we shouldn't expect much improvement. We can expect a slight decline, which leaves him as a low-end RB2. There is some minor risk that Monangai improves to the point where he further cuts into Swift's playing time, but Swift graded notably better as a runner, receiver and pass blocker, making a significant change in snaps unlikely.

    No running back is truly no-risk, given the general injury rates at the position, but Swift is as safe an RB2 option as you can find after the first few rounds.

    47. TE Colston Loveland, Chicago Bears

    Loveland was a rare tight end selected among the first 10 picks of the NFL Draft. He had a slow start to his career, recording 40 receiving yards or fewer in each of his first six games before breaking out for 118 yards and two touchdowns on six receptions against the Cincinnati Bengals.

    He particularly excelled over the final four weeks, including the playoffs, drawing at least nine targets in each of those games while averaging 94 yards and scoring twice. He was also the only tight end with four 90-yard games on the year, postseason included, while Trey McBride had three and several others had two.

    His strong playoff performance helped him to an 86.2 receiving grade, the best mark among tight ends with at least 75 targets. Loveland should be able to build off that 2025 momentum. Chicago moved on from two of its top four players in targets, DJ Moore and Olamide Zaccheaus, and replaced them with Kalif Raymond, who will be 32 before the season, and third-round rookie Zavion Thomas. That should open the door for Loveland to see more consistent targets going forward.

    Loveland has the upside to be the top overall tight end, given his talent, health and Ben Johnson‘s offense. But that production was largely concentrated over a two-month stretch, making him more likely to regress than Brock Bowers and McBride, who have shown more sustained success. That is the main reason he is not in the top tier of tight ends.

    48. QB Drake Maye, New England Patriots

    Maye delivered one of the best sophomore seasons by a quarterback in the past two decades. The only other quarterbacks to earn a PFF grade of 90.0 or higher in their second season were Patrick Mahomes, Lamar Jackson, Joe Burrow and Justin Herbert, and among that group Maye posted the third-highest passing grade and the third-highest rushing grade.

    That performance translated directly into fantasy production, with Maye ranking second among quarterbacks in total fantasy points and third in points per game. The Patriots retained most of their offense and added A.J. Brown and Romeo Doubs at wide receiver, giving Maye a significantly stronger supporting cast than he had a year ago. For a player of his age and résumé, there is a strong case to make him the top overall asset in dynasty formats.

    That said, there are reasons to account for some regression. Quarterbacks who consistently finish inside the top three in fantasy points tend to produce elite rushing numbers, and six of the nine top-three fantasy finishes from 2022 through 2024 came from quarterbacks who rushed for at least 500 yards. Maye totaled 409 in 2025, and it is possible that one or two quarterbacks with heavier rushing workloads provide a higher weekly ceiling. His passing efficiency metrics also point toward at least modest normalization, as his 8.9 yards per attempt led the league while his 6.3% touchdown rate and 1.6% interception rate are both difficult to sustain.

    One common argument against Maye centered on schedule strength in 2025, with several of his best games coming against favorable defenses. However, he averaged 21.9 fantasy points per game across five matchups against top-eight defenses in fewest fantasy points allowed, with only one poor outing in that stretch against Buffalo, which is a meaningful counterpoint to the schedule concerns.

    49. WR Jaylen Waddle, Denver Broncos

    Waddle spent his first two seasons as a top-15 fantasy wide receiver before his value dipped slightly in 2023 despite a career-high 90.6 PFF receiving grade, due in part to injury and in part to Tyreek Hill's dominant season. He had a down 2024 but ranked 16th in fantasy points among wide receivers through the first 16 weeks before a Week 17 injury ended his season. His receiving grade bounced back to 85.2 in 2025, the second-highest of his career.

    Denver paid a significant price to acquire him, sending the 30th overall pick and additional selections to Miami. Waddle immediately strengthens the Broncos' chances of reaching the Super Bowl, but the more relevant question for fantasy is how much value he will have in Sean Payton's offense.

    Payton's top fantasy wide receivers have typically been bigger, more physical players: Marques Colston at 6'5" and 231 pounds, Michael Thomas at 6'3" and 212 pounds, and more recently Courtland Sutton at 6'4" and 216 pounds. Most of the smaller receivers who started in Payton's offense, including Devery Henderson, Lance Moore, Willie Snead IV, Ted Ginn Jr., Jerry Jeudy and Marvin Mims Jr., failed to develop into reliable fantasy starters. Brandin Cooks is the notable exception, finishing as a borderline WR1 in 2015 and 2016.

    That said, those Saints teams were built around Drew Brees and had less receiver depth beyond Cooks and Thomas before his peak. This Broncos roster is a different animal: Denver had a top-six defense by both PFF grade and points allowed last season and figures to be more run-heavy than those Saints teams were. The wide receiver room also includes Pat Bryant, Troy Franklin and Mims alongside Waddle and Sutton.

    The most likely outcome is that Waddle leads the team in targets while Sutton leads in receiving touchdowns, with overall passing volume in Denver running below most offenses because the Broncos will frequently be winning games on the strength of their defense. There is a ceiling where Waddle meets or exceeds ADP, but that scenario requires Denver to play from behind more than expected, and Waddle to become a red zone favorite, despite his size.

    50. WR Parker Washington, Jacksonville Jaguars

    Washington was a sixth-round pick by Jacksonville in 2023. He barely played during the first two-thirds of his rookie season, but an injury to Christian Kirk gave him an opportunity to become the team's slot receiver down the stretch. He posted six receptions for 61 yards and a touchdown in his first game with notable playing time but was quiet the rest of the season.

    Jacksonville drafted Brian Thomas Jr. and added Gabe Davis, and Kirk returned for 2024, pushing Washington to fourth on the depth chart. Kirk and Davis then missed most of the second half of the season, elevating Washington to the second option, where he played in both two- and three-receiver sets. He posted three games of at least 50 yards late in the year.

    The Jaguars drafted Travis Hunter, who took over for Kirk in the slot, and for a third straight season, Washington stepped into the primary slot role midseason. This time, the opportunity came in a Liam Coen offense. He graded notably better throughout the season, and his production exploded over the final four weeks, including the playoffs, when he caught 26 passes for 454 yards and three touchdowns. His opportunities as an outside receiver also grew, and he graded well in that role. His 85.1 receiving grade ranked in the top 10 among wide receivers.

    Washington returns to a crowded receiver room alongside Thomas, Jakobi Meyers and Hunter. The Jaguars also drafted two tight ends, suggesting more 12 personnel going forward. Washington and Thomas are expected to be their top two options.

    Given Coen's history with elite fantasy wide receivers, Washington's ceiling is sky-high. The more likely outcome, though, is a balanced offense, where some weeks Washington posts a 100-yard game and other weeks a different receiver does. That is a frustration for fantasy managers but a benefit to the Jaguars' offense.

    51. WR Jameson Williams, Detroit Lions

    Williams joined the Detroit Lions as an early first-round pick in 2022, where an ACL tear during the NCAA National Championship Game left him limited in his rookie season. He was a part-time player in 2023 and became full-time in 2024.

    Williams was the definition of a high-risk, high-reward wide receiver option. He had three games with over 120 receiving yards and a touchdown, but also had five games with fewer than 40 receiving yards. That resulted in 14.1 PPR points per game, 26th-most for wide receivers.

    His targets per route decreased in 2025, but his snap rate jumped from 80% to 90%, he didn't miss a game, and the Lions' game scripts were more pass-heavy, leading to more routes by Williams. This ultimately led to more targets, receptions and yards. He finished 12th in fantasy points and 21st in fantasy points per game. He remained frustrating, with four 100-yard, one-touchdown games and three games with single-digit receiving yards.

    His role changed over the last two seasons from a deep threat to more of a receiver who could dominate after the catch. His 7.6 yards after the catch per catch over the last two seasons was fourth-best among wide receivers with at least 100 targets. The three wide receivers ahead of him had notably lower average target depths, making Williams' rate all the more impressive.

    Total volume will remain the problem for Williams, with Amon-Ra St. Brown, Sam LaPorta and Jahmyr Gibbs all worthy of being among the league leaders in touches at their position. Williams is right at the borderline of WR2 and WR3. He should be safe for another 1,000-yard season with a handful of touchdowns.

    52. WR DJ Moore, Buffalo Bills

    Moore has spent the past eight seasons as a fantasy-relevant option at wide receiver. He has finished among the top 36 fantasy wide receivers in all eight seasons, including four inside the top 18, though with just one top-10 finish. He has done this despite subpar quarterback play. Last season, Caleb Williams posted a 71.1 passing grade, the best mark of any quarterback he has played with.

    Moore was traded to the Buffalo Bills, where his quarterback will be Josh Allen, who has posted a passing grade of at least 80.0 in each of the past six seasons. That is a good sign he could have a better season than in recent years. In Buffalo, he also reunites with head coach Joe Brady, who was the Carolina Panthers' offensive coordinator in 2020 and 2021, when Moore was their top receiver. Two of his top four seasons in receiving yards came under Brady.

    Moore is expected to be the Bills' X receiver, the role he played in Carolina and the one Keon Coleman had been filling. Khalil Shakir stays in the slot, Dalton Kincaid is the receiving tight end and Josh Palmer takes the Z. That gives Moore some competition for targets, but not as much as he faced from the trio of Rome Odunze, Luther Burden III and Colston Loveland in Chicago last season.

    Several signs point up for Moore, but he posted a career-low 66.8 offensive grade last season, along with career lows in most other metrics. Since Brady became Buffalo's playcaller, he has spread the ball around more than most, making it harder for any one receiver to stay fantasy-relevant. The Bills' most notable receiver over the past three seasons was Stefon Diggs. In Diggs' final Buffalo season, he averaged 81.4 receiving yards per game before Brady took over and 45.1 afterward.

    That puts Moore in the WR3 conversation, generally the highest-upside receivers who also carry a fair share of risk. He sits near the top of that group, given his long track record of finishing as a WR3 at worst.

    53. RB David Montgomery, Houston Texans

    Montgomery has quietly remained one of the NFL's most productive running backs over the past two seasons. His 86.6 PFF grade ranks 11th among all running backs during that span. The issue in Detroit was never his performance; it was the presence of Jahmyr Gibbs, whose grade ranked fifth at the position.

    Montgomery has already proven capable of handling a feature workload dating back to his Bears tenure, when he played 74.3% of offensive snaps in 2020, though his snap share has gradually declined since.

    Houston traded for Montgomery this offseason and immediately revised his contract, paying him roughly in line with the league's average starting running back. With Nick Chubb no longer on the roster, Montgomery's primary competition for touches comes from Woody Marks, who averaged just 3.6 yards per carry last season. That should leave Montgomery positioned for a significant workload.

    The Texans' defensive strength should also work in his favor. Houston fielded the NFL's second-highest-graded defense last season while allowing the league's second-fewest points, creating more opportunities to play with a lead or remain in favorable game scripts for the running game.

    The biggest concern is the offensive line. Montgomery spent his two seasons in Detroit running behind a consistently top-12 run-blocking unit, while Houston's offensive line ranked 21st in PFF run-blocking grade last year. That was still an improvement over previous seasons, and the Texans made several attempts to upgrade the unit this offseason. Veteran right tackle Braden Smith was added after posting three seasons with a PFF run-blocking grade above 86.0, although he fell to a career-low 58.0 last season. If Smith rebounds even partially toward his previous level, Houston's line should improve noticeably. The Texans also invested a first-round pick in Keylan Rutledge.

    Even if the line remains inconsistent, Montgomery is better equipped than most backs to overcome poor blocking. Over the past two seasons, he has averaged 2.0 yards per carry on rushing attempts in which first contact occurred at or behind the line of scrimmage, tied for the sixth-best mark among running backs.

    If Montgomery secures a true lead-back role in the range of 65-70% of offensive snaps, he could become one of the biggest values relative to his ADP and a weekly fantasy starter. Even if his workload settles closer to 55-60%, he still profiles as a strong value and a viable starter in most formats.

    54. RB Travis Etienne Jr., New Orleans Saints

    Etienne joins the Saints after four years as the lead back for the Jacksonville Jaguars. He has played 2,708 offensive snaps over that span, third-most among running backs, posting a 79.6 PFF offensive grade, which ranks 26th of 35 backs with at least 1,500 snaps.

    In New Orleans, he takes the top spot from Alvin Kamara, whose 75.2 grade ranks 32nd among those 35, with Etienne the better rusher and Kamara the better receiver. Etienne signed a four-year, $47 million contract with $28 million guaranteed, making him one of the highest-paid backs in the league and signaling clearly that he will be the starter going forward. Kamara restructured his contract in July, making it clear he will remain on the roster. The likely split will be Etienne on early downs and Kamara on passing downs, with both backs capable of playing in any situation.

    There is a concern that Etienne won't be as effective in New Orleans as he was in Jacksonville. The Jaguars ranked 15th in team run-block grade last season, while the Saints finished 31st. Etienne averaged 1.2 yards before contact per carry, compared to 1.0 for Saints backs. The more yards before contact a back gets, the more room he has to add yards after contact, so this could push Etienne below 4.0 yards per carry.

    His receiving production could also take a hit on early downs. Saints head coach Kellen Moore had Ezekiel Elliott, Austin Ekeler and Saquon Barkley in their primes, and all three saw their receiving numbers dip while Moore was their offensive coordinator, relative to the seasons before and after. Kamara followed the same pattern, averaging 3.0 receptions and 16.9 yards per game under Moore after 4.8 catches and 39.3 yards per game for his career.

    Etienne deserves to be drafted as a top-25 back this season, since his contract should secure top-25 volume, but his upside is more limited than most unless the Saints' line and offense improve significantly.

    55. WR Christian Watson, Green Bay Packers

    Watson has spent the past three seasons in a crowded receiver room with Romeo Doubs, Jayden Reed and Dontayvion Wicks. All four have posted between 1,300 and 2,000 receiving yards and 11 to 18 touchdowns. Each has also earned a receiving grade over 74.0 across the past four seasons, making all of them worthy of significant playing time. Green Bay hasn't run 11 personnel as often as the average team, which has further cut into the fantasy relevance of all four.

    Watson is coming off by far the best season any of them has produced, an 88.5 grade. The next highest was Wicks' 77.8 in 2023. He gained at least 80 receiving yards and a touchdown in three of his final five games, and ranked as the WR12 from Weeks 13 to 17, rewarding anyone willing to start him.

    The Packers have placed significant faith in him going forward. They traded Wicks and let Doubs leave in free agency, which leaves Watson and Matthew Golden as the outside receivers and Reed in the slot, with far less rotation. They also signed him to a four-year, $92 million extension that included a $31 million signing bonus, underscoring his standing as their top receiver.

    Watson has been plagued by injuries throughout his career, largely to his knees and hamstrings, and most recently a torn ACL in January 2025 that cost him half of last season. The Packers clearly believe those injuries are behind him, given their offseason moves, but his history still carries significant risk.

    The best case is that he builds on his late-season momentum and plays like a WR1 for a full season. The worst case is that injuries get the best of him again.

    56. WR Davante Adams, Los Angeles Rams

    Adams has been a fantasy starter at wide receiver for a decade. He gained at least 880 receiving yards every season from 2016 to 2024 and reached double-digit touchdowns in most of them. But he will be 34 before the season ends, and his days as a top-10 fantasy receiver are likely over. His play has already begun to slip: He earned PFF receiving grades of 87.0 or better each season from 2017 to 2022, but that has fallen to 84.0 or lower in each of the past three.

    Adams joined the Rams last season, where he averaged 4.3 receptions and 56.4 yards per game, his lowest marks since 2015. He landed in an offense built around Puka Nacua, on a team that often won and ran out the clock late. Los Angeles also leaned into 13 personnel down the stretch, and Adams was on the field for only 28.3% of its 13-personnel snaps when healthy.

    Despite the dip in receptions and yardage, Adams still finished as a top-10 fantasy receiver in points per game because he scored a league-high 14 touchdowns. He saw 57.4% of the Rams' end-zone targets, the highest share among wide receivers, drawing 25 end-zone targets and converting 13 into scores. Both were the second-most for a receiver in a season over the past 20 years, trailing only Randy Moss in 2007.

    The problem is that end-zone touchdowns aren't reliably consistent from one season to the next. No wide receiver in the past 20 seasons has posted back-to-back years with double-digit end-zone touchdowns. If Adams matches his receptions and yards per game but scores five fewer touchdowns, he falls from top-10 in fantasy points to 25th.

    Terrance Ferguson, a 2025 second-round tight end, is expected to take on a bigger role this season and should be a red-zone threat of his own. Adams should still be an asset to Los Angeles in its pursuit of another Super Bowl, but his footing as a fantasy starter gets shakier if his role shrinks and the touchdowns regress. He could be a frustrating WR3, with big weeks when he finds the end zone and quiet ones when he doesn't.

    57. QB Jalen Hurts, Philadelphia Eagles

    Hurts has finished among the top eight quarterbacks in fantasy points per game in each of the past five seasons, a mark only Josh Allen can match over that span. Lamar Jackson has four such seasons and Patrick Mahomes has three, making Hurts the second-most consistent fantasy quarterback in recent years behind Allen, and the youngest of the group.

    All four of those quarterbacks had down seasons in 2025, and there are real reasons to think the others face more headwinds going forward. Mahomes is recovering from a torn ACL and LCL with Week 1 availability uncertain, Jackson has an injury history and a supporting cast that is aging, and the concerns around Rashee Rice and Travis Kelce in Kansas City are well documented. That context makes Hurts one of the safer options at the position heading into 2026.

    That said, 2025 was below his usual standard. His 7.1 yards per attempt was a career low, and his 26.3 rushing yards per game represented a sharp drop from the prior season and roughly half of his career high. He also converted fewer rushing touchdowns, offsetting them with more passing scores, which is positive for Philadelphia's offense but less impactful in four-point passing touchdown formats.

    The Eagles ranked outside the top 10 in PFF offensive team grade for the first time since 2020 and finished below league average in points scored. Philadelphia traded A.J. Brown and replaced him with Makai Lemon, Dontayvion Wicks, Hollywood Brown and tight end Eli Stowers, and even without Brown the Eagles should have a top-10 offense given the overall talent on the roster. New offensive coordinator Sean Mannion has also indicated that Hurts' rushing ability will remain a central part of the scheme, which should help his fantasy value bounce back toward his typical range.

    58. QB Lamar Jackson, Baltimore Ravens

    Jackson is the highest-risk, highest-reward quarterback in the top five. He is the only quarterback to ever average 25 fantasy points per game in multiple seasons — having done so in both 2019 and 2024 — and he has won the NFL MVP twice while nearly claiming a third in 2024.

    The concern is his declining rushing volume. Jackson averaged double-digit carries per game from 2019 through 2021 before falling to 9.3 in both 2022 and 2023, 8.2 in 2024 and just 5.2 in 2025. Some rebound is reasonable to expect, but a return to his peak rushing workload is not.

    His passing efficiency has remained strong, as he has averaged more than 8.0 yards per attempt in each of the past three seasons. However, his sack rate climbed to a career high in 2025, contributing to a decline in passing volume compared to the previous two seasons.

    The Ravens also overhauled their coaching staff, hiring Jesse Minter as head coach and Declan Doyle as offensive coordinator. Doyle spent 2025 with the Chicago Bears but did not call plays under head coach Ben Johnson, making 2026 the first season he will run his own offense. At 29, he is one of the youngest and least experienced play-callers to inherit an elite quarterback, and some growing pains are to be expected.

    Baltimore also lost several notable offensive players in free agency, including center Tyler Linderbaum, tight ends Isaiah Likely and Charlie Kolar, wide receiver DeAndre Hopkins and fullback Patrick Ricard. The offense now leans on Derrick Henry, Mark Andrews and Ronnie Stanley, all of whom are on the wrong side of 30. Baltimore also used seven draft picks on offensive players, and how quickly those rookies contribute will have a meaningful impact on Jackson's floor and ceiling in 2026. Even with his league-winning upside, the uncertainty surrounding his rushing workload, new coaching staff and supporting cast makes his top-five price difficult to justify when similar upside is available several rounds later.

    59. WR Rome Odunze, Chicago Bears

    Odunze was the ninth overall pick of the 2024 draft. Typically, a wide receiver drafted that early is expected to make an immediate impact, but Odunze joined a crowded wide receiver room that included DJ Moore and Keenan Allen. He played 84% of offensive snaps and accumulated 734 yards over the season, but had only four games above 45 receiving yards.

    Chicago moved on from Allen after the 2024 season, making Odunze a clear-cut top-two receiver on the team. He had an excellent start to the season, with 20 receptions for 296 yards and five touchdowns over the first four weeks. This placed him at WR3 in September. He started appearing on the injury report before Week 9 with what was initially listed as a heel injury, then ankle and heel, then foot. He played five weeks with the injury and then missed five games because of it. He returned for the playoffs, but his playing time was notably affected.

    There was some speculation that he started dealing with the injury before appearing on the injury report, but he still averaged 15.5 PPR points per game over his seven games before showing up on the report. He was held to two or fewer receptions in five of his seven games while on the injury report.

    Chicago traded away Moore, but Luther Burden III and Colston Loveland both played exceptionally well at the end of the season. Both Burden and Loveland had two-game stretches with at least 80 yards in each game, something Odunze hasn't accomplished in his NFL career.

    While his foot is different after the injury, he is no longer always in pain, and he doesn't expect it to keep him from playing. He, Burden and Loveland are very likely to be the top three players in targets, but it's unclear in what order. There is some uncertainty around Odunze's foot, but the uncertainty about the other two, given their strong play, is based on a relatively small sample.

    Odunze has the upside to be a top-10 receiver, but there is also a risk he won't be a consistent fantasy starter. That is the typical range of outcomes for someone drafted to be a WR3.

    60. QB Jayden Daniels, Washington Commanders

    Daniels was a top-five fantasy quarterback as a rookie, averaging 21.5 fantasy points per game on the strength of his rushing, with 148 carries for 891 yards and six touchdowns. No quarterback was close to those marks, with Josh Allen‘s 112 carries for 579 yards coming in second for the position.

    However, Daniels struggled significantly in 2025, largely due to injury. He dealt with a knee sprain, hamstring strain and dislocated elbow across the season, with his only fully healthy game coming in Week 1 — when he ran 11 times and scored 20.1 fantasy points.

    His rushing ability gives him the potential to be the top fantasy quarterback over the next several seasons if he can stay healthy, including a realistic shot at the top spot in 2026. The elite rushing quarterbacks of the past decade have all gradually run less often, and they remain near the top of these rankings largely because no one has stepped in to replace them.

    The biggest concern is that Daniels continues to incur injuries, or that his injury history leads him to become more conservative as a runner. There is some comfort in the fact that he does not need to match his rookie rushing volume to remain a top fantasy quarterback, but any meaningful reduction in that area would significantly lower his potential.

    The other worry is significant turnover in Daniels’ supporting cast. David Blough takes over as offensive coordinator after spending two seasons as the team's assistant quarterbacks coach, providing the offense with some continuity with Daniels while also marking his first time calling plays at the NFL level. Five of the six Commanders with 40 or more receptions over the past two seasons are off the roster, including Zach Ertz, Deebo Samuel, Olamide Zaccheaus, Austin Ekeler and Noah Brown. Terry McLaurin is the only significant returning receiver, with third-round rookie Antonio Williams, tight end Chig Okonkwo and running back Rachaad White among the notable additions. Whether those changes help or hurt the offense immediately adds another layer of uncertainty around Daniels heading into 2026.

    61. WR Carnell Tate, Tennessee Titans

    Tate was expected to go around picks 8-10, so it was a surprise to see him selected fourth overall, both for how early he went and for the fact that the Titans were the team to take him. The best-case scenario is that he ends up like Ja'Marr Chase or Malik Nabers, both of whom were top-six options at their position in their rookie seasons. Jaylen Waddle, Garrett Wilson and Tetairoa McMillan were other recent top-10 wide receivers who immediately worked out as fantasy starters.

    Tate joins Tennessee as the team's top outside receiving option, playing as the X receiver. The Titans restructured Calvin Ridley‘s contract, and he will likely line up as the Z receiver on the outside, while Wan'Dale Robinson was a notable free agent addition signed to play in the slot. Robinson joined Tennessee in part because Brian Daboll is the new offensive coordinator, and Robinson spent the previous four seasons with Daboll in New York.

    In 2024, the Giants featured a wide receiver pairing of Nabers and Robinson, with Nabers generating a 29.8% target rate, second-highest in the league. Tennessee's receiver room sets up similarly, giving Tate a realistic path to a top-10 target share as a rookie.

    Tate's fantasy production will largely depend on how much Cam Ward improves as a passer. The two will be tied together for the foreseeable future, and Ward's 56.4 passing grade last season ranked fifth-lowest among quarterbacks with at least 150 dropbacks.

    Tate is the top wide receiver option in dynasty leagues and should be the first rookie wide receiver off the board in redrafts. He projects as an immediate fantasy starter as long as he holds onto the top target role.

    62. QB Joe Burrow, Cincinnati Bengals

    Burrow opened the 2025 season slowly, drawing the Cleveland Browns in Week 1 and then suffering an injury in Week 2. From his return in Week 13 through Week 18, he ranked third among quarterbacks in fantasy points per game. He finished as the QB3 in 2024.

    Unlike most quarterbacks drafted near the top of fantasy leagues, Burrow does not rely on rushing production. He is the exception because he has been one of the highest-graded passers in the NFL in recent seasons, operates in one of the league's most pass-heavy offenses and throws to elite wide receivers in Ja'Marr Chase and Tee Higgins.

    Continuity also works in his favor, as Cincinnati is retaining its coaching staff and Burrow, Chase, and Higgins are all signed long-term. Twenty of the 22 players who logged at least 25 offensive snaps last season remain on the roster. The only departures are backup tight end Noah Fant and third-string quarterback Jake Browning, with Erick All Jr. returning from injury and veteran Josh Johnson filling those roles.

    Health remains the primary concern. Burrow has missed at least six games in three of his six NFL seasons and has already won Comeback Player of the Year twice. His injuries have affected different areas of his body, including a knee, wrist and toe, which suggests misfortune rather than a recurring structural issue.

    The secondary concern is whether an improved defense reduces his passing volume. The Bengals have ranked among the worst defenses in terms of points allowed in each of the past two seasons, forcing them into negative game scripts and a pass-heavy approach. Cincinnati addressed that by trading for defensive tackle Dexter Lawrence and signing safety Bryan Cook and defensive end Boye Mafe in free agency. If the defense improves significantly, the Bengals should spend more time protecting fourth-quarter leads with the running game, which would likely reduce Burrow's passing volume and cap his fantasy ceiling. His floor is as safe as any quarterback's, but potential volume regression and his injury history make it difficult to justify his cost when similar production is available several rounds later.

    63. RB Rhamondre Stevenson, New England Patriots

    Many fantasy managers were ready to move on from Stevenson after New England used a second-round pick on TreVeyon Henderson last offseason. Offensive coordinator Josh McDaniels, though, has spent two decades deploying committee backfields, making a full takeover unlikely.

    Henderson finished 21st among running backs in total PPR points last season, while Stevenson ranked 25th. On a per-game basis, though, Stevenson outscored Henderson, finishing 22nd to his 25th. As the season progressed, the backfield increasingly tilted in Stevenson's favor, culminating in an average of 17.3 PPR points per game across New England's final six regular-season games.

    The Patriots made few meaningful changes to the offense this offseason. At running back, they let Antonio Gibson walk and drafted Jam Miller in the seventh round. Henderson could earn a slightly larger role this season, particularly relative to the end of last year, but it still seems unlikely he fully overtakes the backfield.

    Stevenson remains the more physically imposing runner and outperformed Henderson in several key areas last season. He forced missed tackles at a higher rate and was more effective in short-yardage and goal-line situations. He also graded significantly better as both a receiver and a pass protector.

    It is reasonable to draft Henderson earlier because of his explosive-play upside, but New England already showed last season that this offense can support two top-30 fantasy running backs. If the Patriots' offense improves again in 2026, repeating that outcome should be well within reach.

    Stevenson's ADP currently sits outside the top 30 at the position. There is also a real chance Henderson doesn't make a substantial leap, allowing Stevenson to maintain the clear lead in touches he held late in the regular season and throughout the playoffs. If that happens, he could become one of the better values in fantasy drafts.

    64. WR Mike Evans, San Francisco 49ers

    Evans spent over a decade as the Tampa Bay Buccaneers' X receiver, famously gaining over 1,000 receiving yards in 11 straight seasons. He has also been dominant as a scorer, with six seasons of at least 11 touchdown receptions. He ranks 10th all-time in receiving touchdowns with 108 and could climb a few spots this season.

    Evans landed with the San Francisco 49ers after a disappointing final season in Tampa Bay, when he missed over half the year and played through injury for much of the rest. San Francisco largely reworked its receiver room, which for a few seasons was the trio of Brandon Aiyuk, Deebo Samuel and Jauan Jennings and now includes Evans, Samuel, Christian Kirk and second-round rookie De'Zhaun Stribling.

    He is the only one in that group with a secure role, since Stribling could push either Samuel or Kirk for snaps. His playing time will likely dip, but mostly in run-first situations: an excellent run blocker earlier in his career, he has declined with age, and San Francisco places a premium on that skill. He will be 33 by the start of the season.

    The good news is that he should still have plenty of touchdown opportunities. San Francisco has typically ranked among the top 10 in offensive yards and touchdowns under Kyle Shanahan and Brock Purdy.

    The 49ers' X receiver has consistently been a fantasy starter, from Aiyuk when healthy to Jennings. Evans should be able to follow in those footsteps, but the risk lies in how much his playing time declines and how much he can still dominate red-zone targets.

    Tier 5

    65. WR Brian Thomas Jr., Jacksonville Jaguars

    Thomas took the league by storm as a rookie, scoring 284 PPR points, fourth-most among all fantasy wide receivers, though only 12th in fantasy points per game. Although Thomas has the body of a prototypical X receiver, he shone most in the slot. His 89.2 receiving grade there as a rookie was the best among wide receivers with at least 125 slot routes, and his 3.12 yards per route run from the slot also led the group.

    The Jaguars hired Liam Coen as head coach, who had guided Cooper Kupp and Chris Godwin to elite fantasy production as his slot receivers. But Jacksonville also drafted Travis Hunter, who played primarily from the slot, pushing Thomas outside. His target rate fell from 24.7% to 18.7%. It is also worth noting that much of Thomas' rookie success came with Mac Jones at quarterback; with Lawrence, his rookie target rate was only 20.8%. Regardless of the quarterback, his average depth of target rose, leading to more uncatchable and contested targets, which in turn meant fewer receptions, receiving yards and touchdowns per game. He finished 47th in fantasy points per game.

    Thomas has the same coach and quarterback this season. Jacksonville shifted from Hunter to Parker Washington in the slot last season, and Washington played well there, so Thomas' role is not expected to change. Even with Jacksonville's several weapons, Thomas is expected to bounce back.

    Lawrence set a goal to improve his chemistry with Thomas, and it showed throughout OTAs. Beat writers consistently praised Thomas' work, though they said the same about Washington. Jacksonville added two tight ends in the draft, a sign it will use more two-tight-end sets, but that shouldn't cut into Thomas' playing time.

    Thomas will likely frustrate fantasy managers, since some weeks the big game will be his and others it will belong to Washington or Jakobi Meyers. Even so, better chemistry with Lawrence and some positive touchdown regression should produce a much stronger third season, even if the raw numbers don't match his rookie year.

    66. RB Bhayshul Tuten, Jacksonville Jaguars

    Tuten was a fourth-round selection by Jacksonville in 2025, landing in a crowded backfield with Travis Etienne and Tank Bigsby. It grew more competitive when seventh-round rookie LeQuint Allen won the third-down job. In Week 1, Tuten played just four snaps, the fewest of the four backs. Jacksonville then traded Bigsby after that game, letting Tuten spend the rest of the season as the early-down backup to Etienne.

    Tuten's only game with double-digit carries was a Week 11 blowout against the Los Angeles Chargers. He finished the season with 83 carries for 307 yards and five touchdowns and added 10 catches for 79 yards and two touchdowns. His yards before contact per carry was a notably low 0.5, second-worst among running backs with at least 75 carries. Backs from the same offense usually post similar marks there, but Etienne was at 1.2, which suggests better run-blocking could lift Tuten's efficiency. The newcomer also posted a high 3.2 yards after contact per carry, tied for 13th, converted first downs at a strong 27.7% clip, ninth-best, and ranked 10th with 0.22 avoided tackles per attempt.

    The Jaguars did not re-sign Etienne, instead adding free agent Chris Rodriguez. Rodriguez is a similarly low-volume, high-efficiency back. He was better in yards after contact per attempt (3.5) and first-down rate (28.6%) and just behind in avoided tackles per carry (0.21). Rodriguez also worked with Liam Coen for a season at Kentucky. They are strong in the same statistical areas but are different backs: Tuten is a smaller, speedy runner, while Rodriguez is bigger and still fast for his size.

    All signs point to some kind of three-man rotation between Tuten, Rodriguez and Allen. Tuten is widely expected to be the best fantasy option of the three, since Rodriguez has shown minimal promise as a receiver and Allen isn't expected to run much. However, just because they might not be worth starting in fantasy leagues doesn't mean they can't be a major thorn in Tuten's fantasy production.

    There is a wide range of outcomes within that rotation, though. Everyone could land near a one-third split, or Tuten could play 60% of the snaps with the other two at 20% each. It is also possible Rodriguez becomes the primary early-down back and Allen the primary passing-down back, with Tuten backing up both. In that case, Tuten would play more than the 21.3% of snaps he saw last season, but perhaps not by much.

    Allen has rotated significantly with Tuten through the start of training camp. Tuten's ADP had been on the rise with Rodriguez injured, but he returned to practice in full pads on Monday and had the highlight play of the day.

    Tuten is currently being drafted as a low-end RB2, but there is serious risk at that price. There is a real chance no one in this backfield can be trusted on a weekly basis. With the talent at quarterback, receiver and tight end, Jacksonville could be a pass-first team. The three-man rotation splits carries among backs with similar profiles, featuring no large quality gap between the top option and the next.

    67. WR DK Metcalf, Pittsburgh Steelers

    Metcalf spent the first six seasons of his career with the Seattle Seahawks, gaining at least 900 receiving yards each year. He peaked in 2020, with 1,303 yards and 10 touchdowns, then saw his production slip a bit each season after, finishing as the WR32 in his final year in Seattle. That decline was largely tied to the Seahawks' offense, as he scored fewer touchdowns and fewer of his targets were catchable.

    The Steelers traded a second-round pick to acquire Metcalf before the 2025 season and gave him a long-term contract. He was the focal point of the offense, but his receiving yards and receptions per game were both his lowest marks since his rookie season. His targets per route were higher than in his final two seasons in Seattle; he was simply running fewer routes. His average depth of target dropped, letting him do more after the catch, but the lack of passing volume hurt his production.

    The good news is that Pittsburgh moved on from offensive coordinator Arthur Smith. Over his two seasons there, the Steelers ranked ninth in run plays and 27th in dropbacks. New head coach and offensive leader Mike McCarthy takes over, and in his five seasons with the Dallas Cowboys, they ranked fourth in pass plays and 10th in rushing attempts.

    Metcalf's routes could rise, but his target rate could fall. The Steelers leaned on a mix of Calvin Austin III, Roman Wilson, Scotty Miller and Ben Skowronek early last season, then added Adam Thielen and Marquez Valdes-Scantling midseason. Austin had 372 receiving yards, and no one else topped 200. Pittsburgh invested more heavily in the receiver room this offseason, trading for Michael Pittman Jr. and drafting Germie Bernard in the second round and Kaden Wetjen in the fourth. More competition for targets isn't necessarily good news, since defenses will still key on Metcalf regardless.

    Metcalf will also keep catching passes from Aaron Rodgers. Rodgers played well for a 41-year-old last season, but he is now 42, and it is entirely possible he continues to decline. Given that age, McCarthy could shift the offense's philosophy to protect Rodgers' arm. That could add up to Metcalf's fantasy value being hurt more than helped.

    Metcalf is a fine flex option as a talented receiver and the top target in his offense, but he is unlikely to see a substantial jump in production from last season.

    68. RB Tony Pollard, Tennessee Titans

    Pollard is one of two running backs with four straight seasons of 1,000 rushing yards, finishing as a top-23 fantasy running back in each of those seasons, split between two with the Dallas Cowboys and two with the Titans.

    Most of Pollard's rate stats have been average, but there are signs he is a great running back held back by his situation. He averaged 3.3 yards after contact per carry over the last four years, tied for fourth-best among running backs with at least 500 carries, behind De'Von Achane, Derrick Henry, and Bijan Robinson. His 1.1 yards before contact per carry are tied for fifth-worst over that span. He has also broken big plays at a high rate, posting a plus-1.0 run grade on 4.2% of his carries, eighth-best among backs, behind Kenneth Walker III, Jahmyr Gibbs, Robinson, Achane, Henry, Nick Chubb, and James Conner.

    The good news is that Pollard is slated to be the clear starter for Tennessee for a third straight season. The Titans passed on Jeremiyah Love at fourth overall and their only backfield additions were Michael Carter and fifth-round rookie Nicholas Singleton.

    If everything stays the same, Pollard will continue to profile as a low-end RB2, and he is currently being drafted slightly later than that by managers chasing more perceived upside. However, if Tennessee's offense improves, that should have a direct impact on his fantasy production. The Titans invested their last four early first-round picks on offensive players, offensive linemen Peter Skoronski and JC Latham both made substantial leaps in run blocking last season and are still under 25, and Cam Ward showed meaningful improvement late in his rookie season, posting a 70-plus passing grade in three of his last seven games after failing to reach that mark in his first 10. Carnell Tate and free agent addition Wan'Dale Robinson should also help the passing game.

    Even if only the passing game improves rather than the run blocking, that should lead to more red zone trips and more touchdowns for Pollard. He is one of eight running backs with over 500 carries over the last two seasons and has just 10 rushing touchdowns in that time, while the other seven have at least 20 each. A few more touchdowns would push him into mid-range RB2 territory, and an improved offensive line on top of that would bring him closer to a top-10 fantasy back.

    69. TE Tucker Kraft, Green Bay Packers

    Kraft was one of the NFL's most efficient tight ends in 2024. The problem was volume.

    His 9.3 yards per target led all tight ends by a wide margin, a full 1.5 yards ahead of the next-closest player. Packers coaches clearly recognized the need to involve him more entering 2025, and his target rate climbed from 14.5% to 19.1%. Rather than losing efficiency with the increased workload, Kraft somehow became even more dangerous after the catch. His 10.8 yards after the catch per reception was the highest by any tight end with at least 25 targets in PFF's 20 years of charting.

    Before suffering a season-ending ACL injury, he averaged 14.7 PPR points per game, tying Brock Bowers and George Kittle for the second-highest mark at the position. Kraft opened training camp on the PUP list but has already been activated and gone through drills with the team, a strong indication he will be ready for Week 1.

    The encouraging part for fantasy managers is that Kraft may not need historic efficiency to improve his production. Even after a breakout season, his target rate still ranked just 19th among tight ends with at least 25 targets last year. That number could climb further given Green Bay's offseason changes.

    Romeo Doubs, last season's team leader in targets, is no longer on the roster, and Dontayvion Wicks, who ranked third, is also gone. Green Bay did not spend a draft pick on a skill-position player. Skyy Moore was its lone free-agent addition at wide receiver, likely brought in more for special teams value than offensive production. That leaves a significant opportunity for the returning pass-catchers.

    For fantasy purposes, a slight dip in efficiency paired with a larger workload would still be a favorable tradeoff. Kraft’s recovery is worth monitoring through camp, but if he returns to full health, he has a realistic path to finishing as a top-three fantasy tight end in 2026. He is currently one of the best values on the board, particularly on ESPN.

    70. TE Tyler Warren, Indianapolis Colts

    Warren was the 14th overall pick in the 2025 NFL Draft, and he started his career strong, recording back-to-back games of 75-plus yards. He appeared to be a league winner through seven weeks, with 101.5 fantasy points, the third-most among tight ends. He also led the position in receiving yards with 439.

    Unfortunately, his production fell off dramatically over the last 10 weeks. He scored just one touchdown, and his receiving yards per game dropped from 62.7 to 37.8. His playing time held steady, but his target rate slipped from 23.1% to 19.9%. His average depth of target also dropped by a yard. That shift usually leads to more yards after the catch per reception, yet his number there declined by 2.5 instead.

    This largely coincided with a general decline in the Colts' offense. Daniel Jones posted a 75.4 passing grade over the first seven weeks, and that fell to 57.8 over the next seven. Philip Rivers' brief return to the NFL was impressive for a player his age, but his passing grade landed at just 50.5.

    If everything goes well, Warren will return to being an elite fantasy option at tight end. He, Michael Pittman Jr. and Josh Downs competed for short targets last year, with Downs drawing 60, Pittman 56 and Warren 54. Pittman was traded, and Indianapolis hasn't made a substantial addition. Chances are, Pittman's targets will be widely distributed among Alec Pierce, Downs, Warren and whoever wins the third receiver spot.

    However, there is still a chance Jones doesn't return to the quarterback he was at the start of 2025. While his recovery from a torn Achilles is the primary concern, his play had started to dip before the injury. If that's the case, Warren would be a low-end TE1. He finished 13th in fantasy points from Weeks 8-18 last season, and a likely increase in targets should push him into the top 12.

    71. RB Rico Dowdle, Pittsburgh Steelers

    Dowdle spent his first four seasons in Dallas as a backup, rising from just seven carries over his first three years to the primary backup job in 2023, before taking over as the starter in 2024. As the starter, he posted a healthy 14.7 carries per game for 67.4 yards but scored only two touchdowns, making him an RB2 candidate.

    Dowdle spent his first four seasons in Dallas as a backup, rising from just seven carries over his first three years to the primary backup job in 2023, before taking over as the starter in 2024. As the starter, he posted a healthy 14.7 carries per game for 67.4 yards but scored only two touchdowns, making him an RB2 candidate.

    Dowdle signed with the Steelers this offseason, reuniting with Mike McCarthy. McCarthy was the head coach in Dallas for all five seasons Dowdle was on the roster, so the two know each other well. Pittsburgh retained Jaylen Warren, while Kenneth Gainwell left in free agency.

    Usually it is fairly straightforward to predict how snaps will be split between two backs, but not here. Warren spent his first few seasons as a third-down back who occasionally played early downs, while Najee Harris handled the early-down work. That flipped last season: Warren was the early-down back, and Gainwell took most of the third downs. Dowdle has shown the same versatility, serving as an early-down back in his year as the Dallas starter and then a third-down back at the start of his time in Carolina.

    By PFF grades, Warren has been the better of the two. That holds for both rushing and receiving, and across any recent window, whether last season, the past two years or the past three. The gap is fairly wide in receiving but narrow in rushing. If Pittsburgh wants an even split, it would make sense to lean on Dowdle more as a rusher and Warren more as a receiver. For immediate fantasy value, how a coach views a player matters more than how good the player actually is, and that could push Dowdle ahead as well. That said, an even split is not guaranteed, and one back could seize the job.

    Dowdle is worth a selection among the top 30 running backs, since in most scenarios he is a flex option at worst and a weekly RB2 at best.

    72. TE Sam LaPorta, Detroit Lions

    LaPorta led all tight ends in fantasy points during his 2023 rookie season. He's remained among the top 10 in fantasy points per game in each of the last two seasons. While he hasn't played like the elite tier of tight ends, he's been the best tight end outside that tier. His 86.9 PFF receiving grade over the last three seasons is fourth-best among tight ends.

    LaPorta's big problem has been his competition for targets. The Lions have Amon-Ra St. Brown and Jameson Williams at wide receiver, in addition to Jahmyr Gibbs at running back. LaPorta earned a target on 20.1% of his routes over the last three seasons, which ranks eighth among the 10 tight ends with the earliest ADP this season. The two players with a lower target rate than LaPorta, Tucker Kraft and Kyle Pitts, both had higher target rates than LaPorta last season.

    Despite the low target rate, LaPorta ranks sixth among all tight ends in yards per route run at 1.75. His 20 touchdowns over the last three seasons are the third-most among tight ends, and he should remain one of the favorites to lead tight ends in touchdowns, given the Lions' high-quality offense.

    The good news is that LaPorta's target rate could change. The Lions added Drew Petzing as offensive coordinator. Petzing spent 2020-2022 with the Cleveland Browns, where the Browns' tight ends combined for the fourth-most targets, and 2023-2025 with the Arizona Cardinals, where Trey McBride had 57 more targets than any other tight end. It's unclear how much Petzing will influence the offense compared to head coach Dan Campbell, but the Petzing addition should help LaPorta's value rather than hurt it.

    The big concern for LaPorta is injury. He's dealt with shoulder, ankle, knee and back injuries over the last two seasons. LaPorta had surgery to repair a herniated disc last season. He participated in OTAs, and it sounds like he will be ready to go for training camp, but his situation will be worth monitoring.

    LaPorta is one of the early-round tight ends who has a chance to be among the top few at the position, but only if he can stay healthy throughout the season.

    73. RB TreVeyon Henderson, New England Patriots

    Henderson was drafted 38th overall in the 2025 NFL draft by the Patriots. Several had lofty expectations that he could take over the backfield, but his offensive coordinator was and still is Josh McDaniels. McDaniels has been a head coach or offensive coordinator for 19 seasons in the PFF era, and in 18 of them, the running back who led the team in rushing yards was not the one who played on third downs.

    Henderson began the season in a three-man rotation. Known in college as a receiving back, he actually played more on early downs than on third downs. A few weeks in, it looked like his role would grow after a season-ending injury to Antonio Gibson, but it shrank instead because of inefficient play. An injury to Rhamondre Stevenson then made Henderson the starter throughout November.

    Henderson had one especially dominant two-week stretch. In the first game, he ran 14 times for 147 yards and two touchdowns, then scored three times the following week. Even with Stevenson back, the rookie held a decent role through the rest of the regular season, though partly thanks to some blowout wins. In the playoffs, Henderson played 40% of snaps or fewer and averaged just 2.5 yards per carry.

    New England didn't re-sign Gibson or make any other meaningful changes to the backfield, so both Stevenson and Henderson should have significant roles. Stevenson graded better as a runner, receiver and pass-blocker. If the two play at the level they did last season, Stevenson should remain the primary back, and Henderson would carry fantasy value mainly when the Patriots are blowing opponents out.

    Henderson is a good bet to improve, though. He was known for his receiving in college, but his pass protection likely kept him off the field in some passing situations. If he sharpens as a pass-blocker this offseason, he could see more receiving work, in which case both backs could be weekly fantasy starters.

    There is also a path where Henderson takes a slight majority of the carries, Stevenson does the dirty work and Henderson handles most of the passing-down work, which would make Henderson a weekly must-start. That would be a deviation from how McDaniels has typically split his backfields, so it is the less likely of the two scenarios, but Henderson's receiving talent makes it a real possibility.

    74. TE Harold Fannin Jr., Cleveland Browns

    Fannin excelled as a rookie and was the focal point of the Browns' offense by the end of the season. His 76.4 PFF receiving grade ranked fourth among tight ends with at least 100 targets last season. He opened the year as David Njoku‘s backup, but Cleveland's heavy use of 12 personnel kept him on the field, and he played at least 90% of offensive snaps in three of his final six games. Fannin caught a respectable six touchdowns last season, and at 6-foot-4, he has the size to score more in 2026.

    There is reason for optimism about an even better sophomore season, since the Browns hired Todd Monken as head coach. Monken spent the past decade as an offensive coordinator for the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, Cleveland Browns, Georgia Bulldogs and Baltimore Ravens. At some stops, he had elite tight ends like Brock Bowers and Mark Andrews, while other rooms were thinner. Even so, his three NFL teams each ranked among the top four in touchdowns to tight ends, and Georgia tallied the seventh-most in the Power Five during his time there.

    Cleveland moved on from Njoku, cementing Fannin's role as a full-time player. The concern is that the Browns made significant investments at wide receiver, adding K.C. Concepcion and Denzel Boston. That should send more targets to the receivers going forward, which could leave fewer for Fannin.

    Fannin’s talent, combined with heavy garbage-time volume, gives him a chance to push into the elite tier of tight ends. But for now, enough strong tight ends in better offenses with superior quarterbacks sit slightly ahead of him in the rankings.

    75. RB Jaylen Warren, Pittsburgh Steelers

    Warren went undrafted in 2022 but immediately won the receiving role in Pittsburgh. He spent three seasons as the third-down back while Najee Harris played on early downs. As the more effective of the two, he earned a steadily larger role: 31.3% of snaps as a rookie, 48.5% in 2023 and 50.8% over the final eight games of 2024 after injuries slowed the start of his season.

    The Steelers moved on from Harris in 2025, added Kenneth Gainwell in free agency and drafted Kaleb Johnson. Many expected Johnson to take over Harris' early-down role, but it went to Warren instead, with Gainwell inheriting Warren's former third-down duties. Warren averaged 13.6 PPR points per game, just ahead of Gainwell's 13.0.

    Warren's role this season is much less clear, with Mike Tomlin stepping away and Mike McCarthy taking over as head coach. Gainwell left in free agency, but McCarthy brought in Rico Dowdle, a running back he coached for five seasons in Dallas.

    Usually it is fairly straightforward to predict how snaps will be split between two backs, but not here. Dowdle has shown the same versatility as Warren, serving as an early-down back in his year as the Dallas starter and then a third-down back at the start of his time in Carolina. That means each back has experience in a two-man backfield in both early-down and third-down roles.

    By PFF grades, Warren has been the better of the two. That holds for both rushing and receiving, and across any recent window, whether last season, the past two years or the past three. The gap is fairly wide in receiving but narrow in rushing. If Pittsburgh wants an even split, it would make sense to lean on Dowdle more as a rusher and Warren more as a receiver. For immediate fantasy value, how a coach views a player matters more than how good the player actually is, and that could push Dowdle ahead as well. That said, an even split is not guaranteed, and one back could seize the job.

    Warren, like Dowdle, is worth a selection among the top 30 running backs, since in most scenarios he is a flex option at worst and a weekly RB2 at best.

    76. QB Justin Herbert, Los Angeles Chargers

    Herbert was the rare rookie quarterback in 2020 who finished among the top 10 at the position despite not being known for his rushing. He placed second in 2021 and 10th in 2022, then missed time in 2023 but still ranked top 12 in fantasy points per game. Los Angeles leaned more run-heavy under offensive coordinator Greg Roman, yet Herbert still finished 16th and 10th over the past two seasons.

    Expectations are higher this season under new offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel. Los Angeles has a young core of skill players around him: running back Omarion Hampton, receivers Ladd McConkey, Quentin Johnston and Tre Harris and tight end Oronde Gadsden II. The offensive line should also be much improved, with Rashawn Slater and Joe Alt healthy and three new interior linemen.

    Typically, quarterbacks run more in their first season or two in the league and gradually take off less as their careers progress. Herbert was relatively consistent over his first five seasons but then ran six times per game last season while averaging 5.3 yards per carry, good for 31.1 rushing yards per game. His 498 rushing yards were the second-most among quarterbacks.

    The stars are aligned for Herbert to be one of the top fantasy quarterbacks this season, if not the top. If all goes well and he turns a few of those carries into rushing touchdowns, he could finish in the top tier. But this wouldn't be the first season he entered with overall QB1 potential only for injuries to his supporting cast to derail it.

    In single-quarterback leagues, I am comfortable passing on every quarterback drafted in the first few rounds and waiting on Herbert. It is still worth adding a second quality option, though, particularly for weeks when Herbert draws a rough matchup.

    77. QB Trevor Lawrence, Jacksonville Jaguars

    Lawrence spent four years as the Jaguars' starter to mixed results, with some stretches where he looked like a Pro Bowl-caliber quarterback and others where he played like a backup. In 2022 and 2023, his fantasy production made him a high-end backup. But in 2024, his passing yards per game fell by more than 45 amid lower volume, and then an injury cut his season short.

    He became a top sleeper quarterback in 2025 after the Jaguars hired Liam Coen as head coach and drafted Travis Hunter with the second overall pick to pair with Brian Thomas Jr., who mostly excelled after Lawrence's injury. Lawrence finished fourth in fantasy points and sixth in points per game at 20.6, though that owed more to his connection with Parker Washington, Jakobi Meyers and Brenton Strange than to Thomas and Hunter. Lawrence also set career highs in rushing attempts, rushing yards and rushing touchdowns.

    Some regression in Lawrence’s nine rushing touchdowns is reasonable to expect, but his overall outlook should be similar to last season. Lawrence will have all five of his receivers available at once, allowing for a rotation and no single player for a defense to key on.

    That should make Lawrence a top-10 option again, yet he is being drafted outside the top 10 since there are several quarterbacks to feel good about this season. That makes him a strong value at his current ADP.

    Tier 6

    78. TE George Kittle, San Francisco 49ers

    Kittle has been among fantasy's elite tight ends whenever healthy. He's finished among the top six tight ends in fantasy points per game in each of the past eight seasons, including two first-place, two second-place and two third-place finishes.

    He's spent his career in Kyle Shanahan's run-first offenses, but he's remained a focal point of them. His 2.27 career yards per route run is the best among all tight ends over the past 20 seasons. Since Brock Purdy took over at quarterback, he's been the league's top tight end on intermediate and deep passes. He has 78 receptions, 1,616 yards and 15 touchdowns on passes of 10 or more air yards, leading all tight ends in each, with nearly 500 more yards than the next-closest.

    Kittle's problem throughout his career has been injuries. He's played every game in a season just once, in 2018. Most recently, a hamstring injury cost him five games early in the 2025 season, and then he tore his Achilles tendon in the 49ers‘ wild-card game.

    The Achilles injury makes it questionable whether he'll be the same player he has been. He's pushing to play Week 1, and we'll likely hear during the preseason that he's ready, but being ready to play and being productive in fantasy are two different things. He returned in Week 7 last season but caught just eight passes for 75 yards over his first three games back. He gained at least 60 yards in each of the following six games.

    This is a more serious injury. Even if he plays Week 1, it might not make sense to start him in fantasy leagues. Kittle will also turn 33 early in the season, and it would be understandable if his quality of play took a step back even without the injury.

    Kittle is the last tight end anyone can draft with overall TE1 upside, but whoever drafts him will also need a second, less volatile option.

    79. TE Kyle Pitts Sr., Atlanta Falcons

    Pitts entered the league with high expectations as the fourth overall pick in the 2021 draft. He posted over 1,000 yards as a rookie but hasn't reached that mark since. In the seasons after, he became more of a rotational player, with his targets per route and PFF receiving grade both declining each year.

    In 2025, Pitts turned his career around. He went from playing only 61.9% of snaps in 2024 to 87.7%. His average depth of target dropped to a more reasonable level for a tight end, while his targets per route rose. The change produced his most explosive plays since his rookie season. He was more effective on contested catches and after the catch, and he gained at least 50 receiving yards on five or more receptions in over half his games. Despite standing 6-foot-6, he hasn't been much of a red-zone threat, though his touchdown total has climbed by one in each season since he scored once as a rookie. He finished fifth in fantasy points per game among tight ends last season, at 12.4.

    There is some reason for concern about Pitts in 2026. The Falcons moved on from their coaching staff and brought in Kevin Stefanski, along with tight end Austin Hooper, who spent two seasons as a starter under Stefanski with the Cleveland Browns. Pitts was initially given the franchise tag, and Atlanta appeared open to trading him for a few months before signing him to a long-term extension. The quarterback spot is unsettled, with Michael Penix Jr. returning from injury to compete with Tua Tagovailoa. Atlanta also added several new receivers, including Jahan Dotson, Olamide Zaccheaus and Zachariah Branch.

    None of these is a major concern on its own, but each adds a bit of uncertainty that accumulates. Pitts graded well last season, at 82.0, but most of the tight ends drafted ahead of him either graded better or were rookies last season who are expected to improve.

    Pitts is worth drafting among the top 10 fantasy tight ends. If everything goes well, he could finish among the top five for a second straight season. But he could also fall outside the top 12 if the new regime doesn't use him optimally.

    80. WR Jordyn Tyson, New Orleans Saints

    Tyson was the second wide receiver selected in the 2026 NFL Draft, going eighth overall, and top-10 wide receivers have historically shown significant fantasy potential even as rookies. PFF's draft guide gave Tyson excellent marks in every category outside of blocking. His injury history was the biggest concern, but reports suggested NFL teams weren't particularly worried, and his draft position reflected that.

    He joins New Orleans as the second option in the passing game across from Chris Olave. Tyson projects as an outside receiver, and that role becomes more likely with the Saints given that Olave plays well in the slot and third receiver Devaughn Vele is primarily a slot option as well.

    The landing spot, however, is not ideal compared to where Tyson could have ended up.
