Most other teams that might have taken a first-round wide receiver had a better offense, a better quarterback, a high chance of landing a top quarterback in the 2027 draft, or fewer competition for targets. The Saints scored the fifth-fewest points last season at 306, and Tyler Shough ranked 19th among quarterbacks in offensive grade. The one relative bright spot is target competition: Chris Olave ranked fifth in targets last season, but his 79.7 receiving grade was notably lower than other high-volume receivers, leaving the door open for Tyson to make an early impact.

    In Kellen Moore's system, most wide receivers who find fantasy success are slot receivers. That has included CeeDee Lamb with the Dallas Cowboys, Keenan Allen with the Los Angeles Chargers, DeVonta Smith with the Philadelphia Eagles, and Olave last season. A.J. Brown in 2024 was a notable exception.

    There is a chance Tyson overtakes Olave as the top option in an ascending Saints offense, in which case he immediately pays off for fantasy managers. There is also a real chance he remains the second option in a below-average offense, making it difficult to finish among the top 40 fantasy wide receivers consistently.

    81. WR Josh Downs, Indianapolis Colts

    Downs has spent the first three seasons of his career as the Colts' slot receiver, and he's been very effective. His 82.5 PFF grade ranks 25th among all wide receivers, and his 1.74 yards per route run similarly places in the top 30 at the position.

    Despite producing like a weekly fantasy starter, Downs has struggled to earn that level of trust because he rarely plays in two-receiver sets. Throughout his career, Alec Pierce has been the Colts' X receiver and Michael Pittman Jr. has filled the Z role, leaving Downs with 1,180 routes in 11 personnel compared to just 33 in two-receiver sets. He was on the field for only 58.7% of the Colts' offensive snaps last season, while most fantasy starters play at least 80% of their team's snaps.

    Indianapolis traded Pittman to the Pittsburgh Steelers this offseason, finally moving Downs to second on the depth chart. And Alec Pierce has started training camp on the PUP list after having ankle surgery in March. While Pierce should be available for the start of the season, Downs will be the top wide receiver in training camp until Pierce is back.

    General manager Chris Ballard said of Downs, “We’re going to see some of the special stuff you’ve seen in the past, but more,” and indicated he will get more opportunities. Head coach Shane Steichen added at the start of training camp, “[We're] trying to find different ways to get him the ball. We'll go through that in training camp and game week prep, but we're excited for him this year.”

    Downs finished 44th in fantasy points last season, but the bump in snaps and targets should be enough to make him a top-36 option. He's currently getting drafted as if he'll take a step backward, making him a nice late-round value.

    82. WR Michael Wilson, Arizona Cardinals

    Wilson was a rare third-round pick in 2023 who immediately cracked a starting lineup. He was Arizona's Z receiver but also served as its deep threat as a rookie. Trey McBride emerged as the team's top receiving option midway through 2023, and Arizona drafted Marvin Harrison Jr. to be its X receiver in 2024.

    Wilson appeared destined for less playing time in 2025. Zay Jones had emerged as the third receiver late in 2024 and began cutting into Wilson's snaps in two-receiver sets early in the season. Wilson averaged 79.7% of offensive snaps in 2024 but was at 65% or fewer in six of his first eight games last season. Jones played outside, which pushed both Harrison and Wilson into the slot more often. Wilson graded worse in the slot than out wide, so the shift hurt his production, and he was held under 20 receiving yards in each of the first five weeks.

    Then the injuries set in for Arizona. Both of its top running backs were lost for the season, pushing the team to lean on the pass. Harrison dealt with a concussion, appendicitis surgery, a bruised heel and a separate foot injury over the second half, leaving him just one game at normal playing time. Jones tore his Achilles in Week 10. Fourth receiver Greg Dortch suffered a season-ending injury in Week 13, and backup Simi Fehoko landed on injured reserve as well.

    Wilson played more of the X receiver role for the first time in his career, and he dominated it. He scored 169.5 PPR points over the final eight weeks, second-most among wide receivers. Every time Harrison got healthy for a short stretch, though, Wilson moved back to Z, and his production immediately took a hit.

    Arizona changed its coaching staff this offseason, bringing in Mike LaFleur from the Los Angeles Rams. He has publicly said Wilson will play the Puka Nacua role and Harrison the Davante Adams role. The biggest change in Wilson's route profile over the final eight weeks was fewer crossing routes and in routes and more slants. Nacua had his second-most receptions and second-most touchdowns on slants. His three other most common routes were hitches, go routes and out routes, which were also Wilson's next three most common.

    Wilson clearly hasn't shown the talent of Nacua, he doesn't have the quarterback Nacua has, and he will lose targets to a tight end in a way the Rams' receivers did not. Still, the role should be good enough for solid fantasy production. He should be a weekly flex option, and if he plays well enough, he could enter the WR2 conversation. Arizona invested too much in its running backs and depth receivers for Wilson to be a top-five wide receiver again.

    83. WR Alec Pierce, Indianapolis Colts

    Pierce has spent the first four seasons of his career as the Colts' X receiver. He was a player to avoid in fantasy for his first three seasons, with a target rate below 16% in each year. Last season, his target rate increased to 17.4%, and he maintained a 20.1 average depth of target, meaning only 51 of his 83 targets were catchable. He brought in 47 of those passes for 1,003 yards and six touchdowns.

    Players with a high average depth of target and a low-to-average target rate are typically difficult to trust in fantasy, because one week they will make a big play or two, and the next they could be held to just one or two receptions. Pierce had seven weeks as a top-24 fantasy wide receiver but also four weeks outside the top 50, resulting in a WR27 finish on the season.

    Pierce signed a four-year, $116 million contract this offseason, signaling he will remain a major part of the Colts' offense. Adonai Mitchell was traded mid-season last year, and Michael Pittman Jr. was dealt to Pittsburgh this offseason, leaving roughly 125 vacated targets in the receiver room to be redistributed among Pierce, Josh Downs, and backups like Ashton Dulin and Nick Westbrook-Ikhine.

    While Pierce should still receive several deep targets in 2026, there is reason to believe he will also see more shorter targets. He was targeted just 14 times on passes of 9 yards or less last season, down from 38 as a rookie. Pittman received 68 targets on short passes last season, and at least some of those will go Pierce's way.

    There is some concern about Pierce recovering from ankle surgery, though he is expected to be back for training camp. Quarterback Daniel Jones has also faced health questions, but all indications suggest he is ahead of schedule, and he recently participated in 7-on-7 work at OTAs. As it becomes clearer that both Pierce and Jones will be ready for the season, Pierce's ADP will likely rise.

    84. QB Brock Purdy, San Francisco 49ers

    Purdy consistently outperforms his ADP in fantasy points per game. He has ranked among the top 12 quarterbacks in each of his three seasons as a starter, and that includes stretches with elite receiving options like Brandon Aiyuk, Deebo Samuel, Jauan Jennings, George Kittle and Christian McCaffrey all on the field together, as well as stretches when most of his intended skill players were hurt. Last season, his wide receivers were Jennings, Ricky Pearsall, Demarcus Robinson and Kendrick Bourne.

    He is currently being drafted outside the top 12 quarterbacks but is worth one of those picks based on his play. Purdy has a 91.1 offensive grade over the past three seasons, sixth among quarterbacks, behind Josh Allen, Joe Burrow, Lamar Jackson, Matthew Stafford and Justin Herbert.

    San Francisco invested in its receiver room this offseason, adding veterans Mike Evans and Christian Kirk and drafting De'Zhaun Stribling in the second round. They join Pearsall and should give the 49ers their best receiver group in years.

    The biggest concern for Purdy is how much he leans on veterans near the end of their careers. Christian McCaffrey, Kyle Juszczyk, Mike Evans, George Kittle and Trent Williams are all among the oldest starters at their positions. There is a decent chance one or more of them starts to decline, dragging down the whole offense, in which case San Francisco will need its younger players to step up.

    Purdy is one of a handful of undervalued quarterbacks, which makes waiting at the position an easier decision.

    85. WR Marvin Harrison Jr., Arizona Cardinals

    Harrison was the fourth overall pick in the 2024 draft, arriving with lofty expectations as the top wide receiver prospect in years. He started immediately as the Cardinals' X receiver and caught four touchdowns over his first four games, but he did not produce as consistently as many fantasy managers expected, held under 50 receiving yards in 10 of 17 games. Part of the problem was his chemistry with Kyler Murray, as Harrison posted an abnormally high rate of uncatchable targets. He finished as the WR30, generally fine for a first-round rookie, but short of his ADP. It stung especially when Brian Thomas Jr. and Malik Nabers finished as top-six fantasy receivers.

    Harrison had a slightly better start to his 2025 season, moving up to 27th in PPR points. He still caught a touchdown nearly every other game, but his volume was inconsistent week to week. Murray was thought to be part of the problem, yet Harrison's fantasy points per game were identical, 12.5, across his five games with Murray to open the season and the four that followed with Jacoby Brissett.

    The wideout then dealt with a concussion, appendicitis surgery, a bruised heel and a separate foot injury over the second half, leaving him just one game at normal playing time. During his absence, Michael Wilson put up more fantasy points than Harrison has at any point in his two seasons.

    Arizona has a new head coach in Mike LaFleur, who comes from the Los Angeles Rams. LaFleur has said Harrison will play the Davante Adams role, which will be a bit of a change for him. Adams gained most of his receiving yards on in routes and out routes, while most of his touchdowns came on go routes very close to the end zone. Harrison's profile has leaned on hitches, more traditional go routes and crossing routes, and in college, he also ran more posts and corners.

    It is possible these new assignments are what Harrison needs to unlock his NFL potential. It is also possible he matches Adams' relatively low reception and yardage totals without the touchdowns, given how the Rams‘ offense compares to the Cardinals‘. He is best viewed as a high-risk, high-reward flex option.

    86. WR Quentin Johnston, Los Angeles Chargers

    Johnston was a first-round pick by the Chargers in 2023, joining a deep roster with Keenan Allen, Mike Williams and Josh Palmer. He opened the season fourth on the depth chart, but a season-ending injury to Williams in Week 3 moved him into three-receiver sets by Week 4. Palmer then missed most of the second half of the season and Allen the end of it, pushing Johnston to at least 80% of snaps for much of the stretch run. He had one game with 91 yards but was held to 35 yards or fewer in 13 of 17.

    Los Angeles let both Allen and Williams go, leaving Johnston as the team's X receiver. It drafted Ladd McConkey, who became the top receiving option. Johnston found the end zone more often, totaling eight touchdowns, but still had several no-impact games, with six under 25 yards and 12 of 15 under 50. He did post two 100-yard games, including a Week 18 line of 13 catches for 186 yards. He finished 39th in fantasy points per game, his value almost entirely tied to scoring. His 2025 looked much like his 2024, with the production a bit more spread out, and he reached at least 70 receiving yards in six games.

    There is reason for optimism in 2026 with the addition of Mike McDaniel as offensive coordinator. McDaniel's offenses have been built largely on speed. Johnston ran only a 4.51 40-yard dash, good for his size, and his tracking data suggests he plays faster than that time, making him one of the more natural fits for a McDaniel offense on the roster. That offense, with Tyreek Hill and Jaylen Waddle, leaned on go and post routes. Johnston hasn't found as much success on those routes, but his average depth of target on them has been notably higher. A lower average depth should ideally lead to a higher catch rate and more fantasy value.

    There is reason for optimism in 2026 with the addition of Mike McDaniel as offensive coordinator. McDaniel's offenses have been built largely on speed. Johnston ran only a 4.51 40-yard dash, good for his size, and his tracking data suggests he plays faster than that time, making him one of the more natural fits for a McDaniel offense on the roster. That offense, with Tyreek Hill and Jaylen Waddle, leaned on go and post routes. Johnston hasn't found as much success on those routes, but his average depth of target on them has been notably higher. A lower average depth should ideally lead to a higher catch rate and more fantasy value.

    The most likely outcome is that Johnston is a frustrating, touchdown-dependent flex option, but there is also a chance he takes another step forward and becomes a weekly starter.

    87. WR Michael Pittman Jr., Pittsburgh Steelers

    Pittman spent the first six seasons of his career as the Z receiver for the Indianapolis Colts, drawing triple-digit targets in each of the past five. He peaked as the WR13 in PPR leagues in 2023. He played through a back injury in 2024 that greatly hurt his quality of play, and last season the emergence of Alec Pierce and the addition of Tyler Warren cut into his total production, even as his efficiency when targeted improved. He finished as the WR30.

    He was traded to the Steelers this offseason, as Indianapolis needed the cap space to pay Pierce and had other underneath options in Warren and Josh Downs. Pittsburgh lacked consistency at receiver last season outside of D.K. Metcalf, adding Adam Thielen and Marquez Valdes-Scantling midseason and making both a major part of the offense.

    The good news is that Pittsburgh moved on from offensive coordinator Arthur Smith. Over his two seasons there, the Steelers ranked ninth in run plays and 27th in dropbacks. New head coach and offensive leader Mike McCarthy takes over, and in his five seasons with the Dallas Cowboys, they ranked fourth in pass plays and 10th in rushing attempts.

    The receivers who have thrived in McCarthy's offenses profile more like Pittman than Metcalf. Pittman's average depth of target more closely resembles that of CeeDee Lamb, Amari Cooper, Donald Driver, Davante Adams and Randall Cobb, while Metcalf's lines up more with Michael Gallup, Brandin Cooks, Jordy Nelson, Greg Jennings and James Jones. The first group has found much more fantasy success, though the second has done reasonably well too.

    The catch is that Pittman will be catching passes from Aaron Rodgers. Rodgers played well for a 41-year-old last season, but he is now 42, and it is entirely possible he continues to decline. Given that age, McCarthy could shift the offense's philosophy to protect Rodgers' arm, which could limit Pittman's fantasy value.

    Pittman can be drafted as a flex option with the upside of becoming Pittsburgh's top target. But there is also a risk that second-round rookie Germie Bernard starts his career strong and overtakes Pittman as the second receiver. In that case, Pittman might play only in three-receiver sets and not be worth a fantasy starting spot.

    88. RB Jadarian Price, Seattle Seahawks

    Price emerged as the second-best running back in the class despite being a backup last season. He has the least collegiate experience among the top-10 running backs in the class, but his 6.0 yards per carry ranked second among the top-20 running backs behind Love, and he is tied for the most yards after contact per carry at 4.3.

    Where he really stands out is his rate of making big plays, posting both the best rate of runs with a 1.5 grade or better and the highest rate of runs with a 1.0 grade among the class. His inexperience as a receiver is a red flag, with just 15 receptions over the last three seasons and a target rate of 9% per route run that is by far the lowest among the top-20 running backs. However, PFF's draft guide notes his strength and willingness as a pass protector could still allow him to develop into a three-down back.

    Most big boards projected Price as a second-round player, but he went higher than expected, landing with Seattle at the end of the first round. The Seahawks were arguably the team most in need of a running back after losing Kenneth Walker III in free agency and Zach Charbonnet to an ACL injury.

    The Seahawks ran an interesting backfield rotation last season, with Walker handling early-down work and Charbonnet serving as both the receiving back and goal-line back. This was an unusual split that made it difficult to trust either in fantasy despite how productive Seattle's offense was. Price could find himself in the Walker role given his lack of receiving experience, and even with Charbonnet sidelined, a free agent addition like Emanuel Wilson could absorb the receiving and goal-line snaps.

    Price should be selected ahead of most running backs in a committee situation given his upside and team context, but he carries the same risk as any committee back. Offseason news about his role, the usage of other Seahawks running backs, and Charbonnet's recovery timeline will all likely influence his redraft ADP.

    Charbonnet received light work in OTAs, which is a month earlier than expected, suggesting he might be ready to play sooner than expected. It’s early, but Price has been hit-or-miss as a receiver without pads. George Holani has been working with the first team throughout OTAs, and while Price is expected to pass Holani on the depth chart at some point, Holani could make this a three-man backfield before Charbonnet returns. While Price’s upside remains, the risk has been increasing.

    89. WR Jayden Reed, Green Bay Packers

    Reed has spent the past three seasons as the Packers' slot receiver in 11 personnel whenever healthy. His 1,626 receiving yards from the slot are the sixth-most among wide receivers over that span, and his 13 slot touchdowns are second only to Amon-Ra St. Brown, just ahead of CeeDee Lamb, DeVonta Smith and Cooper Kupp.

    That has not necessarily led to fantasy success, because Green Bay has not used him in any significant capacity in two-receiver sets, even when dealing with multiple receiver injuries. The team has generally run a below-average rate of 11 personnel, and its game scripts make the problem worse for Reed's value. In 2024, he averaged 74 receiving yards per game in losses, and that rate was cut in half in wins.

    Reed caught three passes for 45 yards and a touchdown in Week 1 of 2025 but suffered both a collarbone and a foot injury in Week 2, costing him most of the season. He returned to his normal role for a few weeks late in the year and into the playoffs, but never topped 60 receiving yards or found the end zone over that stretch.

    The Packers clearly still have faith in Reed, who signed a three-year, $50.25 million extension with $20 million guaranteed. Green Bay traded Dontayvion Wicks and didn't re-sign Romeo Doubs while making no significant receiver signing, which makes it more likely Reed plays some snaps in two-receiver sets, given the thinner depth than usual on the outside. Even if he isn't a full-time player in those sets, a slight bump in snaps could be enough to make him a more reliable flex option. If Matthew Golden struggles or Christian Watson gets hurt, it is at least possible Reed becomes a full-time player in two-receiver sets, since the alternative is an unproven Savion Williams or Bo Melton.

    90. QB Caleb Williams, Chicago Bears

    Williams was a non-factor in single-quarterback leagues as a rookie, but the additions of Ben Johnson at head coach, Luther Burden III at wide receiver and Colston Loveland at tight end helped propel him to 19.0 fantasy points per game last season, tied for eighth-most among quarterbacks.

    Williams was not a consistent passer on a play-by-play basis. His 57.0% accuracy was second-worst among the 28 quarterbacks with at least 300 attempts, and his 23.6% uncatchable rate was the worst. Despite that, he managed a 71.1 regular-season PFF passing grade, right in the middle of the pack. His fantasy production was buoyed by volume and a strong 27-to-9 touchdown-to-interception ratio.

    Williams also ran 77 times for 383 yards, tied for seventh-most among quarterbacks, giving him a nice floor. Some statistical regression is likely given his accuracy concerns, but there is also reason to expect improvement in his second year in Ben Johnson's system. Williams’ young receivers played exceptionally well down the stretch last season, including in the playoffs, and should build on that momentum.

    Williams should be considered a fantasy starter this season. Given his age and likely long-term future with Johnson, Burden and Loveland, he ranks among the top dynasty options at quarterback.

    91. RB Jonathon Brooks, Carolina Panthers

    Brooks was the top running back in the 2024 draft class. Our draft guide praised his vision, footwork, change of direction, contact balance, burst, receiving ability and tackle-breaking skills, with no clear weaknesses in his profile.

    His lone negative was a torn ACL suffered in November of his final collegiate season, which ensured he would miss much of his rookie year. Even so, the Carolina Panthers made him the first running back selected in the 2024 NFL Draft. Brooks made his debut in Week 12, playing five snaps. He followed that with 15 snaps the next week, recording six carries and three receptions.

    Brooks tore his right ACL again the following week. The Panthers shut him down not only for the rest of 2024 but also for all of 2025, giving him ample time to recover and ensuring he would be ready for 2026. Carolina allowed Rico Dowdle to leave in free agency and didn't make a significant investment at running back, suggesting the team is counting on Brooks.

    Brooks looked great in OTAs, and while the Panthers may ease him into action early in the season, the starting job is his if he outplays Chuba Hubbard. Hubbard has earned an 81.8 PFF grade over the past three seasons, ranking in the middle of the pack among running backs with at least 1,500 snaps over that period.

    Brooks is an excellent late-round target as long as his ADP stays in this range. He is one of the few running backs available that late with a clear path to an every-down role without needing an injury ahead of him.

    92. TE Travis Kelce, Kansas City Chiefs

    Kelce spent 2016 through 2022 as the top fantasy tight end, apart from 2021, when he finished second. He missed two games in 2023 but still led the position in fantasy points per game. His decline began in 2024, when he fell to 12.2 fantasy points per game. His PFF receiving grade had ranged from 87.0 to 94.0 across that eight-year span, then dropped to 72.2 in 2024.

    Expectations for last year were for a further decline, given the return of Rashee Rice, projected improvement from Xavier Worthy and Kelce's age. Instead, Rice served a suspension early and was hurt at the end of the year, and Worthy took a step back rather than forward because of injury. Kelce's PFF grade barely moved, and he remained a significant part of the offense. His receptions per game still fell to 4.5, his fewest since 2014, but his receiving yards per game barely dipped and he scored more touchdowns. His 11.4 fantasy points per game ranked ninth among tight ends.

    Kelce will turn 37 by midseason but has chosen to return for another year. A rebound in his efficiency is unlikely after the past two seasons, yet he is still expected to be a large part of the Chiefs' offense. Kansas City moved on from Hollywood Brown and JuJu Smith-Schuster and replaced them only with fifth-round rookie Cyrus Allen, so barring a sharp drop in efficiency, Kelce should continue to see significant volume.

    Kansas City also brought back Eric Bieniemy, his offensive coordinator from 2018 to 2022, during the peak of Kelce's career. It is not clear whether the reunion will help him at this stage of his career, but it shouldn't hurt his production and could lift his target rate.

    Kelce is worth drafting as a fantasy starter, but the expectation should be someone you can start each week when things break right, not someone who will deliver top-five production. There is also a chance he slips out of the top 12 tight ends, given normal decline and a few other borderline TE1/TE2 options working out.

    93. TE Dalton Kincaid, Buffalo Bills

    When healthy, Kincaid has performed like one of the NFL’s best receiving tight ends. Among tight ends with at least 400 routes run over the past two seasons, his 82.0 PFF receiving grade ranks sixth. His 2.12 yards per route run trails only George Kittle and sits ahead of Trey McBride and Brock Bowers. Kincaid also leads all qualifying tight ends in target rate (24.9%) while ranking second in yards after the catch per reception (6.6).

    The issue has been availability.

    Kincaid suffered a collarbone injury in 2024 before tearing his PCL, an injury that continued to flare up throughout the 2025 season. He missed five games and played just 37.6% of offensive snaps on average across his 12 appearances. Notably, he eclipsed 14.0 PPR points in all three games in which he played more than 50% of Buffalo’s offensive snaps.

    The Bills’ offense should remain largely intact entering 2026, outside the addition of DJ Moore. Moore is expected to become Buffalo’s primary outside receiver, though his presence should not prevent Kincaid from remaining Josh Allen’s preferred option over the middle of the field.

    The injury history is concerning, but Kincaid arguably has the strongest quarterback situation among fantasy tight ends outside the elite tier. Alongside George Kittle and Mark Andrews, he is one of the few top-12 fantasy tight ends attached to a top-12 quarterback entering the season.

    Ultimately, Kincaid’s fantasy outlook depends on whether he can finally manage the lingering effects of his PCL injury. If healthy, he has the talent and role to finish among the league’s top fantasy tight ends. Still, fantasy managers drafting him would be wise to pair him with a safer secondary option, given the durability concerns.

    94. WR Chris Godwin Jr., Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    Godwin has spent the past nine seasons as a slot receiver for the Buccaneers. His best fantasy season came in 2019, when he caught 86 passes for 1,333 yards and nine touchdowns for the second-most fantasy points at the position. He averaged at least 60 receiving yards per game in each of the following five seasons.

    His 2024 season stood out, as he had former Los Angeles Rams assistant Liam Coen as his offensive coordinator. Godwin played a similar role to Cooper Kupp, producing 82.3 receiving yards per game, and he scored five touchdowns in his first seven games, as many as he tallied over his previous 32. Godwin ranked second in fantasy points per game before a dislocated ankle ended his season early.

    That offseason, Coen left for the Jacksonville Jaguars‘ head coaching job, and Godwin's injury kept him out until Week 4. He played two weeks, sat again until Week 12, then returned to more than 50% of offensive snaps in Week 13. He never got back to his usual snap rate, which was partly due to the injury but partly the talent Tampa Bay had at receiver. Mike Evans, Jalen McMillan and Godwin were the three receivers to open 2024, and the team added Emeka Egbuka and Tez Johnson in 2025, both of whom flashed as rookies. Godwin led the room in routes but averaged just 49.8 receiving yards per game over the rest of the season.

    Evans left in free agency, but Tampa Bay still has four talented receivers from last season and drafted Ted Hurst, who could fill some of the X role Evans vacated. The Buccaneers changed offensive coordinators again, bringing in Zac Robinson, who worked with Coen for four years in Los Angeles. That should mean an offense similar to the 2024 version that fueled Godwin's fantasy stardom, though it could just as easily be Egbuka absorbing many of those targets.

    There is bound to be some kind of receiver rotation, so Godwin may not return to his usual target rate. But there is a case that he is the most talented receiver in Tampa Bay, and he is by far the most experienced. Godwin’s range of outcomes is enormous, and the low end includes falling to third or fourth in the target pecking order, which would leave him a non-factor for fantasy.

    95. RB Jordan Mason, Minnesota Vikings

    Mason has been one of the top running backs in the NFL since joining the league in 2022. His 91.3 rushing grade over the last four seasons ranks 10th among all running backs. He has averaged 5.1 yards per carry and 3.4 yards after contact per carry, and he has generated a first down or touchdown on 26.6% of his runs. Those marks all rank third among the 56 running backs with at least 300 carries. He is the only running back in that group to have earned a positive play-level grade on over half of his runs.

    That has translated to fantasy success when he has been his team's lead back. He was RB10 in PPR leagues over the first seven weeks of 2024 with the San Francisco 49ers while Christian McCaffrey was injured, and he averaged 14.5 PPR points per game in his four starts last season with Minnesota, 16th-best among running backs during that span.

    He and Aaron Jones return to the Vikings, where they will likely continue to share the backfield, with Mason handling more early-down work and Jones taking third-down work. Jones has missed at least five games in two of the last three seasons and, at 31 years old, is the second-oldest back expected to make a 53-man roster. Chances are, Mason will be the primary running back for some stretch of 2026.

    The Vikings' offense should be much improved with Kyler Murray at quarterback, which should help Minnesota climb from 26th in scoring in 2025 toward the top half of the league. Mason should see the majority of goal-line carries, so a better offense should directly lift his fantasy production, including more fourth-quarter running opportunities, a category where Minnesota was below average last season.

    The offensive line should also be healthier. Four-fifths of it missed at least three games each last season, adding to the unit's instability throughout the year. Ryan Kelly‘s retirement doesn't help, but Minnesota retained the five linemen who started the most games for them last season. A healthier offensive line should help Mason’s efficiency.

    Mason shouldn't be relied on as a Week 1 fantasy starter, but he is one of the top handcuff options in fantasy, with real standalone value even if Jones stays healthy.

    96. QB Dak Prescott, Dallas Cowboys

    Prescott has spent a decade as the Cowboys' starting quarterback, with varying levels of fantasy success. He ranked eighth in fantasy points per game among quarterbacks last season, but was 22nd in 2024. He ranked among the top five in fantasy points per game in both 2019 and 2020, the latter cut to five games by a season-ending ankle injury, but that was in the first half of his career.

    His PFF grades have fluctuated over time, with passing grades ranging from 67 to 87 and little consistency from one season to the next. He ranks seventh among quarterbacks in passing grade over the last three seasons combined. Prescott offers minimal rushing production. He ran 53 times last season, but 20 of those were kneel-downs or fumbles that statistically went down as rushing attempts.

    On the bright side, almost everyone related to the offense remains the same as last season. Prescott has two of the best wide receivers at his disposal, a strong offensive line and a coaching staff willing to throw the ball.

    On the bright side, almost everyone related to the offense remains the same as last season. Prescott has two of the best wide receivers at his disposal, a strong offensive line and a coaching staff willing to throw the ball.

    There is reason to expect some minor regression from Prescott. He dropped back 659 times last season, in part because the Cowboys allowed a league-high 511 points. Dallas spent two first-round picks on defense, another three defenders in the top four rounds, has signed seven unrestricted free agents and has made two trades. They also kept key contributors Quinnen Williams and DaRon Bland. This defense should be improved from last season. That will mean fewer points allowed, and Dallas shouldn't need to throw as much late in games.

    97. RB Blake Corum, Los Angeles Rams

    Corum was a third-round pick by the Rams in 2024. There was some thought he could take over as the lead back, and since Sean McVay tends to lean on one clear leader, that would have made Corum a steal at his rookie ADP. Instead, he remained a backup, topping 20% of offensive snaps in only one game.

    Los Angeles added Jarquez Hunter in the 2025 draft, which made it look like Corum could slide down the depth chart. Instead, he consistently played between 20% and 30% of snaps over the first half of the season while running at a high level. That earned him more work in the second half, when he was regularly at 30% to 40%, including a steady 37% in the playoffs.

    Corum was among the league's most effective runners last season. His 5.1 yards per carry ranked fifth among running backs with at least 125 carries, and his 30.3% first-down-or-touchdown rate was second. He earned a positive grade on 52.4% of his runs, which led all running backs.

    The general expectation is that his snap rate holds near where it ended last season, or climbs a bit. Kyren Williams wasn't far behind on those rushing metrics but was far more involved as a receiver, catching 36 passes to Corum's eight. Corum also dropped three passes.

    The Rams are expected to be the league's best team after significant defensive improvements, which should push them to run even more than last season. That makes Los Angeles one of the more likely teams to support two fantasy-relevant running backs. But as long as Corum has next to no role in the passing game, it caps his upside.

    98. WR Wan'Dale Robinson, Tennessee Titans

    Robinson spent the first four seasons of his career as a slot receiver for the New York Giants. Injuries hampered his first two seasons, but he became a frequent target in 2024. His 93 receptions ranked 10th among wide receivers, though his 699 yards ranked just 44th. His 5.3 average depth of target was the lowest for a wide receiver with at least 125 targets since Wes Welker with the New England Patriots in 2008.

    In 2025, his role expanded to include two-receiver sets after Malik Nabers suffered a season-ending injury. His targets per route rose, as did his average depth of target. His first-down total barely changed, but his explosive plays doubled, producing a career-high 1.87 yards per route run. He finished 14th in fantasy points and 17th in points per game.

    His play-caller, Brian Daboll, left to become the Tennessee Titans' offensive coordinator, and Robinson followed him, signing a four-year, $70 million contract with $38 million guaranteed. Having a play-caller willing to lean on him is a plus, but he will also face significantly more competition for targets. Once Nabers went down, the only other Giants receiver with more than 10 receptions was Darius Slayton.

    The Titans drafted Carnell Tate with the fourth overall pick, and he is expected to be their X receiver and target leader. Calvin Ridley remains after missing most of last season with an injury. Elic Ayomanor and Chimere Dike spent much of last year as two of the three receivers in 11 personnel and now project as backups. It would not be surprising if Robinson is back to playing only in 11 personnel, and he might not even see every 11-personnel snap, with Dike mixing in. That means fewer routes and fewer targets per route.

    Robinson should still see enough volume to be a weekly flex play, but he is a low-upside option as long as the other Titans receivers stay healthy.

    99. RB J.K. Dobbins, Denver Broncos

    Dobbins has spent four years with the Baltimore Ravens, one with the Los Angeles Chargers and one with the Broncos. He has been highly effective as a runner at every stop, with a career 5.2 yards per carry. In Denver, he was the lead back in a three-back committee, with Tyler Badie the third-down back and second-round rookie R.J. Harvey his backup. He played 50.8% of the Broncos' offensive snaps over the first 10 weeks, with double-digit carries in every game but only 11 receptions total. He averaged 11.6 fantasy points per game, 27th among running backs.

    The problem with Dobbins has always been injuries. Leg and ankle problems cost him all of 2021, half of 2022 and nearly all of 2023. He also missed four games midway through the season with the Chargers and, after a foot sprain, the final seven games with the Broncos.

    Denver retained Dobbins, and he remains atop the depth chart, though his usage will probably slip. The Broncos drafted Jonah Coleman in the fourth round, and he figures to replace Badie in the rotation while cutting into Dobbins' carries more than Badie did. Harvey, meanwhile, gained much more experience while Dobbins was out and could see a larger role this season.

    Dobbins should remain effective when given the chance, but his limited volume will likely keep him out of the top 24 running backs. His upside is capped: even if Harvey or Coleman gets hurt, Denver would probably turn to Badie or Jaleel McLaughlin, if either is still on the roster or practice squad, rather than hand one of its primary backs a big jump in snaps.

    That makes Dobbins a solid but low-upside flex option, as long as he stays healthy.

    100. TE Mark Andrews, Baltimore Ravens

    Andrews has spent his eight-year NFL career as a rotational tight end for the Ravens. In his second season, he made the Pro Bowl and finished fifth in fantasy points despite playing 44% of Baltimore's offensive snaps and never exceeding 57% in a single game all year, a sign his low usage held steady rather than spiking late in the season. His playing time climbed in later years, then declined again as the Ravens added two other capable tight ends in Isaiah Likely and Charlie Kolar.

    Andrews was a must-start tight end from 2019 to 2024, finishing among the top eight in fantasy points every season, including a run as the top fantasy tight end in 2021. However, he fell to 24th in fantasy points per game last season at 7.7. His decline began in 2024, as his receptions dropped to 3.2 per game, but his 11 touchdowns made up for it. Last season, that dipped slightly to 2.8, and he scored only five times.

    His quality of play also slipped. His average depth of target dropped to a career-low 8.0 yards. That usually opens up more yards after the catch, but he instead posted a career-low 2.1. Some of that traced to Lamar Jackson‘s injury, though. With Jackson, Andrews’ average depth was 9.0 with 2.6 yards after the catch per reception; without him, it was 5.7 and 1.3.

    The good news for 2026 is that both Likely and Kolar are gone, replaced by Durham Smythe and a pair of third-day rookies. That should mean a significant bump from last season's 62% snap rate for Andrews. The Ravens also have a new coaching staff, with Declan Doyle as offensive coordinator and play-caller. Doyle worked with Colston Loveland last season and was a tight ends coach before joining the Bears. Both new head coach Jesse Minter and local beat writers have called Andrews a major focal point of the Baltimore offense after OTAs.

    There is a risk Andrews keeps declining given his age, but he should remain the second-leading target-earner in one of the league's most promising offenses. If he bounces back, he would return to top-five territory, and his ADP is significantly discounted from past seasons.

    101. RB Chuba Hubbard, Carolina Panthers

    Hubbard has spent five years as one of the Panthers' running backs. Carolina has consistently added competition, including D'Onta Foreman, Miles Sanders and Rico Dowdle, but Hubbard has outlasted all of them. His best season came in 2024, with career highs in carries (250), rushing yards (1,195), rushing touchdowns (10) and receptions (43), good for the 13th-most PPR points per game among running backs at 16.1.

    Hubbard's play declined last season, as his 69.8 offensive grade was his lowest since his rookie year. That, along with a few excellent games from Dowdle, left him a backup for most of the second half. From Week 9 on, Hubbard was held to eight carries or fewer in every matchup except the regular-season and playoff meetings with the Los Angeles Rams.

    The Panthers didn't re-sign Dowdle, but they have Jonathon Brooks returning from injury. Carolina drafted Brooks in 2024, though he entered the league coming off a torn ACL and then tore it again in his third NFL game, costing him the rest of 2024 and all of 2025. Reports during OTAs have been positive. Carolina will likely be cautious with Brooks early, given how little live football he has played in recent years, but it would not be surprising if he takes over the backfield once healthy.

    That makes drafting Hubbard a calculated risk. Any manager who needs a running back, particularly for the first few weeks of the season, should give him strong consideration. There is also a chance Brooks can't return to his previous level, or that the team won't trust him with a heavy workload, in which case Hubbard stays the starter the rest of the way.

    Hubbard's ADP could shift significantly during training camp depending on how glowing the Brooks reports are.

    Tier 7

    102. WR Makai Lemon, Philadelphia Eagles

    Lemon is a unique receiver in that he spent most of his college career in the slot. Several wide receivers selected in the top 20 of recent drafts also played significant snaps in the slot, including Drake London, Treylon Burks, Malik Nabers and Emeka Egbuka. All four transitioned to playing more on the outside in the NFL, but they were also bigger than Lemon.

    Lemon lands with the Philadelphia Eagles, where he should be their primary slot receiver. While DeVonta Smith has played well in the slot earlier in his career, Smith received an 89.9 receiving grade when lined up out wide last season, compared to 62.9 in the slot. We can expect Smith as the primary Z receiver, Lemon in the slot and Dontayvion Wicks as the X receiver. This distribution might leave Wicks with more playing time than Lemon, but Lemon is largely expected to see more targets than Wicks.

    While Wicks is lining up in A.J. Brown's former spot, Sean Mannion is the new offensive coordinator for Philadelphia. The Eagles had the fifth-fewest targets to wide receivers in the slot over the last three seasons, while every offense Mannion has been a part of has been around league average, or slightly below league average. Ideally, the Eagles would have an offensive playcaller who focused even more on slot receivers, but we should still see an uptick in slot targets this season compared to recent years.

    Considering the Eagles are a run-heavy team, and Lemon will be their second receiving option, his fantasy value could be limited unless he excels immediately. However, Brown and Smith both finished among the top-20 fantasy wide receivers in two of the last three seasons, so if Lemon plays well enough and the Eagles offense is playing well enough, he could be an immediate fantasy starter.

    He should be viewed as a high-risk, high-reward, low-end WR3/high-end WR4 heading into his rookie season.

    103. TE Isaiah Likely, New York Giants

    Likely began his career behind Mark Andrews on the Ravens’ depth chart. Although he logged meaningful snaps — particularly in 12 personnel packages — he consistently competed with Andrews and Zay Flowers for targets, resulting in a below-average target rate. That limited many of his traditional production metrics, though his efficiency remained impressive. Over the past two seasons, Likely earned a positively graded play on 9.7% of his targets, the sixth-highest rate among qualifying tight ends.

    He now reunites with former Ravens head coach John Harbaugh in New York after signing a contract worth $40 million in total value, making him the NFL’s sixth-highest-paid tight end. Likely remains a risky fantasy projection because his raw receiving production has not yet matched his reputation, but the opportunity for a breakout workload clearly exists with the Giants.

    New York’s tight ends failed to produce consistently for fantasy purposes last season, though rookie quarterback Jaxson Dart showed a much stronger willingness to target the position than the Giants’ other quarterbacks. Dart targeted tight ends on 23.9% of his throws compared to just 15.8% from the rest of the quarterback room.

    The Giants also reshaped their receiving corps by adding Darnell Mooney and Calvin Austin III in free agency before selecting Malachi Fields in the third round of the draft. Still, Likely’s primary competition for targets will come from Malik Nabers.

    Nabers missed most of last season with a torn ACL and recently underwent a cleanup procedure, leaving his availability for training camp and Week 1 uncertain. Even once he returns, there is no guarantee he will resume playing at full strength immediately. That creates a realistic path for Likely to lead the Giants in targets.

    If he can maintain his explosive playmaking efficiency while taking on a significantly larger workload, Likely has legitimate elite fantasy upside.

    104. WR Xavier Worthy, Kansas City Chiefs

    Worthy was a first-round pick in the 2024 NFL draft. The speedy receiver was seen as a deep threat for Kansas City but made little impact for most of his rookie season, averaging 32 yards per game over his first 11 games with an average depth of target in the double digits for most of them. The Chiefs then used him more in the slot with a lower average depth of target the rest of the way, the role where Rashee Rice thrived as a rookie and early in 2024 before his injury. Worthy's average jumped to 57 yards per game over his next six. He then gained 85 yards in the AFC Championship Game and caught eight passes for 157 yards and two touchdowns in the Super Bowl loss.

    Last season, his average depth of target climbed back to 12.5. JuJu Smith-Schuster often took the slot and the low-average-depth role when Rice was out. Worthy was meant to be the deep target, yet both Tyquan Thornton and Hollywood Brown gained more receiving yards on deep passes. His incomplete deep balls were uncatchable, and something was off with his chemistry with Patrick Mahomes.

    Worthy suffered a shoulder injury in Week 1 and did not have surgery until after the season. Reports later suggested the shoulder bothered him all year, limiting what he could do. On routes where a receiver needs to be more physical, he simply could not. This looks like a case where playing through injury shouldn't be held against him.

    He was named the unofficial MVP of Kansas City's OTAs. The Chiefs moved on from Brown and Smith-Schuster and added only fifth-round rookie Cyrus Allen, a sign they still trust Worthy as a starter and key part of the passing game. It is also worth noting how young he entered the league; he is actually younger than Allen and other receivers in this year's rookie class. It is fair to expect improvement at his age regardless of the injury. Given Rice's off-field issues and Travis Kelce‘s age, it is entirely possible Worthy leads the Chiefs in receiving yards. New offensive coordinator Eric Bieniemy centered his offense around Tyreek Hill, and Worthy could fill that role.

    Worthy is a flex option in drafts, with the upside of a top-10 finish if he becomes the top weapon in Kansas City's offense.

    105. RB Kenny Gainwell, Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    Gainwell spent the first four seasons of his career as the Eagles' receiving back. He was the 2023 Week 1 starter after the team moved on from Miles Sanders, but he was hurt in that game, D'Andre Swift ran 28 times for 175 yards in Week 2, and the opportunity was gone. In 2024, the Eagles added Saquon Barkley, further reducing Gainwell's role.

    He joined the Pittsburgh Steelers in free agency in 2025. They moved on from Najee Harris and drafted Kaleb Johnson, and while it appeared Gainwell would be the third option, he ended up as the receiving back. He started two games, running 19 times for 99 yards and two touchdowns in the first and 10 times for 92 yards in the second.

    Outside of those two starts, his playing time was only up slightly from most of his Philadelphia seasons, but his targets per route jumped to 24.5%, producing significantly more receiving output. He ranked fifth among all running backs in receiving yards and fourth in receptions.

    This offseason, he joins the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, replacing Rachaad White on the roster. That should put his target rate higher than in Philadelphia but lower than in Pittsburgh. If he is limited to the receiving role, he is unlikely to have much fantasy value, but his upside is becoming the top back over Bucky Irving.

    Irving missed six weeks in 2025 with a foot sprain and a shoulder AC joint sprain and wasn't the same after his return. He also missed most of OTAs, giving Gainwell plenty of time to impress the new coaching staff. The coaches, though, have consistently listed Irving first in the backfield committee so far.

    Gainwell should mostly be viewed as a handcuff, but one of the best available, given his strong play last season and his better-than-usual odds of ending up the starter.

    106. WR Stefon Diggs, Washington Commanders

    Diggs was among the league's best receivers in his prime, spending five years with the Minnesota Vikings and four with the Buffalo Bills, including a six-year run with at least 1,000 yards and six touchdowns in every season. He then played 2024 with the Houston Texans and 2025 with the New England Patriots. He logged only 54.7% of New England's snaps but still gained 1,013 yards, making him one of the most efficient receivers in football on a per-route basis. His 2.42 yards per route run ranked fifth among all wide receivers.

    New England moved on from the 32-year-old in favor of trading for A.J. Brown and signing Romeo Doubs. Diggs spent the offseason battling lawsuits, which likely contributed to the Patriots' decision.

    No team has signed him to this point. He is one of several veteran receivers still without a home, a group that includes Tyreek Hill, Deebo Samuel, Keenan Allen, DeAndre Hopkins, Tyler Lockett and Brandin Cooks. Diggs is arguably the best of the bunch, but cheaper options may appeal more to teams depending on their needs.

    If Diggs signs somewhere, he instantly becomes worth drafting, even if only as a late-round pick in standard leagues. The risk that he never signs is real, and that uncertainty is what pushes him down the rankings and makes him a deep-league option for now.

    107. RB Aaron Jones Sr., Minnesota Vikings

    Jones spent seven seasons with the Packers and has spent the past two as the Minnesota Vikings' lead back. He is one of 16 running backs in NFL history to average 4.9 yards per carry or better for their career.

    He finished as the RB14 in his first year with the Vikings, though 20th in fantasy points per game. His 75.8 offensive and rushing grades were both career lows, and his 18 touches per game ranked 12th. He wasn't as effective as at earlier points in his career, but he was still around league average. His main problem was a lack of touchdowns relative to his touches, with seven total.

    Jones took another step back in 2025, his offensive grade dropping to 65.0 and his rushing grade to 69.5. A hamstring injury cost him four games early in the season, and a hip strain cost him the end of it. When healthy, his touches per game fell to 13.3, as the Vikings added Jordan Mason and gave him significantly more competition.

    Jones ranked 33rd in fantasy points per game, and his efficiency could bounce back if he stays healthy. But he is also the second-oldest halfback expected to make a roster. Mason graded notably better as a runner, at 83.8, and the two were comparable as receivers. Minnesota also added Demond Claiborne in the sixth round, and there is a chance he takes over some of the receiving work.

    At best, Jones remains the starter and bounces back, making him an RB2 or flex option. At worst, his role shrinks, with Mason taking the majority of the carries and Claiborne most of the receiving work.

    108. QB Kyler Murray, Minnesota Vikings

    Murray finished as a top-12 fantasy quarterback in points per game in each of the first five seasons of his career. His fantasy value declined in 2024 because of injury, as his rushing attempts per game dropped to 4.6. That number rebounded to 5.8 in 2025, but his fantasy production did not follow, as he posted career lows in yards per attempt and passing yards per game across five healthy starts.

    Murray joins a Vikings offense led by Kevin O'Connell, whose system has consistently produced fantasy-relevant quarterbacks outside of last season. Minnesota typically operates at an average-to-high pace and consistently ranks among the league leaders in pass rate. Sam Darnold finished as a top-10 fantasy quarterback in 2024, while Kirk Cousins did so in 2022 and ranked in the top 10 in fantasy points per game in 2023. After Cousins suffered a season-ending injury in 2023, Joshua Dobbs concluded as a top-12 fantasy quarterback in three of the four games in which he played every offensive snap, while Nick Mullens placed among the top 14 in games where he handled the full workload. Both quarterbacks also posted top-five weekly finishes.

    The Vikings' offense struggled last season, largely because of inconsistent quarterback play and persistent injuries along the offensive line. Center Ryan Kelly missed half the season, while Christian Darrisaw and Brian O'Neill were also sidelined for significant time and played through injuries when available. The addition of Murray, combined with improved health up front, should help the Vikings’ offense return to its typical level of play.

    It is also worth noting that Murray performed at his best alongside an elite wide receiver. While he played with several strong starters in Arizona, his best stretch came with DeAndre Hopkins from 2020 to 2022. Hopkins' final All-Pro season came in 2020, the same year Murray finished as the overall QB2. Murray now joins forces with Justin Jefferson, who has earned higher PFF receiving grades in recent seasons than Hopkins did during his time with the Cardinals.

    If both Murray and the Vikings’ offense return to their expected level of play, he has a chance to be a consistent fantasy starter in single-quarterback leagues.

    109. RB RJ Harvey, Denver Broncos

    Harvey was the 60th overall pick of the 2025 draft by the Broncos, arriving with lofty expectations. Receiving backs in Sean Payton's offenses have found significant success in the past, including Alvin Kamara, Darren Sproles, Reggie Bush and Pierre Thomas, among others.

    Though Harvey was thought to form a two-back committee with J.K. Dobbins, Tyler Badie handled third downs, restricting the playing time of both Dobbins and Harvey. Harvey was largely a non-factor for fantasy purposes over the first 10 weeks, playing 20% to 30% of offensive snaps most games.

    It wasn't until Dobbins suffered a season-ending injury that Harvey became the lead back. He received plenty of volume and scored some touchdowns, but he averaged just 3.7 yards per carry in the regular season and 3.0 in the playoffs. From Week 11 on, the first-year rusher averaged 15.2 PPR points per game, 13th among running backs.

    There is a wide range of outcomes for Harvey in 2026. The Broncos added Jonah Coleman in the fourth round, who will likely replace Badie as the third option. All three should see noteworthy playing time, and whoever plays best will earn more.

    Harvey could vault into the top spot, especially if the often-injured Dobbins gets hurt again, and hold top-20 value like he did to end last season. But Harvey could also settle in as an occasional receiving back and see even less time than he did early last season if Coleman plays well.

    110. QB Bo Nix, Denver Broncos

    Nix has spent the past two seasons as the Broncos' starting quarterback. He has finished seventh in fantasy points each year, though he has benefited from staying healthy both regular seasons, and he has ranked ninth and 11th in points per game.

    His 75.9 PFF passing grade over the past two seasons ranks 20th among the 45 quarterbacks with at least 45 dropbacks, so he has outproduced that mark for fantasy purposes for a few reasons. He has leaned heavily on volume, ranking second in total dropbacks at 1,402. Denver has had a top-10 defense, and Nix has had the best pass-blocking offensive line in each of the past two seasons. That line has stayed remarkably healthy, with its most important members, Garett Bolles and Quinn Meinerz, not missing a game and the other three each playing at least 1,500 snaps over the span.

    The good news is that running is part of Nix's game. He ranks fifth in rushing attempts and eighth in rushing yards over the past two seasons. He can't elude tacklers the way some rushing quarterbacks can, but his willingness to run has helped make him a top-10 fantasy quarterback both years. Denver also traded for Jaylen Waddle this offseason, giving him a new top weapon, which should help his passing efficiency.

    The downside is that Denver could lean on the run this season. J.K. Dobbins is healthy again, the team should trust RJ Harvey more in his second year, and it drafted Jonah Coleman. A team with a great defense typically pairs it with heavy rushing once it has the lead, which could cut into Nix's volume. He may also run less himself, having fractured his right ankle in the divisional round last year, and while all signs point to him playing in Week 1, he might not run as often. Some regression in the offensive line's health is possible too.

    It is entirely possible Nix looks like a better quarterback this year while posting worse fantasy production. His ADP is rightfully a bit lower than where he has finished the past two years. He is one of several quarterbacks who should be paired with another in single-quarterback leagues.

    111. QB Jaxson Dart, New York Giants

    Dart was highly impressive as a rookie from a fantasy perspective, averaging 20.5 fantasy points per start, seventh among quarterbacks. His rushing ability drove much of that production, as his 86 rushing attempts ranked fourth at the position and resulted in nine rushing touchdowns. As a passer, however, he averaged just 6.7 yards per attempt and threw 15 touchdown passes.

    There are several reasons to expect improvement from Dart and the Giants' passing game this season. By the end of last year, his top receiving options were Darius Slayton, Isaiah Hodgins, Gunner Olszewski and tight end Daniel Bellinger. This season, Malik Nabers is expected to be healthy, while the Giants added Darnell Mooney, Calvin Austin III and Malachi Fields at wide receiver and Isaiah Likely at tight end. Running back Cam Skattebo and tight end Theo Johnson should also be healthy to begin the season, and New York used the 10th overall pick on offensive lineman Francis Mauigoa to further bolster the offense.

    The biggest question is Nabers' health. If he is not ready for Week 1 or is limited during the season, Dart's chances of joining the top tier of fantasy quarterbacks become much slimmer.

    Injury concerns are the primary reason Dart is not ranked higher. He missed two games because of a concussion and underwent concussion evaluations several other times. While he could adjust his playing style to reduce the number of head impacts, additional concussions would increase the risk of missed time. Ranking him outside the top tier reflects that risk.

    112. QB Patrick Mahomes, Kansas City Chiefs

    At his best, Mahomes is the league's best quarterback. He averaged 26.1 fantasy points per game in 2018 despite only 272 rushing yards and two rushing touchdowns. He finished first again in 2022 and was a clear-cut fantasy starter in every year between. He fell outside the top 10 fantasy quarterbacks in 2023 and 2024 before jumping back to a tie for second last season.

    Part of that decline came with a drop in passing grade. He posted three seasons at 89.0 or better in his first five years as a starter, then slid steadily to 70.7 last season. His average target depth fell from 9.5 at his peak to 6.8 and 6.9 in 2023 and 2024. It climbed back to 8.3 last season, which helped his yards per attempt and yards per game, but also brought more negatively graded and less accurate throws.

    The big reason for his fantasy bounce-back was his rushing. He ran once more per game than the previous season, averaged 1.3 more yards per carry and scored three more touchdowns. That added 2.5 fantasy points per game, while his passing rose by just 0.3.

    Mahomes then suffered a torn ACL and LCL. Signs point to him playing in Week 1, but he is unlikely to keep running as much as he did last season because of the injury. There are also question marks around his receivers, as Travis Kelce is another year older, Rashee Rice could face a suspension at some point, and Xavier Worthy had a down year.

    The good news is that Eric Bieniemy is back as offensive coordinator. He held the role from 2018 to 2022, when Mahomes was at his fantasy best. If Kansas City returns to the offense that made him a fantasy star and MVP, that could lift his production despite the concerns.

    In single-quarterback leagues, Mahomes should be drafted alongside another option, so whoever has the best matchup that week can slot into your lineup.

    113. QB Matthew Stafford, Los Angeles Rams

    Stafford was the league's MVP last season. He led the league in passing yards per game at 276.9, only slightly above his career average. His 7.9 yards per attempt tied for the third-best mark of his career. What really set him apart statistically was his touchdowns and interceptions. He threw a touchdown on 7.7% of his passes, a career high, and an interception on just 1.3%, a career low outside of his 2010 season, when he played only three games.

    All of that added up to the fourth-most fantasy points per game at 21.1, but significant regression is fair to expect. If his touchdown rate falls back to his 2023 rate, his best season from 2022 to 2024, he would have averaged 4.3 fewer fantasy points per game, which would have put him at QB18. He coincidentally finished as QB18 that season.

    Stafford's age, 38, is also a concern. His play clearly didn't regress last season, but at some point a decline is inevitable. There is also a chance Los Angeles is simply too good this season. The Rams led the league in points scored but ranked only 10th in points allowed. They added Myles Garrett, Trent McDuffie and Jaylen Watson to the defense, and there are at least rumors that Aaron Donald could join them. If that defense improves as expected, the Rams won't need to throw as much and will spend the fourth quarter running the ball with backups.

    Stafford might only belong in fantasy starting lineups in weeks the game projects to be close, which means he shouldn't be drafted as a starter in single-quarterback leagues. He is also a notable case of a quarterback ranked much lower in dynasty than in redraft, given his age.

    114. RB Jacory Croskey-Merritt, Washington Commanders

    Croskey-Merritt was the talk of fantasy circles last August when he went from a seventh-round rookie who was seemingly buried on the depth chart to a potential starter, leading former starter Brian Robinson Jr. to be traded. He didn't see as much playing time as many wanted in September, but an injury to Austin Ekeler led to more snaps. He ran 14 times for 111 yards and two touchdowns in Week 5, leading him to average 12.4 carries per game over his next five contests. He averaged 3.0 yards per carry during that stretch, which led to him losing his starting job.

    Croskey-Merritt was the talk of fantasy circles last August when he went from a seventh-round rookie who was seemingly buried on the depth chart to a potential starter, leading former starter Brian Robinson Jr. to be traded. He didn't see as much playing time as many wanted in September, but an injury to Austin Ekeler led to more snaps. He ran 14 times for 111 yards and two touchdowns in Week 5, leading him to average 12.4 carries per game over his next five contests. He averaged 3.0 yards per carry during that stretch, which led to him losing his starting job.

    The Commanders moved on from Rodriguez and Ekeler, kept Croskey-Merritt and Jeremy McNichols, and added Rachaad White, Jerome Ford and Kaytron Allen. Croskey-Merritt caught nine passes in 17 games, while White, Ford and McNichols are better known for their receiving, which makes it highly unlikely he will be playing much on third downs.

    The best-case scenario is that Croskey-Merritt has a role similar to the middle and end parts of last season. He averaged 10.7 PPR points per game over the eight games in which he played at least 40% of offensive snaps, which could be good enough for a flex option. Ideally, he improves and the entire offense plays better, which could move him into RB2 territory.

    The best-case scenario is that Croskey-Merritt has a role similar to the middle and end parts of last season. He averaged 10.7 PPR points per game over the eight games in which he played at least 40% of offensive snaps, which could be good enough for a flex option. Ideally, he improves and the entire offense plays better, which could move him into RB2 territory.

    When deciding which running back to draft beyond the 10th round in fantasy drafts, it's worth knowing what kind of running back you want. Croskey-Merritt is a good option if you think you need a starter for a few weeks, provided the matchup is right. Another running back with a similar ADP is the man he replaced, Robinson. Croskey-Merritt is likely to have better standalone value this season, but Robinson is the better handcuff option. If another Commanders running back suffers an injury, that will have a relatively minor impact on Croskey-Merritt's fantasy value. If Bijan Robinson suffers an injury, Brian Robinson is a weekly starter for as long as Bijan Robinson is out.

    115. TE Jake Ferguson, Dallas Cowboys

    Ferguson has spent the last three seasons as the primary tight end for Dallas, making two Pro Bowls. He's ranked 10th, 24th and 10th in fantasy points per game over his three seasons as a starter.

    He was excellent from a fantasy perspective from Weeks 3 to 8 last season, the stretch from CeeDee Lamb‘s injury to the final week before Lamb's playing time was limited. Ferguson was the top tight end during that span, averaging 16.1 PPR points per game. By comparison, he averaged 8.3 fantasy points per contest over the rest of the season.

    Ferguson received a 60.0 PFF receiving grade last season, which ranked fourth-lowest among the 29 tight ends with at least 300 routes run. That would suggest his job is in danger, as it was for some of the other tight ends in the bottom five. However, Dallas didn't address the tight end position outside of undrafted free agents, so Ferguson's playing time and targets are very secure. The fact that he has the same offensive coaches, the identical quarterback and mirrored competition for targets also helps him.

    Ferguson is a very safe low-end starter or high-end backup. There is a good chance he finishes outside the top 12 fantasy tight ends if he doesn't reach the end zone enough, but his floor is also higher than most tight ends with a similar ADP.

    116. RB Kyle Monangai, Chicago Bears

    Monangai was a seventh-round pick by the Bears in 2025. Typically, not much is expected from seventh-round running backs, but Monangai quickly overtook Roschon Johnson and Travis Homer to become the second running back on the Bears’ depth chart. Chicago eased him into action in September, but after the Week 5 bye, he consistently played at least 20 offensive snaps and ran at least five times each week.

    Monangai received double-digit carries in seven games, including every game from Weeks 11 to 15. Like several backup running backs, his attempts partially depended on game script. He averaged 11.5 carries per game in victories and 7.0 in losses.

    Chicago didn't make any notable acquisitions to the running back room, so we will likely have another season with Monangai sharing time with D'Andre Swift. Swift graded notably better as a runner, receiver and pass-blocker last season, so it wouldn't be shocking to see a similar split this season.

    Monangai averaged 10.4 PPR points per game from Week 6 until the end of the season, ranking 30th-best among running backs. Ideally, he improves as a player, but that could be offset by the loss of Drew Dalman at center and more carries going to wide receivers.

    Monangai's value in fantasy drafts will vary widely by league type. Several running backs ranked near him are very high-risk, high-reward given the uncertainty around their roles. Monangai is relatively safe in his role and doesn't carry as much risk, but he also doesn't have as much upside.

    If only 20 to 24 running backs are starting in a fantasy league in any given week, Monangai won't have much value, because he won't typically be worth a starting spot unless Swift suffers an injury. It would generally be better to select a running back who has better odds of finishing as a top-20 back.

    If 28 to 36 running backs are starting in a given league, he has much more value, because he would be a weekly starter in that league.

    117. WR Courtland Sutton, Denver Broncos

    Sutton has spent most of the past seven seasons as the top receiver in the Broncos' offense. He missed nearly all of 2020 with an injury, and in 2022, Jerry Jeudy ended up with more yards. But that role did not lead to much fantasy success until Sean Payton took over as head coach and turned Sutton into a touchdown machine. Sutton’s 25 receiving touchdowns over the past three seasons rank sixth among all wide receivers, and he has finished 26th and 21st in fantasy points per game the past two years.

    Denver has spent several high draft picks on young receivers recently, taking Marvin Mims Jr. in 2023, Troy Franklin in 2024 and Pat Bryant in 2025, and none has cut into Sutton's playing time so far. The Broncos didn't draft a receiver this year, but they traded their first-round pick for Jaylen Waddle. Waddle owns a 90.0 PFF receiving grade over the past three seasons, 12th among all wide receivers, while Sutton's 80.7 ranks 32nd.

    At 6-foot-3 and 216 pounds, Sutton has fit nicely as an X receiver throughout his career, while Waddle is more of a Z and slot. The two could coexist fine on their own, but the complication is that Denver still has the three young receivers it believes in, and Payton has a long history of rotating his receivers.

    Franklin and Mims will likely rotate in the slot and take a few snaps from Waddle at Z. Bryant began his career as Sutton's backup at X, playing there throughout the preseason, and only saw more time once he moved to Z. It is entirely possible Bryant shifts back to X and cuts further into Sutton's snaps.

    Sutton signed an extension a year ago but will turn 31 early in the season, and the Broncos could move on from him after the year if Bryant plays well enough. To find out whether Bryant is ready, they have to give him the reps.

    The one reason Sutton remains a flex option is his scoring history, and even then, he projects as a frustrating, touchdown-dependent play. Normally, a starting receiver doesn't gain much when a backup goes down. But in Denver, an injury to any of the depth receivers could lift the playing time and target share of everyone else.

    118. WR Jakobi Meyers, Jacksonville Jaguars

    Meyers was a consistently underrated receiver in four years with the New England Patriots and two-and-a-half with the Las Vegas Raiders. His PFF receiving grades have stayed solid, between 71.0 and 80.0 in each of the past six seasons. Meyers’ role has steadily grown, producing WR29 finishes in both 2021 and 2022, a WR24 in 2023 and a WR19 in 2024, before he fell to WR32 in 2025.

    Meyers started 2025 atop the Raiders' depth chart with a strong opening stretch but was less involved from Weeks 4 to 6. He then sat out a few games, played against the Jaguars in Week 9 and was traded to Jacksonville a few days later. He was acquired in part because of the Travis Hunter injury but ended up taking over as the Z receiver.

    From Weeks 12 to 18, Meyers led Jacksonville's receivers in routes and receptions, but Parker Washington filled Hunter's slot role and paced the group in receiving yards. Meyers ranked 24th in fantasy points per game over that stretch, while Washington was 11th.

    The best case is that Meyers stays the Z receiver in both two- and three-receiver sets with a normal starter's workload. An offseason to learn the offense and build chemistry with Trevor Lawrence should sharpen his efficiency, putting a top-20 fantasy season within reach.

    There are several complications on that path, though. Hunter should be back and is expected to feature on both offense and defense. Washington has emerged as the team's best receiver, and if he and Hunter are on the field together, that could push Meyers off it. Reports throughout OTAs have been more positive about Washington and Brian Thomas Jr. than Meyers, so there is a chance those two see a higher target rate this season at Meyers' expense.

    Jacksonville also drafted two tight ends, which should mean more 12 and 13 personnel and fewer receivers on the field. Meyers was consistently featured in two-receiver sets but was the least likely wideout to be out there in single-receiver looks.

    119. QB Jared Goff, Detroit Lions

    Goff has spent the last five seasons as the Lions' starting quarterback and hasn't missed a game in the last four seasons. He's finished the last four seasons in the QB6-QB10 range in total points, but has finished outside the top 12 quarterbacks in fantasy points per game in three of those four seasons.

    His 85.0 passing grade over the last four seasons is 10th-best among quarterbacks. His passing production has been elevated by his coaching staff, skill players and offensive line. Goff has thrown for 18,205 yards over the last four years, the best mark among quarterbacks by over 1,000 yards. He's accomplished this despite Patrick Mahomes and Justin Herbert having more dropbacks.

    Goff was one of three quarterbacks last season with over 4,000 passing yards but fewer than 100 rushing. Most of Goff's official rushing attempts were either kneel downs or fumbles. His only designed runs for positive gains were three sneaks, and he scrambled five times. The lack of rushing production puts a major damper on his high-end fantasy value. The last quarterback to average over 22 fantasy points per game with fewer than 200 rushing yards was Tom Brady in 2021, and he posted the third-most passing attempts in a season in NFL history to reach enough fantasy volume. Brady only finished at QB3 that season.

    Quarterbacks like Matthew Stafford, Goff and Sam Darnold, who have great receivers in high-scoring offenses, aren't being drafted as starters in single-quarterback leagues, because it's more likely their passing volume decreases than increases, and there isn't much reason to expect notable increases in rushing production. Quarterbacks with more rushing upside are generally favored in other high-scoring offenses. However, Goff is one of several quarterbacks who are great to pick up late, pair with another solid fantasy option, and start whichever quarterback has the best matchup that week.

    120. TE Dallas Goedert, Philadelphia Eagles

    Goedert has a long history of reliability in fantasy football. He finished as TE10 in his second NFL season and has concluded top 12 in either fantasy points or fantasy points per game in every subsequent season.

    For most of his career, he accomplished this through volume, giving him a high floor and low ceiling. From 2019 to 2024, Goedert posted the sixth-most receiving yards among tight ends but only the 12th-most touchdowns. He also posted the seventh-best PFF receiving grade by a tight end during this span at 87.8.

    Goedert was 30 years old during the 2025 season, and by several measures, it was the worst of his career. His 69.7 receiving grade was a career low, and his 39.4 receiving yards per game was the worst mark since his rookie season. His 9.8 yards per reception and 4.1 yards after the catch per reception were career lows, as was his 0% contested catch rate.

    Most notably, Goedert stopped making as many big plays. He earned a +1 grade or better on at least 1.5% of his routes in each of his first four career seasons. That rate fell to the 1.1-1.3% range from 2022 to 2024, and to 0.5% last season.

    He was able to counteract this from a fantasy perspective with a career-high 11 receiving touchdowns, more than doubling his previous high of five. Eight of those scores came when the Eagles were within 6 yards of scoring, tied for the most by a tight end in a season over the last 20 years. Jimmy Graham matched that mark in 2017. No tight end has finished a season with seven such touchdowns, and only six have reached six.

    Moreover, five of Goedert's touchdowns came on shovel passes and wide screens, and no other tight end in the last 20 years has more than two touchdowns from that combination of routes in a season. All of this makes it highly likely that Goedert's touchdown total regresses. The Eagles drafted Eli Stowers in the second round to be their tight end of the future.
