While it's unlikely Stowers overtakes Goedert in 2026, he could cut into Goedert's playing time more than recent Eagles backups have.

    To remain a weekly fantasy starter, Goedert will need to bounce back in his quality of play and have his new offensive coordinator, Sean Mannion, scheme up touchdowns the way Kevin Patullo did. Some combination of continued regression with age, a decrease in snaps in favor of Stowers and a return to his usual touchdown totals would be enough to make Goedert only a bye-week fill-in.

    121. WR Jayden Higgins, Houston Texans

    Higgins was the 34th overall pick in the 2025 draft by the Texans, and he appeared in line for a large role. Nico Collins was locked into the top receiver spot, and Houston added Christian Kirk, Justin Watson and Braxton Berrios, drafted Higgins and Jaylin Noel, and still had Xavier Hutchinson.

    The initial concern was that Kirk would play in two-receiver sets ahead of Higgins, but it was actually Hutchinson who cut into his playing time. The two shared the Z receiver role, with Higgins playing more in two-receiver sets and Hutchinson more in three. When Collins missed Week 8, Higgins played 82% of snaps and caught four passes for 34 yards and a touchdown. He continued to play more than 55% of offensive snaps most weeks after that, and topped 80% again in the regular-season finale and the divisional round, though Collins was out for both of those as well.

    Higgins graded notably better than Hutchinson, 71.9 to 62.3 in receiving grade. His 1.46 yards per route run were the second-most on the team behind Collins, while Hutchinson sat at 1.05. The general expectation is for Higgins to see a bigger role this season. Kirk left in free agency, leaving slot snaps up for grabs. Most will likely go to Noel, but Hutchinson graded well in the slot, so he could play more there in 11 personnel, which would open the door for additional Higgins snaps.

    Higgins graded notably better than Hutchinson, 71.9 to 62.3 in receiving grade. His 1.46 yards per route run were the second-most on the team behind Collins, while Hutchinson sat at 1.05. The general expectation is for Higgins to see a bigger role this season. Kirk left in free agency, leaving slot snaps up for grabs. Most will likely go to Noel, but Hutchinson graded well in the slot, so he could play more there in 11 personnel, which would open the door for additional Higgins snaps.

    Higgins is being viewed as a potential breakout star for Houston, and there is a good chance he improves his efficiency. But whether that translates from helping the Texans to helping fantasy managers will depend on his role and playing time, which will be worth monitoring throughout training camp.

    122. TE Chig Okonkwo, Washington Commanders

    Okonkwo spent the last four seasons as the Tennessee Titans‘ lead receiving tight end. He posted a ridiculous 2.62 yards per route run as a rookie, the second-best mark for any tight end with at least 150 routes in the last four seasons. He's also been a top-20 tight end by PFF grade.

    Okonkwo has averaged 1.46 yards per route run over the last four years, which is tied for 10th-best among tight ends with at least 1,000 routes. Where he's really stood out is his play after the catch. His 5.8 yards after the catch per catch are tied for third-best among tight ends, behind George Kittle and Jonnu Smith.

    While he's been good enough on a per-route basis to be a high-end TE2, Okonkwo hasn't run enough routes, and he hasn't scored enough touchdowns. He's finished at TE21 each of the last three seasons. Okonkwo has scored only eight touchdowns in four seasons, in part because the Titans' offense hasn't reached the end zone as much as other teams. While Okonkwo sports the 19th-most routes for a tight end over the last four years, he’s run the 40th-most routes within the 5-yard line, and he’s garnered only two targets in those situations.

    The Washington Commanders signed Okonkwo to a three-year, $27 million contract in free agency, investing more in him than any skill player outside of Terry McLaurin. He replaces Zach Ertz, who ended last season on injured reserve, while John Bates will be the primary blocking tight end, and Ben Sinnott is expected to take more of an H-back role. Ertz was TE7 as recently as 2024 in Washington's offense.

    Expectations for Okonkwo will be higher than they were for Ertz at ages 34 and 35. Given the uncertainty at wide receiver outside of McLaurin, Okonkwo is the favorite to finish second on the team in targets. This gives him a solid floor, making him a low-end TE2.

    His ability to turn into a TE1 will largely depend on the quality of the Commanders' offense in general. It's unlikely Okonkwo becomes a frequent target near the goal line, given his lighter size for a tight end, but a few more touchdowns than usual are a possibility.

    Okonkwo is one of the ideal targets at tight end for someone who wants to focus entirely on running backs and wide receivers earlier in the draft and punt on the position.

    123. RB Rachaad White, Washington Commanders

    White spent the first four seasons of his career with the Tampa Bay Buccaneers. He was the full-time starter in his second season, finishing fourth in fantasy points per game. He accomplished this with the second-most rushing attempts and fourth-most receptions, but he wasn't particularly efficient as a runner. He slowly but surely lost playing time throughout the 2024 season to Bucky Irving. He was a starter at times in 2025 when Irving was injured, but remained a receiving back while Irving was healthy.

    White is one of the more accomplished receiving running backs over the last three seasons. His 1,463 yards after the catch rank fifth-most among running backs during that time, just ahead of Christian McCaffrey, Jaylen Warren and Alvin Kamara.

    White signed a one-year, $2 million contract with the Washington Commanders this offseason. They also added Jerome Ford for slightly less and drafted Kaytron Allen in the sixth round. They retained Jacory Croskey-Merritt and Jeremy McNichols, while not retaining Chris Rodriguez and Austin Ekeler.

    White signed a one-year, $2 million contract with the Washington Commanders this offseason. They also added Jerome Ford for slightly less and drafted Kaytron Allen in the sixth round. They retained Jacory Croskey-Merritt and Jeremy McNichols, while not retaining Chris Rodriguez and Austin Ekeler.

    The bad news is that the Commanders appear set to have a two- or three-man committee, with someone else playing on early downs. There is an outside chance White can outshine the other four running backs on early downs and become an every-down back, but if that was the plan from the start, he would have been given a better contract. White also participated in some kick return drills in OTAs, which also would not be normal for a feature back. Ford and McNichols are also known for their receiving, and if one of them has a better training camp, White might not even make the roster.

    The bad news is that the Commanders appear set to have a two- or three-man committee, with someone else playing on early downs. There is an outside chance White can outshine the other four running backs on early downs and become an every-down back, but if that was the plan from the start, he would have been given a better contract. White also participated in some kick return drills in OTAs, which also would not be normal for a feature back. Ford and McNichols are also known for their receiving, and if one of them has a better training camp, White might not even make the roster.

    124. RB Chris Rodriguez Jr., Jacksonville Jaguars

    Rodriguez was a sixth-round pick by Washington in 2023. He initially served as the third-string back behind Brian Robinson Jr. and Antonio Gibson, running just 51 times as a rookie but averaging 4.8 yards per carry. He fell to fourth on the depth chart in 2024 behind Robinson, Austin Ekeler and Jeremy McNichols, leaving him without a role for most of the season. His carry total dropped to 35, but he stayed effective, averaging 4.9 yards per attempt.

    He again looked like the odd man out in 2025 after Jacory Croskey-Merritt was drafted in the seventh round. But Robinson was traded, Ekeler was hurt early in the season and Croskey-Merritt's efficiency declined, opening the door for Rodriguez to take over the starting job in Week 10. He missed two games late in the year with an injury but had double-digit carries in each of his final five.

    Rodriguez has averaged 3.6 yards after contact per carry over the past three years, second-best among running backs with at least 150 carries. His 28.3% first-down rate also ranks second, and his 0.25 avoided tackles per attempt are tied for fifth. He has earned a +1.0 rushing grade or better on 6.6% of his carries, the third-highest rate behind Raheem Mostert and Jahmyr Gibbs. His rushing efficiency suggests he could be an excellent fantasy back with more volume, but the Commanders never used him as a receiver, as he caught just six passes in 35 career games.

    Rodriguez signed with the Jaguars in free agency, reuniting with his former college coach, Liam Coen. He will compete for playing time with Bhayshul Tuten and LeQuint Allen. Tuten has the best chance to lead the team in fantasy points as the one back most likely to see significant volume as both a rusher and a receiver, but Rodriguez has been the more efficient runner. He is also notably bigger than Tuten and Allen, which could earn him goal-line carries in what may be a high-scoring offense.

    There is a chance Tuten leads the Jaguars' backfield in fantasy points, and also a chance the offense plays well enough to support two fantasy-relevant backs. That makes Rodriguez an intriguing late-round option.

    125. RB Keaton Mitchell, Los Angeles Chargers

    Mitchell entered the league as an undrafted rookie with the Baltimore Ravens in 2023 and received his first carry in Week 9 of his rookie year. Over the following six weeks, he recorded 47 carries for 396 yards and two touchdowns, averaging 8.4 yards per carry and at least 6.0 in five of those six games. A significant ACL injury late in 2023 sidelined him for much of 2024 and clearly affected his play upon his return. By 2025, he had settled in as Derrick Henry‘s primary early-down backup, averaging 5.8 yards per carry on a limited 4.5-carry per-game workload.

    Mitchell signed with the Los Angeles Chargers this offseason, joining a backfield that also includes 2025 first-round pick Omarion Hampton and Kimani Vidal, who played well in Hampton‘s absence last season. Despite his down 2024 campaign, Mitchell earned the highest PFF grade of the trio over the past three seasons, including the top receiving grade.

    He is also the running back most closely tied to new offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel's preferred archetype. McDaniel brought Mitchell to Los Angeles himself, while Hampton and Vidal are holdovers. More importantly, Mitchell most closely resembles McDaniel's previous featured back, De'Von Achane. Achane, Mitchell, and Jaleel McLaughlin are the only running backs 5-foot-8 or shorter and 200 pounds or lighter to log at least 300 snaps over the past three seasons. That group joins Isiah Pacheco, Ty Chandler, Latavius Murray, and former McDaniel back Raheem Mostert as the only backs to run both a sub-4.40 40-yard dash and a sub-1.50 10-yard split at the combine while recording at least 300 snaps in a season.

    Given those similarities to Achane, there is a real chance McDaniel ultimately prefers Mitchell over his other options. If Mitchell approaches Achane's level of production, he offers top-10 upside at the position.

    126. WR Rashid Shaheed, Seattle Seahawks

    Shaheed went from undrafted rookie to All-Pro returner in his second season. His offensive playing time grew as his rookie year progressed, including three late games with 75 or more receiving yards. He played more than 50% of snaps and became more of a deep threat in his second season, with six games over 60 yards, five of which included a touchdown, and nine games of 36 yards or fewer, with nothing in between.

    In 2024, Shaheed leaned even further into the deep-threat role, with an 18.8 average depth of target and a 39% deep-target rate. He made eight explosive plays over six games, gaining 73 or more yards in four of them and 11 or fewer in two others, before a meniscus tear ended his season early.

    Shaheed was off to a much more consistent start in 2025, with four or more receptions in each of his first nine games. He was in the final year of his contract, and the Saints did not plan to re-sign him long-term, so they traded him to the Seattle Seahawks. Seattle had lost its third receiver, Tory Horton, for the season, and Seahawks offensive coordinator Klint Kubiak had been Shaheed's coordinator in 2024.

    His playing time was inconsistent in Seattle. He had two games over 60 receiving yards and ran the ball more than he caught it during the playoffs. His average depth of target fell to 11.5, down from his previous stint in Kubiak's offense.

    Seattle changed offensive coordinators again but still signed Shaheed to a long-term contract. There is significant speculation that he is in line for a larger role this season, presumably passing Cooper Kupp on the depth chart to become the second option in the passing game behind Jaxon Smith-Njigba.

    Shaheed is a fine, high-upside late-round option. Unlike most younger late-round targets, we should have a good read on whether he will pay off in fantasy after the first month or two of the season.

    127. TE Brenton Strange, Jacksonville Jaguars

    Strange was a second-round pick in 2023. He served as a rotational backup as a rookie but experienced a four-game stretch as a starter early in the season and another four-contest run late with Evan Engram injured. He averaged 8.3 PPR points per game across that span, which included three games with at least 60 yards, two with a touchdown and three with minimal production. He graded just as well as Engram, so Jacksonville let Engram leave in free agency and made Strange the full-time starter.

    Last season, Strange averaged 9.3 fantasy points per game over the first four weeks, putting him at TE14, before a quad strain early in Week 5 led to a six-game absence. He returned immediately with a five-reception, 93-yard game. He also scored three touchdowns and averaged 11.1 PPR points per game over that seven-game stretch, 12th among tight ends.

    The Jaguars spent a second-round pick on Nate Boerkircher, which could mean a slight dip in snaps, but the plan is for Boerkircher to do the dirty work as a blocker and free up Strange as a receiver.

    Jacksonville has plenty of talent at receiver in Parker Washington, Brian Thomas Jr., Jakobi Meyers and Travis Hunter. But three of those four were healthy at the end of last season, and Strange still played well enough to be a fantasy starter down the stretch.

    Strange is unlikely to become a top-five fantasy tight end given the Jaguars' receiver talent, but his ADP is that of a mid-range backup. He has the talent and quarterback to finish more like a borderline starter.

    128. RB Zach Charbonnet, Seattle Seahawks

    Charbonnet spent the first three seasons of his career as the Seahawks' receiving back, playing roughly 50% of snaps in each. He started six games in 2024 with Kenneth Walker III injured and was excellent for fantasy, running 14 or more times in five of them while averaging a touchdown and over three receptions per game.

    In 2025, Charbonnet was consistently the goal-line back, leading to 12 rushing touchdowns. Seattle's commitment to the run would usually be enough to produce a top-10 fantasy back, but the team split the work enough between Walker and Charbonnet that it was hard to trust either. Both averaged 11.3 PPR points per game, tied for 28th among running backs.

    Charbonnet tore his ACL in the divisional round of the playoffs, which makes it very likely he misses the start of the season. He did some work off to the side during OTAs, putting him ahead of schedule, though being ready to play does not guarantee he will be as effective as he was before the injury.

    Once he's back, he will no longer have Walker to compete with. Instead, first-round rookie Jadarian Price, backup George Holani and free-agent addition Emanuel Wilson are competing for the playing time.

    The ideal case for Charbonnet is that he returns early in the season, plays at his pre-injury level, and sees a larger role than he did alongside Walker. The more likely scenario is that he isn't as effective for much of the season and his role looks similar to last year's.

    129. RB Tyler Allgeier, Arizona Cardinals

    Allgeier was a fifth-round pick by the Falcons, beginning his career in 2022 far down their depth chart. He was inactive in Week 1 but ran 10 times for 84 yards in Week 4, then started seven games as the primary runner the rest of the season, finishing at 4.9 yards per carry.

    His fantasy value took a major hit in 2023 when the Falcons drafted Bijan Robinson. Robinson hasn't missed a game in his three-year career, so Allgeier never got another shot at the lead role. He has mostly been used in short-yardage situations, which lowered his career yards per carry to 4.3 but gave him a 51.6% success rate.

    Allgeier signed with the Arizona Cardinals hoping to be their lead back, but Arizona drafted Jeremiyah Love. He is in a worse spot now, since the Falcons had a top-10 offensive line while the Cardinals have had a bottom-10 one.

    Allgeier signed with the Arizona Cardinals hoping to be their lead back, but Arizona drafted Jeremiah Love. He is in a worse spot now, since the Falcons had a top-10 offensive line while the Cardinals have had a bottom-10 one.

    Allgeier is more valuable in larger or deeper leagues, where he could have some flex playability. In smaller leagues, you are probably better off drafting handcuffs in better situations if their starter gets hurt.

    130. TE Hunter Henry, New England Patriots

    Henry has spent the past five years as the Patriots' lead tight end after five seasons with the Los Angeles Chargers. Through his first three years with New England, he mixed a few high-ceiling games with several in which his role in the offense was minimal.

    He had three games with 75 or more yards in his first season, and 42 or fewer in the other 14. In his second, he had two games with 60 or more yards and a touchdown but was held under 22 yards in over half his games. 2023 was a similar mix, three games with 50 or more yards and a touchdown, but under 30 in half of them.

    Once Drake Maye became the quarterback, Henry grew more reliable, trading some of his low-end games for a new ceiling. In both 2024 and 2025, he had two games with seven or more receptions for 90 or more yards. He had reached 90 yards just three times in his first eight seasons.

    He finished 16th in fantasy points per game in 2024 and 13th in 2025. The concerns are that he is 31 and could begin to decline, and that the Patriots added A.J. Brown and Romeo Doubs, which could cut into his target rate. They also swapped blocking tight ends, losing Austin Hooper and adding Julian Hill, but Hill then suffered a season-ending injury, so it would not be surprising if New England adds another tight end at some point.

    Henry is a low-upside backup option, a fine fill-in if a starter gets hurt or if a riskier tight end drafted earlier doesn't pan out.

    131. RB Tyrone Tracy Jr., New York Giants

    Tracy has started 23 of a possible 34 games over the last two seasons for New York, which is impressive for a former fifth-round pick. He posted four weeks as a top-10 fantasy running back during his rookie season and added two weeks as a top-five back over the last month of last season.

    His fantasy value across the last two seasons has largely been tied to volume, with his big weeks often containing one or two touchdowns. His 74.0 rushing grade over the last two seasons is sixth-lowest among running backs with 250 or more carries. He has received a positive rushing grade on 37.3% of plays, the lowest rate among those running backs.

    Tracy had lost his starting job last season to Cam Skattebo before Skattebo's season-ending injury. Skattebo is expected to be ready for Week 1 and is the likely starter for New York this season. The Giants have a new coaching staff led by John Harbaugh as head coach and Matt Nagy as offensive coordinator.

    The most likely scenario is that Tracy spends the season as a handcuff to Skattebo without much fantasy value. However, there is a chance Skattebo isn't ready for the season, or that his injury hurts his effectiveness, leading Tracy to receive more playing time. Given the new coaching staff, there is also a chance Tracy simply wins over the new staff and sees the majority of touches. That's enough to make him a late-round draft pick in deeper leagues. Any negative reports about Skattebo's injury would quickly turn Tracy into a draftable fantasy asset, even if only for the first few weeks of the season.

    132. QB Baker Mayfield, Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    Mayfield’s 2025 did not go as expected. His touchdown rate returned to his career norm, and his 6.8 yards per attempt was the second-lowest of his career. Further, Mayfield’s accuracy rate fell from 61.3% to 55.9%, while his uncatchable pass rate climbed from 16.0% to 23.1%, and his accuracy on deep and intermediate passes dropped by 12%.

    Mayfield dealt with several injuries, including an oblique and a shoulder issue, which likely drove the accuracy dip. He also played through leg injuries but kept running at a strong rate, similar to 2024. That jump came from a higher rushing success rate that lifted his yards per game, along with a big spike in touchdowns.

    Mayfield’s career rate of passing attempts becoming touchdowns is 5.0%, but in 2024, it was 7.2%. He also became much more productive as a rusher, averaging 22.2 yards per game after 9.6 the previous year.

    That jump came from a higher success rate that lifted his yards per game, along with a large growth in touchdowns. His career rate of passing attempts becoming touchdowns is 5.0%, but in 2024 it was 7.2%. He also became much more productive as a rusher, averaging 22.2 yards per game after 9.6 the previous year.

    Expectations for Mayfield should land somewhere between his 2023 and 2024 seasons. His touchdown rate is unlikely to peak like 2024 again, but his rushing floor should stay higher, as it has the past two years. Tampa Bay lost Mike Evans but still has a strong receiver group in Emeka Egbuka, Chris Godwin and Jalen McMillan. The Buccaneers have a new offensive coordinator in Zac Robinson, though the offense should resemble the strong 2024 version under Liam Coen. Robinson and Coen worked together for several years in Los Angeles, including 2022, when Mayfield was there too.

    Mayfield isn't ranked among the top 12 quarterbacks given the uncertainty of a new coordinator, the loss of Evans and how he plays coming back from several injuries. But, he is a strong later-round option who can start when the matchup is right.

    133. RB Dylan Sampson, Cleveland Browns

    Sampson showed some sparks during his rookie season with the Browns. His 80.6 receiving grade was eighth-best among running backs with at least 100 routes run, and his 1.86 yards per route run was second-most among backs, just behind Bijan Robinson and just ahead of Christian McCaffrey and Jahmyr Gibbs.

    He wasn't very effective as a runner, but that was due to his offensive line. His 0.3 yards before contact per carry was tied for the second-lowest mark by a running back with at least 50 carries in a season over the last 20 years, and teammate Quinshon Judkins wasn't much luckier at 0.4 yards before contact per carry.

    Cleveland completely overhauled its offensive line this offseason, drafting Spencer Fano at left tackle, trading for right tackle Tytus Howard, and signing interior linemen Zion Johnson and Elgton Jenkins. The unit may still project below league average, but it should no longer rank among the NFL's worst.

    Sampson was part of a three-man rotation last season as the primary backup in all situations, with Judkins handling early downs and Jerome Ford handling third downs. Ford left in free agency and wasn't replaced by a free agent or draft pick; the only backfield additions were undrafted free agents unlikely to make the Week 1 roster. Sampson should see a substantial increase in playing time and efficiency, which could be enough to make him a flex option against the right opponents.

    Judkins ended last season with a dislocated ankle and fractured fibula and might not be 100% to start the season. This makes Sampson more likely to be a starter at some point this season than the typical handcuff would be.

    Tier 8

    134. QB Jordan Love, Green Bay Packers

    Love has spent the past three seasons as the Packers' starter, ranking seventh (19.4), 16th (16.3) and 21st (16.1) in fantasy points per game. The big reason for the decline has simply been volume. He averaged 34 attempts per game in 2023, and that fell to 28.3 in 2024 and 29.3 in 2025. A small part of that is the games he left with injuries the past two years, but he threw at least 30 times in 13 games in 2023, more than he managed across the two seasons since combined.

    Love has been among the highest-graded quarterbacks, his 86.6 passing grade ranking fifth over the past two seasons, but the Packers have had no problem leaning on the run. They rank 31st in dropbacks and fourth in rushing attempts. There is a chance Green Bay throws more this season. Tucker Kraft emerged as one of the league's top receiving tight ends last season before an injury, Christian Watson and Jayden Reed both showed enough promise to earn extensions this offseason, and Matthew Golden is expected to have a larger role in his second year.

    The one big concern is the declining offensive line. Green Bay ranked third in pass-block grade in 2024, with Elgton Jenkins, Zach Tom and Rasheed Walker all grading in the 80s, but the team fell to 21st last season. Jenkins and Walker both declined while remaining among its top three linemen in pass protection, and neither is on the roster now. Sean Rhyan and Aaron Banks remain starters despite never posting a PFF pass-blocking grade of 70.0 or better. Jordan Morgan is moving to left tackle full time, which adds uncertainty, and Anthony Belton struggled as a rookie and will need to improve. That uncertainty will make Love's life harder, but it could also mean less effective rushing, which would push the team to throw more.

    Love is a top-10 quarterback, and with a different offensive philosophy or better players around him, he would be a top-10 fantasy quarterback. Without that, he is a safe QB2, one who can start weekly in superflex leagues and in the right matchups elsewhere. If some of Green Bay's young skill players improve, Love could become a weekly starter in single-quarterback leagues.

    135. WR KC Concepcion, Cleveland Browns

    Concepcion started his career as a slot receiver for NC State but was used in a more well-rounded role at Texas A&M. According to our draft guide, he wins with quickness, which helps him create separation. He struggled with drops throughout college, but that hasn't historically been a predictive stat heading into the NFL. His player comp was Luther Burden III, another receiver who can line up all over the offense.

    He joins Cleveland, which entered the offseason with the biggest receiver need in the league. Last season, 10 teams posted a team PFF receiving grade below 70.0. Seven of them made at least one free-agency move to address it, and two others dealt with significant injuries at the position. The lone receiver room that looks largely unchanged is the Browns', and they carried the lowest team receiving grade of the group.

    Jerry Jeudy, Cedric Tillman, Isaiah Bond, Malachi Corley, Gage Larvadain and Jamari Thrash made up Cleveland's receiver corps last season, and they remain the top six on the depth chart for now. Corley was the only one with a PFF receiving grade above 60.0 (65.1), but he caught just 11 passes for 79 yards. Jeudy led the way as the only player to top 25 receptions, hauling in 50 for 602 yards.

    The Browns have a new head coach in Todd Monken, who was most recently the offensive coordinator of the Baltimore Ravens. The centerpiece of that passing attack was Zay Flowers, who posted 1,211 receiving yards last season, and Concepcion is the closest comparison to Flowers on Cleveland's roster.

    Concepcion has a chance to lead the Browns in targets as a rookie, which gives him possibly higher 2026 upside than some receivers drafted ahead of him. His long-term outlook is murkier, though, as Cleveland will likely keep investing at the position in the coming years.

    136. QB Malik Willis, Miami Dolphins

    Willis spent two seasons as a backup for the Tennessee Titans but received only three starts. Tennessee drafted Will Levis 33rd overall a year after taking Willis 86th, which made the latter expendable. The Packers traded for Willis to serve as a backup, and over two years, he made three starts and saw notable playing time in three other games.

    Willis has dropped back to pass just 118 times over the past two years, but his 79.9 PFF passing grade ranks 15th among all quarterbacks. Most important for fantasy, he scrambled on 15.1% of his dropbacks over that span, which leads all quarterbacks. That was notably ahead of Jayden Daniels, Tyrod Taylor, Justin Fields, Drake Maye and Jaxson Dart.

    Willis signed with the Miami Dolphins to be their starting quarterback this season. Dolphins head coach Jeff Hafley and general manager Jon-Eric Sullivan were both with the Packers before joining Miami. Some passing regression is fair to expect, since Willis won't have the same weapons in Miami that he had in Green Bay. Defenses will also be better prepared for him, since his appearances in Green Bay were often a surprise; because he plays so differently from Jordan Love, defenses weren't ready for him.

    Even if Willis’ scrambles per dropback regress somewhat, he should remain among the league leaders in quarterback rushing production, which is a big plus for his fantasy value. Extrapolating his rushing attempts per start and yards per carry in Green Bay, he would have posted 119 rushing attempts and 738 rushing yards over a season, both of which would have led all quarterbacks last year. It's fair not to expect numbers quite that big in Miami, but he could be a fantasy starter if he holds the job and scores enough touchdowns.

    Willis is a strong option for anyone willing to wait before adding a second quarterback in redraft leagues.

    137. RB Woody Marks, Houston Texans

    Marks was a fourth-round pick by the Texans. He quickly took over as the receiving back ahead of Dare Ogunbowale, then outran Nick Chubb enough to become the lead runner as well.

    Houston's run game wasn't particularly effective with Marks, though part of that was the offensive line. He was held to 2.7 yards per carry or fewer in four of his 11 games with double-digit carries. The Texans ranked 19th in team run-block grade, their best mark in a decade. Marks graded notably better as a receiver before shifting to the early-down role.

    Houston didn't re-sign Chubb but traded for David Montgomery, and it has been clear since the deal that Montgomery would lead Marks in a one-two punch. The rotation will likely lean toward Montgomery on early downs and Marks on passing downs, though both can handle either. The change should also help Marks' receiving efficiency.

    Marks is unlikely to help fantasy managers much this season outside of very deep leagues, where he could be a standalone flex if the snaps land near 50-50. He is also the clear handcuff in Houston, in which case his value could look much like the second half of last year, when he ranked 32nd in fantasy points from Weeks 10 to 18.

    138. WR Deebo Samuel Sr., San Francisco 49ers

    The 49ers selected Samuel in the second round of the 2019 NFL Draft, and his 2021 campaign was one of the most unique fantasy seasons in NFL history. Through the first nine weeks, he averaged 20.7 PPR points per game. His 882 receiving yards ranked second among wide receivers, and he averaged an absurd 18.0 yards per reception. Beginning in Week 10, San Francisco scaled back his role as a receiver and turned him into one of its primary ball carriers. Samuel averaged 6.2 yards per carry and nearly one rushing touchdown per game the rest of the way, producing 21.7 PPR points per game over that span, the fourth-highest mark among wide receivers.

    Samuel's next three seasons followed a familiar pattern: stretches of high-end production interrupted by injuries. In 2022, he was the WR11 through the first seven weeks before a hamstring injury slowed him, and he later suffered MCL and ankle sprains. In 2023, he was the WR18 through five weeks before a shoulder injury sidelined him. He missed two games, eased back into the lineup for one week and then finished as the WR3 from Week 10 through the end of the season. He was the WR11 through two weeks in 2024 before the injuries piled up again. Samuel missed Week 3, and over the first two months of the season he appeared on the injury report with calf, wrist, rib and oblique injuries, as well as an illness. Rib and wrist injuries in January also cost him the final week of the season.

    Samuel spent one season with the Washington Commanders after the 49ers were forced to make a difficult financial decision. He was the WR7 through the first five weeks, ranking seventh among wide receivers in receptions and second in rushing attempts. However, a heel injury landed him on the injury report throughout October, and by the time he returned to full health, Jayden Daniels was dealing with injuries of his own and missed much of the remainder of the season. Samuel ultimately finished 33rd among wide receivers in fantasy points per game at 11.8.

    He spent most of the offseason as a free agent before returning to San Francisco after Ricky Pearsall's 2025 PCL injury led to lingering knee swelling. Pearsall was soon ruled out for surgery and placed on injured reserve, opening the door for Samuel to reclaim his familiar role at Z receiver. The 49ers had already added Mike Evans at X and Christian Kirk in the slot, leaving Samuel's path to playing time largely unchanged. They also spent a second-round pick on De'Zhaun Stribling, but in the practices between Pearsall's injury and Samuel's signing, veteran Demarcus Robinson filled in at Z. That suggests Stribling is more likely to compete for snaps with Evans or Kirk than with Samuel.

    Samuel's level of play has declined over the past two seasons, though injuries have almost certainly contributed. The wear and tear may have caught up to him, and there is a chance he's no longer capable of producing as a consistent fantasy starter. Still, as long as he's healthy, San Francisco's offense gives him a chance to bounce back. The combination of Kyle Shanahan and Brock Purdy provides one of the league's most favorable environments, and Shanahan has already said he plans to use Samuel as a runner, raising his fantasy floor.

    He is worthy of a mid-to-late-round pick. As usual, he should be a weekly fantasy starter if healthy, and health has always been the concern. You can find more statistics and information on his past performance, his competition for touches and how much his teammates and coaches will help or hurt him below.

    139. WR Jordan Addison, Minnesota Vikings

    Addison has spent the past three seasons as the second option in the Vikings' offense opposite Justin Jefferson. He averaged 53.6 receiving yards per game in his first season and 58.3 in his second. The yardage was modest, but he delivered real fantasy value on the strength of his scoring, with 10 touchdowns as a rookie and nine in 2024. He finished 31st and 24th in fantasy points per game, respectively.

    Addison is a rare, small receiver who thrives as a deep threat. Of the 17 wide receivers with at least 60 deep targets over the past three seasons, he is the lightest at 175 pounds and one of only four under 6-foot. Over his first two years, he benefited from Kirk Cousins and then Sam Darnold at quarterback, who rank third and first, respectively, in deep-ball accuracy over that span among the 31 quarterbacks with at least 75 deep attempts.

    The Vikings struggled to find consistent quarterback play last season, and that matters more for a deep threat than for an underneath receiver. The instability limited Addison to just two touchdowns, and both of his games over 70 receiving yards came with Carson Wentz at quarterback. He finished 48th in fantasy points per game.

    Minnesota's quarterback this season will most likely be Kyler Murray. He should be an upgrade over last year, but his deep-ball accuracy ranks 21st of 31 quarterbacks, and his 7.3-yard average depth of target over the past three years is the fourth-lowest among quarterbacks.

    The Vikings also added Jauan Jennings later in free agency. Jennings has graded better than Addison in each of the past three years and could replace him in two-receiver sets, which would further dent Addison's value.

    Without Jennings, Addison would still be a clear flex consideration. With him in the mix, he is now just a late-round option.

    140. RB Isiah Pacheco, Detroit Lions

    Pacheco was the 251st overall pick of the 2022 NFL Draft and became the Kansas City Chiefs' starter by Week 7. He began seeing double-digit carries each week starting in Week 10. Pacheco ranked 18th in fantasy points for running backs over the second half of that season. He returned as the starter in 2023, where he averaged 15.3 PPR points per game, ranking 14th-best among backs.

    Expectations were high for 2024, but he fractured his fibula, and he hasn't been the same player since. He had PFF grades above 75 in his first two seasons and below 75 in his two most recent seasons. Pacheco signed a one-year, $1.8 million contract with the Detroit Lions this offseason to replace David Montgomery as Jahmyr Gibbs' backup. Montgomery's playing time fell from 47.8% in 2023 to 41.4% in 2024 and 37.1% in 2025.

    Expectations were high for 2024, but he fractured his fibula, and he hasn't been the same player since. He had PFF grades above 75 in his first two seasons and below 75 in his two most recent seasons. Pacheco signed a one-year, $1.8 million contract with the Detroit Lions this offseason to replace David Montgomery as Jahmyr Gibbs' backup. Montgomery's playing time fell from 47.8% in 2023 to 41.4% in 2024 and 37.1% in 2025.

    In 14-team leagues or leagues with multiple flex spots, Pacheco could keep some flex appeal, given the high-scoring Detroit Lions offense. Pacheco is notably larger than Gibbs, which should lead to at least some goal-line opportunities. His primary value should be as a handcuff, since Pacheco could be the feature back if Gibbs misses time.

    It's worth noting that it's no guarantee Pacheco wins the backup job. Sione Vaki is a former fourth-round pick who had seven career carries in two seasons, but has played over 500 special teams snaps, including the playoffs. Vaki and Pacheco are similar backs, and Vaki would take the backup spot if he simply outplays Pacheco in training camp and the preseason. This adds to the risk of drafting Pacheco.

    141. RB Tyjae Spears, Tennessee Titans

    Spears has spent the first three years of his career as a receiving back for the Titans, paired first with Derrick Henry and then with Tony Pollard. He was a major factor in the offense in 2023, averaging 4.5 yards per carry and catching 52 passes.

    Over the past two seasons, Spears has dealt with multiple injuries. Early in the season it was leg injuries, and later he suffered two concussions. In 2025, a high ankle sprain cost him the first four weeks.

    He consistently played between 41% and 47% of Tennessee's offensive snaps when healthy last season, but he averaged just 3.9 yards per carry and 5.9 per catch. The Titans kept Pollard and Spears and drafted Nicholas Singleton in the fifth round, though Pollard and Spears are still expected to be the top two backs for a third straight season.

    Tennessee has a new head coach in Robert Saleh and a new offensive coordinator in Brian Daboll, which could shake up the rotation. Daboll was willing to ride whichever back was more effective at the time during his stint with the New York Giants. Pollard has graded better than Spears as a rusher in both seasons, while Spears has been the better receiver in both.

    The most likely outcome is that Spears' role stays similar to his first three seasons, making him a deeper-league flex and a handcuff. The uncertainty around the new staff nudges him ahead of other backs in similar situations, at least until training camp clarifies the usage.

    142. RB Brian Robinson, Atlanta Falcons

    Robinson spent three seasons as the lead rusher for the Washington Commanders, working as an early-down back. At 225 pounds, he is one of the bigger runners in the league, with a high success rate and a 4.1 yards-per-carry average. Backs his size usually aren't much in the receiving game, but he caught 36 passes for 368 yards and four touchdowns in 2023. He finished 21st in fantasy points per game in 2023 and 29th in 2024.

    The Commanders traded Robinson to the San Francisco 49ers in the preseason, opting to rely on Jacory Croskey-Merritt and Chris Rodriguez as their early-down backs. Robinson became the primary backup, but Christian McCaffrey didn't miss a game, so Robinson never played more than 30% of snaps in a game. He averaged 5.4 carries per game, with as many as nine some weeks and no touches in others.

    He signed with Atlanta this offseason after the departure of Tyler Allgeier, becoming the backup to Bijan Robinson. The Falcons have a new head coach in Kevin Stefanski. His Browns offenses generally leaned on an early-down back as the main runner and a third-down back with less fantasy value. That won't be the setup here, since Bijan Robinson is locked in as the primary passing-down back. It is unclear, though, how the carries will be split between the two, particularly at the goal line.

    Brian Robinson is unlikely to carry much standalone value unless Atlanta's offense performs better than expected. He does have notable handcuff value, however. The Falcons' third back is Tyler Goodson, so Brian Robinson would likely get the bulk of the carries if Bijan Robinson were hurt, making him a probable fantasy starter in any matchup from excellent to slightly below average.

    143. WR Matthew Golden, Green Bay Packers

    The Packers drafted Golden into a crowded wide receiver room, though Green Bay has historically shown a willingness to draft players a year before they are needed. Injuries ultimately forced Golden into significant playing time as a rookie.

    He opened the season as the team's starting Z receiver while Christian Watson was sidelined. A later injury to Jayden Reed pushed Golden into the slot during the middle portion of the season. By the end of the year, with the receiving corps healthier, Golden had begun rotating with Romeo Doubs at X receiver.

    Green Bay moved on from Doubs and Dontayvion Wicks before the draft. Despite those departures, the Packers were one of only four teams not to draft a wide receiver, with Skyy Moore representing their lone notable addition at the position. That leaves Golden positioned to open 2026 as the starting X receiver, with Watson at Z and Reed in the slot.

    Golden may enter the season lower in the target pecking order, but nearly every notable Green Bay skill-position player carries durability concerns. Tight end Tucker Kraft missed the second half of last season and could also miss time entering 2026. Josh Jacobs spent much of that same stretch on the injury report, while Watson has missed multiple games in every NFL season of his career.

    Golden may not begin the season as a consistent fantasy starter, but additional injuries elsewhere in the offense could quickly elevate his role within Green Bay's passing attack.

    144. QB Tyler Shough, New Orleans Saints

    Shough was drafted 40th overall by the Saints in 2025 and took over as the team's starter partway through Week 8. From Weeks 9 to 18, he averaged 17.6 PPR points per game, 12th among quarterbacks. He had the right combination of volume as both a passer and runner, along with good efficiency for a rookie.

    He benefited from an easier-than-average schedule. He faced a top-five defense against fantasy quarterbacks three times and posted two top-10 finishes in those games. In three more against teams in the top half of the league at limiting quarterback fantasy points, he had his other top-10 finish. His remaining three starts came against bottom-half teams, and he failed to crack the top 10 in all three.

    The Saints leaned heavily on Shough last season. Alvin Kamara and Kendre Miller were both hurt for most of his time as a starter, and the team had traded away Rashid Shaheed and cut Brandin Cooks, leaving it thin at two important positions. New Orleans signed Travis Etienne and drafted Jordyn Tyson, which should help Shough's passing efficiency but could also push the Saints to run more.

    Shough's ADP is relatively low because there's usually a reason to prefer other quarterbacks, whether more experience at a high level, a better supporting cast or more rushing upside. The likeliest outcome is he regresses somewhat despite the upgraded weapons, but there's a chance Etienne and Tyson lift the offense to a new level and Shough improves on his 17.6 PPR points per game.

    He is a clear example of how deep the quarterback position is this year, and a reason it makes sense to wait at quarterback in single-quarterback leagues. Shough can be drafted in the later rounds and slotted into lineups when the matchup is right.

    145. TE Greg Dulcich, Miami Dolphins

    Dulcich impressed as a third-round rookie in 2022 with 33 receptions for 411 yards, including five games of at least 11 PPR points. Hamstring injuries derailed the start of his career, costing him the first five and final two games of his rookie season, and all but 32 offensive snaps of his second. He fell down the Denver Broncos’ depth chart below Adam Trautman, Nate Adkins and Lucas Krull, then spent two weeks as a healthy inactive before Denver released him. The New York Giants claimed him, but he didn't tally a reception during his time there.

    Dulcich was released shortly before the 2025 season, then claimed by the Dolphins. He made his debut in Week 8 and became a regular part of the Miami offense by Week 9. He never played more than 60% of offensive snaps, but was an effective receiver when given the opportunity. His 2.31 yards per route run ranked third-best among tight ends last season. Dulcich wasn't used much on first down, but his 2.84 yards per route run on second and third downs led the position.

    Miami re-signed Dulcich to a $3.25 million deal while moving on from Darren Waller, Julian Hill and Tanner Conner, replacing them with Ben Sims and rookies Will Kacmarek and Seydou Traore. Former head coach Mike McDaniel typically relied on a heavy tight end rotation, while new offensive coordinator Bobby Slowik featured Dalton Schultz in 2023 and 2024.

    New general manager Jon-Eric Sullivan pointed to Dulcich as a player poised to make a leap this season. Miami let go of wide receivers Tyreek Hill and Jaylen Waddle, filling those spots with third-round rookies and free agents making less than $1.5 million. Dulcich is the favorite to lead Miami in targets.

    Dulcich should have a high floor given his situation. The question is how many touchdowns he can score, and that will largely depend on the quality of the Dolphins‘ offense.

    146. TE Kenyon Sadiq, New York Jets

    Sadiq was the 16th overall pick in the 2026 NFL draft. There was no equivalent to Brock Bowers, Tyler Warren or Colston Loveland in this year's tight end class, but Sadiq was the only tight end taken among the first 50 picks.

    He joins a group of seven tight ends who have been expected to go in the first round over the past six drafts. He profiles more like Bowers, Loveland, Kyle Pitts and Dalton Kincaid, lighter tight ends known mostly for their receiving, than like Michael Mayer and Warren, who are more well-rounded.

    Sadiq is on the shorter side, like Kincaid, but has elite speed like the other receiving tight ends. He matches that group athletically, but not in production. His career 73.7 PFF receiving grade trails the 88.0 or better each of the other four posted. Playing behind Terrance Ferguson in 2023 and 2024 did not help, and while he led Oregon in receiving last season, the top wide receivers and even his backup, Jamari Johnson, had higher target rates.

    The Jets are an interesting landing spot. They drafted Mason Taylor in the second round last year and first-round wide receiver Omar Cooper Jr. this year, so Sadiq is far from the only young option who deserves opportunities. He has a realistic chance to finish second on the team in targets, but the offense will need to improve significantly for him to score many touchdowns.

    The Jets are an interesting landing spot. They drafted Mason Taylor in the second round last year and first-round wide receiver Omar Cooper Jr. this year, so Sadiq is far from the only young option who deserves opportunities. He has a realistic chance to finish second on the team in targets, but the offense will need to improve significantly for him to score many touchdowns.

    147. WR Denzel Boston, Cleveland Browns

    Boston improved each year at Washington. He has the profile of an X receiver but has also found success in limited snaps at other spots, a combination that is ideal for fantasy production. Our draft guide listed his hands and his competitive toughness as his best traits.

    He was selected 39th overall by the Browns, not long after KC Concepcion, and he pairs well with his fellow rookie, since Concepcion projects more as a Z or slot option. Jerry Jeudy was Cleveland's X receiver last season but is also more experienced at Z or in the slot. The most likely outcome is that those three lead the position in playing time and receiving yards. The one concern is Isaiah Bond working his way into the lineup and leaving Boston as the fourth option.

    Todd Monken is the new head coach in Cleveland. X receivers did not produce much fantasy value under Monken in Baltimore, but he was the offensive coordinator in Tampa Bay during Mike Evans' only seasons with more than 1,300 receiving yards. The willingness of Shedeur Sanders and Deshaun Watson to push the ball downfield could also help Boston deliver immediate value.

    Boston is a strong late-round option who probably shouldn't be in fantasy starting lineups early in the season, but who has the upside to become a favorite target in Cleveland on a team that may need to throw often.

    148. TE Juwan Johnson, New Orleans Saints

    Johnson has played six seasons with the Saints, steadily growing into a fantasy-viable tight end. He was held under 20 receptions in each of his first two seasons, caught between 35 and 50 in each of the next three, and set a career high with 77 last season.

    Part of that jump was more playing time. He played roughly 65% of offensive snaps from 2022 to 2024, then 75.6% last season. Some of that was because Foster Moreau and Taysom Hill weren't healthy until a few weeks into the year, so Johnson played over 90% of snaps in each of the first three weeks and at least 80% in two more, before averaging 68% the rest of the way.

    His target rate also climbed to a career-high 19.0%, largely because the team moved on from Rashid Shaheed and Brandin Cooks midseason. Johnson's PFF receiving grade has stayed remarkably steady over the past five seasons, ranging from 66.0 to 72.0 each year.

    Johnson finished 12th in fantasy points per game last season at 10.6, but some decline is fair to expect. New Orleans added Noah Fant in free agency and Oscar Delp in the third round, so while Johnson should remain the starter, his snaps might dip slightly. The Saints also drafted Jordyn Tyson in the first round, which pushes Johnson down the target pecking order.

    Johnson is a fine, low-upside backup option who can be drafted late.

    149. WR Jauan Jennings, Minnesota Vikings

    Jennings spent the first three seasons of his career as the 49ers' slot receiver. He graded relatively well but rarely played in two-receiver sets, and San Francisco consistently ranks among the teams with the lowest rate of 11 personnel. That kept Jennings off fantasy radars throughout those three seasons and into the lead-up to 2024.

    When Deebo Samuel missed Week 3 of the 2024 season, Jennings stepped in as the Z receiver and caught 11 passes for 175 yards and three touchdowns. He added 88 yards back in his usual role the following week, then had two disappointing games from a fantasy perspective before missing two weeks with an injury. He later took over as the 49ers' X receiver with Brandon Aiyuk on injured reserve, averaging a respectable 14.1 PPR points per game over the final nine weeks, 29th among wide receivers.

    Aiyuk didn't play in 2025, leaving Jennings as the X receiver, and he posted five receptions for 89 yards and a touchdown in Week 2. He landed on the injury report in Week 1 with a calf injury and appeared throughout the season with shoulder, ankle and rib issues, sometimes all in the same week. At one point early in the season, he said he was playing with high and low ankle sprains, a shoulder at 20% and five broken ribs.

    Jennings kept playing through it, missing only two weeks all season. He also kept scoring, with eight touchdowns over an eight-week span, though he was held under 75 yards in every game the rest of the way.

    Jennings didn't reach a new deal in San Francisco this offseason, presumably because he couldn't get the terms he wanted, and instead signed a one-year prove-it deal with the Minnesota Vikings. His initial role will likely be the third receiver in three-receiver sets, which makes fantasy value hard to come by. But he has graded better than Jordan Addison in each of the past three seasons and could take Addison's place in two-receiver sets. New quarterback Kyler Murray has the fourth-lowest average depth of target among quarterbacks over the past three seasons, and Jennings has a notably lower average depth than Addison, which works in his favor.

    Jennings is an intriguing late-round target who could pay off in a major way if the Vikings' offense clicks and he builds a strong connection with Murray.

    150. RB Tank Bigsby, Philadelphia Eagles

    Bigsby spent the first two seasons of his career as a backup for the Jacksonville Jaguars, where he took significant early-down snaps from Travis Etienne. In 2024, he averaged double-digit rushing attempts per game. He was replaced by Bhayshul Tuten on the roster in 2025 and was traded to the Philadelphia Eagles after one week.

    Bigsby was initially used as a kick returner, but that experiment lasted only four weeks. He ran nine times for 104 yards against the New York Giants in Week 8, which eventually helped him take over as the primary backup behind Saquon Barkley. He also received significant time against the Las Vegas Raiders in Week 15 and the Washington Commanders in Week 18, reaching 17 and 16 carries in those games, two of his top-five outings.

    Bigsby received an 89.1 PFF rushing grade in Philadelphia last season compared to 76.7 by Barkley, while averaging 4.0 yards after contact per attempt compared to 2.7 by Barkley. It would not be surprising if Bigsby's rushing attempts per game see a notable increase this season. However, that will only help fantasy managers in very deep leagues.

    It's fair to consider Bigsby the primary handcuff to Barkley, but it's worth noting he has totaled 11 career receptions in 50 games. If Barkley suffers an injury, Will Shipley would also see notable playing time. Bigsby would still be the player to pick up off waivers, but it won't be a one-for-one replacement. His lack of receiving production makes him a worse handcuff option than other backup running backs, despite how good his surrounding cast is.

    151. TE T.J. Hockenson, Minnesota Vikings

    Hockenson spent three and a half seasons with the Detroit Lions and the past three and a half with the Vikings. He ranked among the top six tight ends in fantasy points per game from 2020 to 2023, including a second-place finish in 2023.

    He tore his ACL and MCL at the end of the 2023 season, and his production hasn't been the same since. He missed the start of 2024, and his receptions dropped to 4.1 per game from 6.3 the year before, with no touchdowns.

    Last season was another step in the wrong direction. His receptions per game fell to 3.4, and his 29.2 receiving yards per game was a career low. His PFF receiving grade had held at 77.4 in 2024, in line with past seasons, but it dropped to 62.6 last season.

    Part of that could have been the quarterback play. His average depth of target fell to 5.3 after sitting at 7.7 or better every other season, and his targets per route also dropped significantly.

    This season, Hockenson will catch passes from Kyler Murray, who leaned on Zach Ertz and Trey McBride in his later years with the Arizona Cardinals. There is a chance that two years removed from the injury, Hockenson is fully recovered and his target rate and average depth return to normal. But there is also a chance he is never the same player, or that he has too much target competition now that Minnesota upgraded its third receiver spot with Jauan Jennings.

    152. WR Khalil Shakir, Buffalo Bills

    Shakir has spent the past three seasons as the Bills' top slot receiver. He has caught more than 70 passes in each of the last two, producing WR37 and WR36 finishes, and he is one of five wide receivers with at least 100 slot receptions over that span.

    Ideally, the top receiving option playing with one of the league's best quarterbacks would be a more consistent fantasy producer, but the Bills have not expanded Shakir's role beyond the slot. He averaged snap rates of 61.0% in 2024 and 60.1% in 2025.

    There is real reason for concern this season. Buffalo traded for DJ Moore, who is expected to be its new top receiver. Joshua Palmer could improve in his second season with the team, and tight end Dalton Kincaid could be healthier, both of which would mean more targets for them and fewer for Shakir.

    Shakir might also see fewer snaps. Teams are trending toward more 12 and 13 personnel, and the Bills have the tight ends to follow suit in Kincaid, Dawson Knox and Jackson Hawes, with Shakir coming off the field whenever they do. Buffalo also spent a fourth-round pick on Skyler Bell, who also projects as a slot receiver.

    Shakir is still worth drafting, since he remains one of the most trusted targets of one of the league's best quarterbacks, but his upside is mostly as a flex option or bye-week fill-in unless his role starts to expand beyond 11 personnel.

    153. RB Ray Davis, Buffalo Bills

    Davis has spent the first two years of his career in a three-man backfield with James Cook and Ty Johnson, and that will be the case again this season. Cook has served as the starter, Davis the backup and Johnson the third-down back.

    As a rookie, Davis averaged 5.2 carries per game when Cook was healthy, staying involved without carrying a real workload. Cook missed Week 6, and Davis responded with 20 carries for 97 yards and three catches for 55 yards. Davis also saw significant playing time in Week 18, when Buffalo rested some starters, running 15 times for 64 yards.

    In 2025, the Bills leaned even more heavily on Cook and Johnson. Davis was held without a carry in seven games and limited to three or fewer in another six. Of his 58 carries on the season, 21 came in Week 18, when he ran for 151 yards.

    Davis is expected to see more work this season, likely closer to his rookie usage. That still would not be enough to make him worth starting in the vast majority of leagues. His value this year lies in being Cook's handcuff. If Cook were to get hurt, Davis would likely step into the lead role, though Johnson could cut into that work, as could Frank Gore Jr., who has spent a few seasons on Buffalo's practice squad and would presumably become the third back.

    Even so, in that scenario, Davis would be the lead back in one of the league's best offenses, which would make him a must-start fantasy option. That makes him one of the more intriguing pure handcuffs available.

    154. TE Dalton Schultz, Houston Texans

    Schultz has spent the past three seasons as the Texans' lead tight end after starting his career with the Dallas Cowboys. He set a career high in receptions in his eighth season with 82, gaining 777 yards and scoring three touchdowns, and finished 13th in fantasy points per game. His 76.7 receiving grade was the second-best of his career and his best in Houston.

    He benefited from a Texans team in transition. Tank Dell missed the entire season and the team moved on from Stefon Diggs, leaning more on rookie receivers Jayden Higgins and Jaylin Noel. He also benefited from injuries at his own position, as Brevin Jordan and Irv Smith Jr. started the season on injured reserve and Cade Stover missed the first half.

    Stover's role grew by Week 15, when he played at least 48% of snaps in the final four regular-season games and both playoff appearances. That came mostly from the Texans using more 12 personnel rather than Schultz seeing a reduced role.

    Houston invested in the position this offseason, signing Foster Moreau and drafting Marlin Klein in the second round. That again points to more 12 personnel, though it could also mean less playing time for Schultz. Growing trust in receivers like Higgins could also cost him targets. His high receiving grade, though, suggests his per-target production won't fall off dramatically.

    Schultz is a safe, low-upside TE2, perfect for deeper leagues or when your starter has a bye.

    155. QB Sam Darnold, Seattle Seahawks

    Darnold is a Super Bowl-winning quarterback with an 83.8 passing grade over the past two seasons, eighth among quarterbacks. Despite the offense playing well, he ranked 27th in fantasy points per game last season at 14.7. The problem was volume, as the Seahawks ranked second in designed rushing plays and second-to-last in dropbacks.

    He topped 34 attempts in four games, including two of their three losses, an overtime win and a two-point win, but was held to 27 or fewer in 10 of 17 games. His attempts fell as low as 12 in a 44-22 win over the Arizona Cardinals in which he played more than 90% of the offensive snaps.

    There is reason to believe Darnold could throw more this season. Seattle did not re-sign Kenneth Walker III, drafting Jadarian Price instead, and Zach Charbonnet is expected to miss the start of the season. The Seahawks also re-signed Rashid Shaheed, who will now have a full offseason working with Darnold. If the run game struggles, the passing game should be able to pick up the slack.

    Seattle also had the league's best scoring defense last season, which is likely to regress at least somewhat, since defensive production tends to regress more than offensive. The team lost safety Coby Bryant, cornerback Tariq Woolen and edge rusher Boye Mafe, replacing them with rookies and free agents mostly expected to be backups. A step back on defense would also push the offense to do more, which again means more passing.

    Darnold is a fine late-round option with real reason to expect better fantasy production, though the question is how much. He ran for only 95 yards last season, so there is a clear ceiling on his rushing. Even if he doesn't finish among the top 12 fantasy quarterbacks, he can be started in weeks that project as close games rather than clear Seattle wins.

    156. WR Romeo Doubs, New England Patriots

    Doubs joins the Patriots after four years as a starting wide receiver for the Green Bay Packers. He has always sat on the border between a receiver worth rostering in a typical redraft league and one who is a top available free agent, a consistent option with touchdown upside in a good offense. He finished 45th in fantasy points per game last season, at 10.3.

    Doubs lands in New England, where his role is expected to change. He performed well out of the slot early in his Green Bay career before the Packers drafted Jayden Reed, then moved to X receiver after Davante Adams left. The Patriots, though, already had Kayshon Boutte and Kyle Williams at X and then added A.J. Brown, another X receiver.

    That means Doubs will likely fill a role similar to the one Stefon Diggs held last season: some Z in two-receiver sets and either Z or slot in three-receiver sets. The change adds some uncertainty, which could settle him in as a steady flex option or push him off fantasy rosters entirely.

    New England signed Doubs to a four-year, $68 million contract with $39 million guaranteed, enough to give him a fair shot at a big role in the offense.

    That makes Doubs a fine late-round option who can work as a flex play in the right matchup. He is a particularly good fit for anyone taking several risks at receiver who might need a safer option in the starting lineup early in the season.

    157. TE AJ Barner, Seattle Seahawks

    Barner was a fourth-round pick by the Seahawks in 2024. He began his career in a rotational role alongside Noah Fant and Pharaoh Brown. Seattle moved on from both in 2025 but added second-round rookie Elijah Arroyo and blocking veteran Eric Saubert. Barner still took a clear hold of the starting job, playing 77.7% of snaps in the regular season and 89.1% in the playoffs.

    He posted the 12th-best receiving grade among tight ends last season at 72.7, including the playoffs, suggesting he has the talent to be a borderline fantasy starter. The Seahawks have a new offensive coordinator in Brian Fleury, who most recently coached tight ends for the San Francisco 49ers and worked with George Kittle. At only 24, Barner has room to grow, and there is a chance he becomes a bigger focal point of the offense and a regular fantasy starter.

    Barner was also one of five tight ends with seven or more rushing attempts from direct snaps when the team needed a yard for a first down, and he was the most successful of the group, converting 10 of 11 into first downs. He has a decent chance to keep that role given those results. Some teams embraced using tight ends to pick up short yardage, but few used one at the goal line. With goal-line back Zach Charbonnet out to start the season, Barner could see a few carries down there. It would take only a few touchdowns to meaningfully boost his fantasy appeal.

    There is also a serious reason for concern. The Seahawks were one of the most run-heavy teams last season, and given the quality of their defense and roster, that could hold again. Jaxon Smith-Njigba should continue to dominate the target share even if Barner plays better. There has been talk of Rashid Shaheed becoming more involved and of Seattle throwing to running backs more often, and Arroyo was drafted to be a receiving tight end, with similar talk of a bigger role for him. All of that makes it difficult for Barner to substantially improve a target rate that was already low. He was targeted on 16.4% of his routes last season, eighth-worst among tight ends with at least 300 routes.

    Barner is a high-risk, high-upside late-round backup. The tight end you draft as your backup should balance out your starter. Kyle Pitts, for instance, is a relatively safe starter, so pairing him with a riskier backup makes sense. If you take a riskier starter like George Kittle, given his injury history, a safer backup like Hunter Henry is the better fit.

    158. RB Alvin Kamara, New Orleans Saints

    Kamara was once considered one of the top running backs in the NFL, despite never posting a 1,000-yard rushing season. He opened his career with five straight Pro Bowls and was a top-10 fantasy running back in each, peaking as the No. 1 back in 2020. He and Austin Ekeler were the only backs with a PFF grade of 85.0 or better in both rushing and receiving over that span, and Kamara played more than 1,000 additional snaps in the process.

    His grades began declining in 2021. His PFF receiving grade has fallen to 66.0 or lower in four of the past five seasons, and his rushing grades have not approached the heights of his rookie or 2020 campaigns. He stayed a top-20 fantasy back from 2022 to 2024 largely on volume, returning to the top 10 in 2024 behind 21.1 touches per game, fifth among running backs.

    In 2025, Kamara set career lows in both rushing and receiving grade. The receiving drop is at least partly tied to new head coach Kellen Moore. Moore has coached Ezekiel Elliott, Ekeler and Saquon Barkley in their primes, and all three produced less for fantasy under him than they did before and after. Kamara's receiving yards per game were cut in half, to 16.9. He also began losing snaps to Kendre Miller before Miller suffered a season-ending injury.

    The Saints signed Travis Etienne to be their new lead back, which led to several months of speculation around Kamara's future. In mid-July, Kamara renegotiated his contract, assuring him of staying with the Saints. The likely split will be Etienne on early downs and Kamara on passing downs.

    Kamara will primarily only have value in deep leagues where there is a chance his receiving production could give him flex value. Outside of that, his value is only as a handcuff.

    159. WR Adonai Mitchell, New York Jets

    Mitchell was drafted 52nd overall in the 2024 draft by the Indianapolis Colts, with the thought that he would push Alec Pierce for the starting X job. Pierce had been held under 600 yards and to two touchdowns in each of his first two seasons.

    Mitchell ended up as the primary backup to all three Colts receivers as a rookie, starting the season in the slot and filling in for both Pierce and Michael Pittman Jr. during the middle of the year. In one of his two games above a 65% snap rate, he caught six passes for 71 yards, and he was held under 40 yards in every other game. He also posted notably high separation rates.

    Mitchell ended up as the primary backup to all three Colts receivers as a rookie, starting the season in the slot and filling in for both Pierce and Michael Pittman Jr. during the middle of the year. In one of his two games above a 65% snap rate, he caught six passes for 71 yards, and he was held under 40 yards in every other game. He also posted notably high separation rates.

    The New York Jets acquired him, and he quickly became the top passing-game option while Garrett Wilson was on injured reserve. From Weeks 12 to 17, he averaged 10.6 points per game, which ranked 31st among wide receivers over that stretch. That window intentionally sets aside Week 11, when he was eased into action, and Week 18, when he played only 25 snaps.

    New York plans to keep Mitchell as its X receiver, alongside a healthy Wilson and first-round rookie Omar Cooper Jr. His new offensive coordinator is Frank Reich, and his new quarterback is Geno Smith. Mitchell is worth a late-round pick as a talented former early-round selection who will finally get a chance to start.

    160. WR Jalen Coker, Carolina Panthers

    Coker was an undrafted rookie out of Holy Cross. An injury to slot receiver Adam Thielen opened the door for his debut in Week 4 of 2024, and when Xavier Legette got hurt in Week 5, Coker played most of the second half, catching four passes for 68 yards.

    Legette returned the following week, but Thielen was not back until Week 12, and with Diontae Johnson traded after Week 8 and Jonathan Mingo after Week 9, Coker was at least a top-three receiver whenever healthy over the rest of the season. Once Thielen returned, Coker shifted to the Z role while Legette moved to X. His best game was four receptions for 110 yards and a touchdown.

    The Panthers drafted Tetairoa McMillan and installed him at X, sliding Legette to Z and Thielen to the slot, with Coker competing for both the Z and slot spots. On Aug. 27, Carolina traded Thielen to the Vikings, and three days later it was reported that Coker would miss time with a quad injury. He returned in Week 7 but spent most of the season playing only in the slot in three-receiver sets.

    Coker graded noticeably better than Legette for a second straight season, and in Week 16 he finally began outsnapping him. He played more than 85% of snaps in the regular-season finale and the wild-card game, catching six passes for 47 yards and a touchdown in the first and nine for 134 yards and a touchdown in the second.

    The Panthers rewarded Coker with a three-year, $34 million extension, a move they did not have to make, and he is all but assured a full-time role this season. He is worth a late-round pick in case Carolina's offense plays well enough to support two fantasy-relevant receivers.

    161. RB Mike Washington Jr., Las Vegas Raiders

    Washington is a big 6-foot-2, 228-pound back out of Arkansas. The only halfbacks that size on NFL rosters are Derrick Henry and Adam Randall of the Baltimore Ravens. He has strong straight-line speed as a power runner, and his PFF rushing grades improved every season in college.

    He was drafted in the fourth round by the Raiders, where there is a clear path to being the backup to Ashton Jeanty. Raheem Mostert and Zamir White were the primary backups last season, but neither re-signed. Dylan Laube, a 2024 sixth-round pick, is the only other notable back on the roster, and he is more of a third-down option.

    The Raiders added head coach Klint Kubiak, who is on his sixth team in six seasons. He has worked with feature backs like Dalvin Cook, Christian McCaffrey and Alvin Kamara, but also ran a two-man committee last season in Seattle and another in 2022 in Denver. Jeanty is a special player and will see the clear majority of touches, but Washington could still be involved.

    Washington could have standalone value in deeper leagues if the Las Vegas offense improves significantly, and he takes over as the goal-line back. Last season, Zach Charbonnet consistently took goal-line work from Kenneth Walker III, boosting the bigger back's fantasy value, and the Raiders could make a similar call here.

    Washington also carries handcuff appeal. Given the backs currently on the roster, Washington would be a must-start option if Jeanty were to get hurt. That said, as a fourth-round pick with a specific skill set, he could easily be leapfrogged if Las Vegas brings in someone else after an injury. Kamara in particular has been the subject of trade rumors, and his best season of the past few years came in Kubiak's offense.

    Washington is a solid pick in deep leagues for his touchdown upside and his handcuff value.

    162. QB C.J. Stroud, Houston Texans

    Stroud has spent three seasons as the Houston Texans' starting quarterback. His PFF passing grade started out excellent as a rookie, at 79.6, but has regressed every year since, down to 70.0 last season. His fantasy finishes have followed the same path, 10th as a rookie and outside the top 20 in each of the last two seasons.

    A quarterback who grades like Stroud usually posts better fantasy results, but Houston's offensive line has held him back. He has the eighth-most dropbacks under pressure over the past three seasons. When kept clean over that span, he averaged 8.1 yards per attempt, tied with Drake Maye and Matthew Stafford for fifth among quarterbacks, but his 5.9 mark under pressure sits right at league average.

    The Texans largely reworked the line, signing three free agents and drafting Keylan Rutledge with their first-round pick. Rookie linemen tend to struggle, though, and the two free agents slated to start are on the wrong side of 30 and have already seen their PFF grades slip from their peak. There is a chance the line improves, but perhaps not by much.

    The good news is Houston's young receiver core. Nico Collins has been among the league's best for a few years, Jayden Higgins and Jaylin Noel both flashed as rookies, and Tank Dell should return this season. The other factor, good for the Texans but not for Stroud, is that Houston has one of the league's best defenses, which means it won't allow many points. The addition of David Montgomery should mean more rushing, and the team could lean conservative if that approach is working.

    That makes Stroud someone to mostly avoid in single-quarterback redraft leagues, though he remains an intriguing dynasty option. The receivers should stay with him for a few years, the offensive line should trend up rather than down, and the defense is likely to regress at some point. If Stroud improves as a passer on top of that, it is a perfect storm for him to become a much better fantasy option over the next few seasons.

    163. WR Jalen McMillan, Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    McMillan was a third-round pick by Tampa Bay who won the third receiver job behind Mike Evans and Chris Godwin in training camp, on a team that played a lot of 11 personnel. Hamstring injuries cost him four games early in the season, but he had a classic rookie late-season breakout, scoring seven touchdowns over the final five games with at least 50 yards in each. He was the WR8 across that stretch.

    The Buccaneers' receiver room looked much more crowded heading into 2025, as the team spent its first-round pick on Emeka Egbuka, giving it four receivers worthy of starting. Injuries kept the group from ever playing together at full strength.

    McMillan was hit hardest, suffering a scary neck injury in the preseason. He played in only the final four games, at roughly half of the offensive snaps. He did not score, but he caught seven passes for 114 yards in Week 17.

    Evans left for the San Francisco 49ers in free agency, though Tampa Bay spent a third-round pick on Ted Hurst. It is unclear how the rotation will shake out, but as long as Baker Mayfield plays better this season, Tampa Bay should be able to support two fantasy-relevant receivers. The most likely pair is Egbuka and Godwin, though it is possible McMillan passes Godwin on the depth chart, or that one of the top two gets hurt.

    McMillan is a fine pick at the end of drafts, in case training camp news points to a larger role for the talented young receiver.

    Tier 9

    164. WR Jalen Nailor, Las Vegas Raiders

    Nailor was a sixth-round pick in 2022 and played more than 50% of the Vikings' offensive snaps as their No. 3 receiver in each of the past two seasons, holding off Brandon Powell and Trent Sherfield in 2024 and Adam Thielen in 2025.
