Jordan Addison ranked second on the team in snaps and targets, but Nailor posted a higher PFF receiving grade than Addison in 2025.

    This offseason, Nailor joined the Raiders, whose receivers ranked third-to-last in receiving yards and second-to-last in receiving touchdowns last season. Las Vegas added head coach Klint Kubiak and quarterbacks Kirk Cousins and Fernando Mendoza, all of whom should help the passing game. Still, the team made minimal changes at receiver, with Nailor in and Tyler Lockett out. That leaves Nailor and Tre Tucker projected as starters, while Jack Bech and Dont'e Thornton compete for the No. 3 role.

    According to PFF's draft guide, Mendoza performs best on short and intermediate throws outside the numbers. On those routes last season, Nailor turned six of 11 targets into first downs or touchdowns, compared with Tucker's nine of 28. Tucker was the Raiders' primary option in that area, while Justin Jefferson and Addison filled that role for the Vikings, which helps explain the gap in volume.

    Both receivers fit Mendoza's strengths, but Nailor has earned the better PFF grades so far, even though Tucker carries an earlier average draft position (ADP) on most sites. Based on his play to date, Nailor should be the first Raiders receiver off the board in fantasy drafts.

    165. WR Tre Tucker, Las Vegas Raiders

    Tucker started his career in a crowded receiver room led by Davante Adams, Jakobi Meyers and Hunter Renfrow. Renfrow left after 2023, Adams was traded in 2024 and Meyers was traded in 2025, which eventually left Tucker atop the Raiders' depth chart. Las Vegas drafted Jack Bech and Dont'e Thornton Jr. in 2025, but neither held a starting job during the season.

    Tucker finished last season 38th in fantasy points with 57 receptions for 696 yards and five touchdowns, though he ranked only 49th in points per game. He posted one excellent outing against the Washington Commanders in Week 3, catching eight passes for 145 yards and three touchdowns, but was held under 50 yards in 13 of 17 games.

    He faces a bit more competition for targets this season with the free-agent addition of Jalen Nailor. Nailor has graded better than Tucker over their careers, which could push Tucker down the target pecking order, though his playing time is only truly in question if Bech, Thornton or both take a real step forward.

    Even with the added competition, Tucker should benefit from an improved offense. The Raiders added Kirk Cousins and Fernando Mendoza at quarterback along with new linemen, led by center Tyler Linderbaum. Tucker is a speedy deep threat who caught 18 passes on deep and intermediate throws last year but also drew 19 uncatchable deep and intermediate targets. Las Vegas quarterbacks posted the second-lowest team accuracy on those throws at 36.8%. Cousins' 47.0% rate over the past two seasons is above league average, and Mendoza ranked third among Power Four quarterbacks last season at 58.7%. Whoever starts, Tucker should see more catchable balls, which should mean more big plays.

    Tucker is a fine late-round target, with a chance to be the second-best receiving option behind tight end Brock Bowers on an offense that could be far better than last season. But the offense might not improve, and he could slide down the depth chart if Nailor or one of the second-year receivers earns the quarterback's trust.

    166. WR De'Zhaun Stribling, San Francisco 49ers

    Stribling was the 33rd overall pick by the 49ers in the 2026 draft, going much earlier than most draft analysts expected. In college, the 6-foot-2 receiver ran a vertical route tree as a physical player who does not create much separation but wins at the catch point. Our draft guide gave him high marks for his competitive toughness and strength as a blocker.

    He joins a receiver room that changed significantly this offseason. Mike Evans was signed in free agency to play X, Deebo Samuel was brought back after a year absense to be their Z receiver, and Christian Kirk was added to work the slot. Stribling's long-term role is likely at X, replacing Evans, who turns 33 before the season starts.

    There has been significant speculation about what he does this season. Some beat writers think he will push Kirk for slot work, and those have amplified after Kirk missed time early in training camp. Some speculated he could see time at the Z receiver spot. The most likely outcome is that he rotates with Evans. The 49ers are run-heavy, and Stribling's willingness to block could earn him meaningful early-down snaps. Evans was a fine blocker earlier in his career, but his 38.2 run-block grade over the past four seasons ranks seventh-lowest among receivers with at least 2,000 snaps. It would not be surprising to see Stribling on the field for most 21 personnel snaps and Evans for most out of 11.

    That is unlikely to translate to much fantasy value as a rookie. He is still worth a late-round pick in deeper leagues, in case he both handles early downs for Evans and pushes one of the other receivers for playing time. His value should be considerably higher as soon as 2027, as San Francisco's X receiver has consistently produced since Brock Purdy took over at quarterback.

    167. TE Pat Freiermuth, Pittsburgh Steelers

    Freiermuth impressed as a rookie in 2021 with seven touchdowns. He gained 732 receiving yards the following season, ranking ninth among tight ends in fantasy points per game. His 2023 started slowly and was cut into by injuries, but he rebounded in 2024 with another seven-touchdown season, finishing 13th in fantasy points per game.

    His fantasy value plummeted in 2025, after offensive coordinator Arthur Smith added Jonnu Smith, a tight end he had worked with on both the Tennessee Titans and Atlanta Falcons. Former third-round pick Darnell Washington also took on a larger role, playing 57.1% of snaps.

    That left Freiermuth at just 50.6% of snaps. He had one excellent game against the Cincinnati Bengals, catching five passes for 111 yards and two touchdowns, but he was held to 33 yards or fewer in 14 of 18 games, including the playoffs.

    Neither of the Smiths is in Pittsburgh anymore, with Mike McCarthy taking over as head coach. That should mean more playing time for Freiermuth. It sounds like Pittsburgh wants him to be a bigger part of the offense, though his PFF receiving grade has finished below 72.0 in each of the last three seasons after coming in at 74.0 or better in his first two. Pittsburgh also added Michael Pittman Jr. in a trade and Germie Bernard in the second round of the draft, giving him much more target competition from the receiver room.

    Freiermuth is a fine late-round tight end if you're looking to gamble on your backup, since there is a chance he returns to borderline fantasy starter status. But there is also a chance he and Washington split the tight end room too evenly, and that Aaron Rodgers focuses on his receivers as he has for most of his career.

    168. TE Terrance Ferguson, Los Angeles Rams

    Ferguson was the 46th overall pick by the Los Angeles Rams. He joined a crowded tight end room that included Tyler Higbee, Colby Parkinson and Davis Allen. The Rams under Sean McVay have historically been slow to work rookies into their plans. He played only eight offensive snaps over the first five weeks, one of which produced a 21-yard reception.

    His role expanded after that single catch, and he played one-third of snaps from Weeks 6-13. That included over half the snaps in 11 personnel on third downs and in 13 personnel, but just under 50% in 12 personnel. He almost never saw the field in 11 personnel on early downs. Higbee missed Weeks 12-17, though it wasn't until Week 14 that Los Angeles trusted Ferguson with a larger role. He played 73.1% of snaps from Weeks 14-17, including 80% of 11 personnel on third downs and every 13 personnel snap.

    He caught just 11 passes on the season, but averaged 21.0 yards per reception and scored three touchdowns. The Rams returned all three tight ends and added Max Klare with the 61st overall pick of the 2026 draft. Even so, there is real optimism that Ferguson could break out.

    Several people around the team have pointed to the offseason he put together. McVay in particular has spoken glowingly of him, going into detail on how he can be used. The head coach called him a total stud with an incredibly bright future, praising his ability to line up at essentially every skill position on the offense and crediting his maturity, intelligence and instincts. McVay added that the best is yet to come, particularly as a receiver working short, intermediate and downfield.

    The deep-threat ability is a plus, but his 18.3 average depth of target was more than six yards higher than any other tight end. If the Rams can mix in easier catches alongside the deep shots, he could be an excellent option. Given the number of weapons in Los Angeles, though, the hesitancy is understandable.

    He is a late-round pick in deeper leagues, and it would not be surprising if a big week early in the season turns him into a popular waiver wire target.

    169. RB Jaydon Blue, Dallas Cowboys

    Blue was a fifth-round pick by Dallas in 2025. He was a common sleeper target because many thought he had a chance to win the starting job for the Cowboys ahead of veterans Javonte Williams and Miles Sanders. Instead, he was behind both at the start of the year. They had fullback Hunter Luepke, who could also play halfback. Most weeks, Dallas kept only three active running backs, leaving Blue as a healthy inactive.

    When Sanders suffered a season-ending injury, Blue saw limited playing time from Weeks 5 to 8. He ran 22 times for 65 yards and caught one pass. He lost the backup job to Malik Davis, but got to see significant playing time in Week 18, running 16 times for 64 yards and a touchdown.

    Head coach Brian Schottenheimer spoke glowingly of Blue's approach heading into his second season throughout OTAs, saying it's “night and day from where he was last year.” He added that Blue is “going to be a huge part of what we want to do, but he's got to continue to do his part, which he's doing great right now.”

    Blue is competing with Davis and Phil Mafah for the backup job, but Dallas seems to want Blue to win it. Williams remains the clear-cut starter, but Blue has handcuff value if he can win the backup role during training camp and the preseason.

    170. RB MarShawn Lloyd, Green Bay Packers

    The 2024 season was supposed to be a changing of the guard at running back for Green Bay, moving on from Aaron Jones and A.J. Dillon and replacing them with free-agent addition Josh Jacobs and third-round pick Lloyd. Instead, Lloyd suffered a hamstring injury in the preseason that cost him Week 1 of his rookie year. He played in Week 2 but hurt his ankle, landing on injured reserve. He was activated in November, only to develop appendicitis that ended his season.

    In 2025, another preseason hamstring injury saw Lloyd miss the entire year. He tried to return but reinjured it and went back on injured reserve. Through two NFL seasons, he has played a small role in one regular-season game.

    Despite the string of injuries, the Packers still intend for Lloyd to be their primary backup this season. Chris Brooks and Emanuel Wilson have handled that role recently, but Wilson left for the Seattle Seahawks in free agency. Brooks has been working at fullback in OTAs, suggesting he will be on the field in two-back sets but may not see much single-back work outside of third downs. The only other notable backs on the roster are Pierre Strong Jr., who spent a few years as a Cleveland Browns backup and most of last season on Green Bay's practice squad, and Damien Martinez, a 2025 seventh-round pick by the Seahawks who also landed on the Packers' practice squad late in the year.

    There is a path for Lloyd to be a top-10 fantasy running back. Jacobs has typically ranked in that range, and an injury or suspension could hand Lloyd the keys to the backfield, where his production profile is similar. The catch is that Green Bay could just as easily add another back if Jacobs misses time, and Lloyd carries obvious injury risk himself.

    That makes Lloyd a deep-league sleeper, though enough training camp and preseason hype could push him into late-round consideration in standard-sized leagues.

    171. RB Braelon Allen, New York Jets

    Allen was a fourth-round pick by the Jets, where he joined Breece Hall. Fewer than 40 picks later, the team added Isaiah Davis, who played 26.5% of snaps as a rookie in an early-down role.

    New York changed coaching staffs in the offseason, and there was some suggestion of a three-man rotation. Allen's snap rate rose only slightly over the first three weeks, to 27.9%. He ran the ball six times in two of the three games, something that happened in just 41% of his games as a rookie. It was Davis who saw the larger increase, taking over as the third-down back and cutting into Hall's work.

    An MCL sprain early in Week 4 cost Allen the rest of the 2025 season. Head coach Aaron Glenn still views his backfield as a three-headed monster, so Allen's snap rate should again land between 25% and 30% this season.

    Allen is listed at 235 pounds on the Jets' website but is reportedly up to 250, putting him alongside Derrick Henry and A.J. Dillon among the biggest backs in the league. That size should earn him his share of goal-line work.

    Allen has a chance at standalone value if the Jets' offense exceeds expectations and delivers more goal-line chances. Without them, his value is as the primary handcuff should Hall get hurt.

    172. WR Tre' Harris, Los Angeles Chargers

    Harris was the 55th overall pick of the 2025 draft by the Chargers. When he was drafted, it appeared he had a path to the second receiver spot behind Ladd McConkey given Quentin Johnston's early struggles. His chance at fantasy relevance as a rookie was gutted when the team signed Keenan Allen, a veteran with significant experience alongside Justin Herbert.

    Harris still carved out a real role as Los Angeles' primary receiver in single-receiver sets, playing 86.6% of those snaps from Week 7 through the end of the season. By Week 11, he was also taking time from Johnston and even McConkey in two-receiver sets, though those were primarily run plays.

    The Chargers have not re-signed Allen, opening the door for Harris to play more consistently in three-receiver sets. Given his usage on early downs, there is a chance he ends up with the most playing time of any receiver on the roster.

    The remaining question is how well he performs with that expanded role. He posted a 66.6 receiving grade as a rookie, with seven explosive plays and five avoided tackles, and his best statistical game was four catches for 54 yards in Week 16 against the Dallas Cowboys. The Chargers also have a new offensive coordinator in Mike McDaniel, whose Miami Dolphins offense was built on speed. Harris is not slow, but he lacks the speed of the other receivers on the roster.

    Harris is a fine speculative late-round pick in deeper leagues, and the first month of the season should tell us whether he is taking to the new role and the new offense.

    173. WR Ryan Flournoy, Dallas Cowboys

    Flournoy was a sixth-round pick by Dallas in 2024. He played sparingly as a rookie, catching 10 passes for 102 yards while buried down the Cowboys' depth chart. He started 2025 fifth on the depth chart behind CeeDee Lamb, George Pickens, Jalen Tolbert and KaVontae Turpin.

    Several early injuries led Flournoy to play over 50% of snaps for the first time in Week 5, where he caught six passes for 114 yards. He continued to see more playing time once the other receivers were healthy, typically playing between 40% and 45% of snaps. In a Week 14 shootout with the Detroit Lions, Flournoy caught nine passes for 115 yards and a touchdown despite the other Dallas receivers all being healthy.

    Flournoy received an 80.3 overall PFF grade last season, which would usually be good enough to be the second option in an offense. But Dallas still has Lamb and Pickens, so Flournoy is locked into the third receiver spot. Dallas ranked sixth in 11 personnel usage last season, and most of the teams ahead of them invested in tight ends this offseason. That means the Cowboys could lead the league in 11 personnel rate in 2026.

    Flournoy should be among the top third receivers in the NFL. He will likely go undrafted in most redraft leagues. Other third wideouts will likely get selected because they will have more opportunities to move up their respective depth charts without an injury. However, if Lamb or Pickens suffers a serious injury at some point this season, Flournoy would be one of the top waiver wire targets of the year.

    In deeper leagues and best ball leagues, Flournoy is worth rostering, as he will have a few big games throughout the year.

    174. RB George Holani, Seattle Seahawks

    Holani went undrafted in 2024 and caught on with the Seahawks. He spent most of his rookie season on the practice squad, appearing in five games but primarily on special teams. He beat out seventh-round rookie Damien Martinez for the third running back spot in 2025, behind Kenneth Walker III and Zach Charbonnet.

    He played 133 special teams snaps and 36 offensive snaps during the regular season, and 26 of those offensive snaps came in the fourth quarter of three blowout wins. When Charbonnet tore his ACL in the playoffs, Holani took over as the second running back option in the conference championship game and Super Bowl, playing more on offense in those two games than in the rest of his career combined. He inherited Charbonnet's role as the two-minute back and handled most of the third downs. Charbonnet had also been the primary goal-line option, but Seattle ran only one play inside the five-yard line across the two games, and Walker got the snap.

    Walker is no longer on the roster, and Charbonnet is still recovering from the ACL injury. Seattle spent a first-round pick on Jadarian Price, who is Holani's primary competition early in the season. The general expectation is that Price will be the early-down back and Holani will work on third downs until Charbonnet is ready. If he ends up with the goal-line carries over Price, he could be a flex option, and a strong training camp relative to Price could also make him viable in Week 1.

    He is unlikely to hold much fantasy value over the course of the season, but he is the cheapest running back you could draft and plausibly stick in your Week 1 lineup, even before accounting for any new injuries at the position.

    175. WR Isaac TeSlaa, Detroit Lions

    TeSlaa was a third-round pick by the Lions and opened his career fourth on Detroit's receiver depth chart behind Amon-Ra St. Brown, Jameson Williams and Kalif Raymond. He caught a touchdown in his first game but hauled in only three passes over his next 10. Injuries began cutting into the receiver room late in the season, opening up a more consistent role, and he caught 12 passes with four touchdowns across his final six games.

    The Lions did not re-sign Raymond this offseason, sliding TeSlaa up to third on the depth chart. Their main addition at the position is Greg Dortch, more of a backup slot option, while TeSlaa plays X. That should mean steadier playing time throughout the season.

    The bad news is that TeSlaa will face significant target competition all year, barring injuries. St. Brown and Williams remain the clear top two options, and Jahmyr Gibbs and Sam LaPorta return at running back and tight end, giving Detroit arguably the most well-rounded skill-position group in the league.

    TeSlaa converted six of his 16 receptions into touchdowns, and he should remain a touchdown magnet. He is noticeably taller than both Williams and St. Brown, and even taller than LaPorta, the league's only every-down tight end at 6-foot-3 or shorter. TeSlaa should deliver a few spike games where he catches a couple of passes and a touchdown while defenses focus elsewhere. His real value, though, would come from a significant receiver injury in Detroit, which could turn him into an every-down player.

    176. WR Omar Cooper Jr., New York Jets

    Cooper is a well-balanced receiver with significant experience in the slot who should also be able to play the Z role in the NFL. He was the second pass catcher the Jets took on the first day of the 2026 draft, coming 14 picks after tight end Kenyon Sadiq.

    Cooper will likely help the Jets more than he helps fantasy managers, at least early in his rookie season. Both he and Garrett Wilson fill more of a Z/slot role, while Adonai Mitchell is a clear X. New offensive coordinator Frank Reich is accustomed to a tall X receiver like D.J. Chark, Alec Pierce and Michael Pittman Jr., though T.Y. Hilton, at 5-foot-10, also filled that role at times in Indianapolis. It is questionable whether Cooper will see the field in two-receiver sets, and the Jets look like a team that will lean on 12 personnel after drafting tight ends early in each of the past two seasons.

    Cooper should still have an easy path to the third receiver spot, with Isaiah Williams and Arian Smith as the top backups in the Z and slot roles. The two combined for fewer than 250 yards and no touchdowns despite substantial playing time at points last season. Wilson is also coming back from an MCL sprain that cost him nearly half of last year, and Cooper could become the Jets' top passing-game option if Wilson were to get hurt again.

    He is worth a late-round pick regardless of the situation. Plenty of first-round rookie receivers have landed in crowded rooms and broken out anyway. The catch is that Cooper is more likely to break out in the second half of the season than the first.

    177. RB Kaytron Allen, Washington Commanders

    Allen was the ninth running back selected in the 2026 NFL Draft, going to the Washington Commanders with the 187th pick in round six. Our draft guide gave him high marks for his mentality and tackle-breaking ability, but low marks for his receiving and speed. He is relatively short and heavy for a running back. While some backs of that size have become stars, like Jonathan Taylor and Josh Jacobs, several turned into two-down backs, like Jordan Mason, Tyler Allgeier or the man he's replacing, Chris Rodriguez Jr.

    He joins a crowded running back room, with incumbents Jacory Croskey-Merritt and Jeremy McNichols, and free agent additions Rachaad White and Jerome Ford. The general expectation is that Washington uses a three-back committee, with White playing on third downs and some combination of Croskey-Merritt and Allen playing on early downs.

    Allen could carve out a role where he sees a few carries per game, typically in short-yardage and goal-line situations. That could give him some fantasy value if Washington returns to being a top offense and Allen is consistently scoring the short-yardage touchdowns.

    Since being drafted, Allen has been one of the most hyped rookies, both by what the coaches said after drafting him and what beat writers have said during OTAs. There is at least a chance Allen can overtake Croskey-Merritt for the lead rusher role. Croskey-Merritt showed some great play early in his rookie season, but lost his starting job to Rodriguez late in the season.

    Even if he wins the early-down role, he will need to share the backfield with White, putting his ceiling at a low-end fantasy starter. He will be a player worth drafting if the hype for Allen continues early in training camp.

    178. WR Travis Hunter, Jacksonville Jaguars

    Hunter was the second overall pick of the 2025 draft and the rare player who can contribute significantly on both offense and defense. He had a consistent role in the Jaguars offense as their third receiver in 11 personnel, playing 90.9% of snaps in three-receiver sets but only two of a possible 112 in two-receiver sets and none in single-receiver looks. His cornerback usage was far less predictable, with three games at 20% or fewer, two around 40% and two between 60% and 70%.

    He failed to make much of a mark as a receiver over the first six weeks, held to 33 yards or fewer in four of them and peaking at 64 with no touchdowns. Then, in Week 7 against the Los Angeles Rams, he caught eight passes for 101 yards and a touchdown. He posted receiving grades above 70.0 in that game and in Week 5 against the Kansas City Chiefs. Unfortunately, he tore his LCL just as a second-half breakout seemed to be coming.

    Hunter appears ready for training camp, but his path to fantasy relevance has taken significant hits since the injury. Jacksonville traded for Jakobi Meyers, and Parker Washington thrived in Hunter's role, gaining at least 85 yards in each of his last four games, including the playoffs, and scoring in three of them. That presumably drops Hunter to fourth on the receiver depth chart. The Jaguars also let Greg Newsome II walk, opening the door for a more permanent defensive role, and drafted two tight ends, which likely means more two-tight-end sets, formations Hunter did not play in last season.

    Hunter appears ready for training camp, but his path to fantasy relevance has taken significant hits since the injury. Jacksonville traded for Jakobi Meyers, and Parker Washington thrived in Hunter's role, gaining at least 85 yards in each of his last four games, including the playoffs, and scoring in three of them. That presumably drops Hunter to fourth on the receiver depth chart. The Jaguars also let Greg Newsome II walk, opening the door for a more permanent defensive role, and drafted two tight ends, which likely means more two-tight-end sets, formations Hunter did not play in last season.

    179. QB Daniel Jones, Indianapolis Colts

    Jones has spent seven years as an NFL starter, with wildly different fantasy results. He topped out in 2022 with the ninth-most fantasy points per game but has also finished outside the top 30 multiple times. Last season, his first with the Colts, he finished 12th at 18.0 fantasy points per game.

    Jones’ 73.2 career PFF passing grade ranks sixth-lowest among the 29 quarterbacks with at least 2,000 dropbacks, and three of the quarterbacks below him are now backups. His single-season grade has hovered between 65.0 and 71.0 most years, so there's little reason to expect a substantial jump in his passing quality.

    A big part of Jones’ earlier success was his rushing. In his best season, 2022, he ran 120 times for 708 yards and seven touchdowns. That production has steadily declined since, and last season, he posted career lows of 3.5 attempts and 12.6 yards per game, though he did score five rushing touchdowns.

    Those scores helped him to a strong year in Indianapolis, and he also posted a career-high 8.6 average depth of target. That raised Jones’ big-time throw rate but also his turnover-worthy play clip, a combination that generally helps fantasy production.

    Jones then suffered a ruptured Achilles that ended his season early. He is expected to be ready for Week 1, but he is unlikely to run for as many touchdowns. The Colts also traded away Michael Pittman Jr. and lost right tackle Braden Smith in free agency without making substantial additions, so the offense could take a small step back.

    Jones is a fine second option in superflex leagues but should be drafted in single-quarterback redraft leagues only if the benches are deeper than usual.

    180. RB Jonah Coleman, Denver Broncos

    Coleman is a unique back at 5-foot-8 and 228 pounds, a slower power runner with excellent tackle-breaking ability and balance. Unlike most backs his build, he has found success as a receiver and has been strong in pass protection, as long as he picks up the right assignment.

    Coleman is a unique back at 5-foot-8 and 228 pounds, a slower power runner with excellent tackle-breaking ability and balance. Unlike most backs his build, he has found success as a receiver and has been strong in pass protection, as long as he picks up the right assignment.

    The general expectation is that Dobbins, Harvey and Coleman form a three-man committee, though there is a wide range of outcomes for how the snaps get divided. Coleman could see plenty of third-down work given that was Badie's role and he has graded well as a pass protector, and he could take on short-yardage duty as well.

    Considering Dobbins' injury history and Harvey's slow start as a rookie, there is a path for Coleman to become the lead option in a one-two punch. But with five backs Denver is comfortable with, even a Dobbins injury could simply produce a different three-back committee. The closest comparison to a back head coach Sean Payton has leaned on is Mark Ingram, who did not post his first 1,000-yard season until his sixth year and his first double-digit touchdown season in his seventh.

    Coleman is likely in for a similar slow build, making meaningful fantasy value unlikely this season. Still, given his talent and the unpredictability of Payton's backfield rotations, he is worth a late-round pick in deeper leagues.

    181. WR Dontayvion Wicks, Philadelphia Eagles

    Wicks was a fifth-round pick by the Green Bay Packers. When everyone was healthy, he was a backup behind Romeo Doubs, Christian Watson and Jayden Reed. His 75.4 grade over the past three seasons was the worst among the Packers‘ four receivers, but would typically be good enough to rank top-three on most depth charts. He avoided 22 tackles over those three years, the most among Green Bay's receivers, and posted the highest rate of positive grades per route, though also the most drops and fumbles.

    Wicks was traded from the Packers to the Philadelphia Eagles this offseason, and Packers quarterbacks coach Sean Mannion also joined the Eagles as their offensive coordinator. The Eagles spent the past two seasons with A.J. Brown, DeVonta Smith and Jahan Dotson as their top three receivers, but Brown was traded and Dotson left in free agency. Philadelphia also drafted Makai Lemon and added Hollywood Brown to fill out the depth chart.

    The expectation is that Wicks will be the X receiver, Smith the Z, Lemon in the slot and Hollywood Brown the top backup. Wicks doesn't have A.J. Brown‘s size, but he is clearly the biggest of this group. He is also similar to Brown in one respect: both excel at avoiding tackles. They are two of just 13 wide receivers to avoid 0.18 tackles per reception over the past three seasons among those with at least 100 receptions.

    Smith is highly likely to be the top target earner, and there is a decent chance Lemon emerges as the second option. But Lemon could struggle as a rookie, and even if he plays well, he might be limited to three-receiver sets while Wicks plays every down. When the Eagles' offense is clicking, it has been able to support two fantasy-relevant receivers. There is a chance Wicks is that second receiver, making him worth a late-round pick in deeper leagues.

    182. WR Calvin Ridley, Tennessee Titans

    Ridley has spent the past two seasons as the Titans' top receiving option when healthy. He has gone through stretches as a non-factor and others as a dominant fantasy force. In 2024, he caught two passes for 14 yards across three games despite 14 targets, then ranked 19th in fantasy points from Weeks 8 to 18, including the seventh-most yards at 834. In 2025, he posted a five-catch, 131-yard game but was also held under 30 yards in two of the first three weeks, two of only four healthy games all season.

    Ridley suffered a hamstring injury in October and a fibula fracture shortly after returning, ending his 2025. He is now 31 and has played just two full seasons over the past five years, though he cleared 1,000 receiving yards in both.

    He faces significant new competition for targets. The Titans drafted Carnell Tate fourth overall, and he is expected to play X. Wan'Dale Robinson was a notable free-agent addition and will work the slot. That leaves the Z role for Ridley, who projects third on the team in targets. Tennessee also has second-year receivers Elic Ayomanor and Chimere Dike, who could take snaps away as the team works to get them on the field.

    The third receiver role in Tennessee is unlikely to hold much fantasy value. But there is a chance the offense improves significantly with a new offensive coordinator and Cam Ward in his second season. Some rookie receivers make an immediate impact and others need time, so Ridley could remain the top outside option for at least part of the season. If the offense takes a step forward, that could translate into real fantasy value.

    183. RB Kaelon Black, San Francisco 49ers

    Black has the size and speed of a three-down back. He is near the middle of the pack in height and weight, while his speed, acceleration and change of direction all rate above average compared to running backs in this draft class.

    The first concern is his lack of receiving production at Indiana. He averaged a solid 1.38 yards per route run in his final season at James Madison before transferring, but he rarely ran routes and was targeted even less often after transferring. His snap rate also never exceeded 45%, as he saw the field primarily on rushing downs.

    Our draft guide noted he is better suited to zone-blocking schemes, and the data supports that. He averaged 5.5 yards per attempt on zone runs compared to 4.7 on gap concepts. The guide also noted he has occasionally been a half-step late identifying running lanes in power concepts and that his run game lacks true difference-making creativity.

    Black was the only running back selected on Day 2 of the 2026 draft and is a strong fit for Kyle Shanahan's zone-blocking scheme. San Francisco has used a third- through fifth-round pick on a running back in five of the past six drafts, and the first four have not worked out. There is a real chance Black earns the backup job ahead of Jordan James and Isaac Guerendo. Shanahan has said he wants to get Christian McCaffrey help, and Black could fill that role. Given McCaffrey's injury history, which has cost him time in multiple seasons, Black would be a strong fantasy option if he misses time again.

    Guerendo tore a pectoral muscle during OTAs and opens training camp on the PUP list, while James fractured his ribs at the start of camp. That could leave Black winning the backup job by default rather than having to earn it in competition. Combined with McCaffrey's injury history and the quality of San Francisco's offensive line, it makes him one of the top handcuff options this season.

    184. WR Jerry Jeudy, Cleveland Browns

    Jeudy has spent the past two seasons as the top wide receiver for the Browns after opening his career with four years in Denver. His production in Cleveland has depended almost entirely on who is at quarterback. He posted 1.05 yards per route run, an 18% target rate and three touchdowns on 989 routes without Jameis Winston, compared to 2.50 yards per route run, a 21.8% target rate and three touchdowns on 316 snaps with him. Winston, unfortunately, is now the backup quarterback for the New York Giants.

    The two candidates to start for Cleveland this season are Deshaun Watson and Shedeur Sanders. Jeudy has averaged 1.03 yards per route run on a 14.5% target rate with Watson and 1.08 on a 15.6% target rate with Sanders.

    To make matters worse, he faces far more competition for targets. Cleveland's top six pass catchers by receptions last season were three running backs, two tight ends and Jeudy. Cedric Tillman was the next receiver on the list at 21 catches. The Browns then spent a first-round pick on K.C. Concepcion and a second-rounder on Denzel Boston, and Isaiah Bond, who joined late last offseason, is expected to have a larger role.

    There is some reason to believe those additions could help. Jeudy spent the first five seasons of his career as a Z or slot receiver but primarily played X last season, logging 621 snaps out wide on the line compared to a previous career high of 335. In Cleveland, he has averaged 1.14 yards per route lined up out wide on the line, compared to 1.99 off the line and 1.28 in the slot. On a typical play out of 11 personnel, Boston projects to line up at X, Jeudy at Z and Concepcion in the slot, which puts Jeudy in his best position to succeed. Defenses have also been able to key on him the past two seasons, which becomes harder if the rookies produce.

    Jeudy is worth a late-round pick in deep leagues, since he could remain Cleveland's top option despite the additions, and those additions could make him more efficient enough to be a weekly starter. But if one of the rookies takes over or he fails to develop chemistry with his quarterback, he can probably be dropped after a month or two.

    185. WR Ted Hurst III, Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    Hurst is an X receiver with excellent hands and competitive toughness. He is unlikely to post great separation numbers in the NFL but should win his share of contested catches. He was the 16th-ranked receiver on our big board and the 15th selected in the draft, going 84th overall to the Tampa Bay Buccaneers.

    On paper, Hurst seems unlikely to contribute much as a rookie given that Tampa Bay has Emeka Egbuka, Chris Godwin, Jalen McMillan and Tez Johnson. But he was one of the most hyped rookies of the offseason, and offensive coordinator Zac Robinson has talked about moving Egbuka to Z and playing Godwin in the slot.

    Robinson was previously an assistant with the Los Angeles Rams, where they typically did not use a traditional X receiver. He compared Hurst to Odell Beckham Jr. during their Super Bowl season. Beckham joined that team midseason and played at least 70% of snaps in eight of 12 games, with two of the four misses coming in his first game on the roster and in the Super Bowl, which he exited early. Beckham was not consistent for fantasy, but he scored seven touchdowns in those 12 games and cleared 50 yards six times.

    It is not entirely fair to compare a third-round rookie to a 29-year-old Beckham, but there is a path to playing time if Tampa Bay wants an X receiver on the field consistently, and a path to him becoming a red-zone threat. Mike Evans was the team's touchdown magnet, and while Hurst is not Evans, he is well suited to convert contested end-zone targets into scores.

    It typically does not make sense to draft a third-round rookie receiver in most redraft leagues, but if the buzz continues through training camp, Hurst becomes a late-round sleeper.

    186. WR Rashod Bateman, Baltimore Ravens

    Bateman has spent the past five seasons as the X receiver for the Ravens. He dealt with multiple injuries over his first two years but has started at least 12 games in each of the last three. The former first-round pick flashed some promise in 2024, scoring nine touchdowns, though he finished just 50th in fantasy points per game with only 2.6 receptions per game.

    Bateman averaged 69% of offensive snaps last season, playing 86.9% in three-receiver sets, 77.3% in two-receiver sets and only 7.8% in single-receiver looks. He was targeted on 11.0% of his routes, sixth-lowest among receivers with at least 300 routes, and only one player below him is still on the same roster. His 0.70 yards per route run ranked third-lowest. Part of the problem was Lamar Jackson's injuries, but even with Jackson at quarterback, his target rate climbed only to 11.8%.

    To make matters worse, the Ravens keep drafting receivers capable of playing X. Devontez Walker was a fourth-round pick in 2024, and Ja'Kobi Lane and Elijah Sarratt were added in the third and fourth rounds of the 2026 draft. Several veteran receivers also remain available, and Baltimore is among the teams most likely to add one.

    All of that adds up to a player who should be off fantasy radars, but the Ravens have a new coaching staff, with Declan Doyle as offensive coordinator. Bateman is currently one of two starting receivers on a team with an MVP quarterback, and he is only two years removed from a nine-touchdown season. He belongs only in deeper leagues for now, though it is entirely possible he emerges as a flex option this season.

    187. RB Jordan James, San Francisco 49ers

    James was a fifth-round pick by the 49ers, a team that has consistently drafted running backs in the middle rounds in recent years. That initially put him in a backfield behind Christian McCaffrey, competing with Isaac Guerendo for the backup job. San Francisco then made a midpreseason trade for Brian Robinson Jr., pushing both young backs further down the depth chart.

    James was a healthy inactive for the first 15 weeks of the season. Guerendo served as the third running back and did not play an offensive snap all year, logging 173 special teams snaps instead. His special teams role shrank in Week 15, and the 49ers flipped to making James active and Guerendo inactive, a move that held the rest of the way. James appeared in three regular-season games and two playoff games, with his special teams work growing each week. His only offensive touches came in the 41-6 playoff loss to the Seattle Seahawks, when he ran six times for 28 yards and caught a 7-yard pass.

    Robinson left in free agency, but San Francisco drafted Kaelon Black in the third round, putting James in direct competition with him for the backup job. McCaffrey has missed significant time in three of the past five seasons, so there is a solid chance another 49ers back holds value at some point this year.

    For now, both James and Black are late-round picks in deep redraft leagues. If one takes a firm hold of the backup job in training camp and the preseason, he will be worth drafting in every redraft league.

    188. WR Malik Washington, Miami Dolphins

    Washington was a sixth-round pick by the Dolphins in 2024. The 5-foot-8, 195-pound receiver made his NFL debut in Week 4 and was held to 20 yards or fewer every week until Week 15. He was rotating with Odell Beckham Jr. for the third receiver spot behind Tyreek Hill and Jaylen Waddle during that stretch. The Dolphins released Beckham, opening the door for an extended role. Waddle also missed time, and Washington played over 86% of snaps in both Weeks 16 and 17, gaining at least 25 receiving yards in each.

    He was the clear second receiving option throughout 2025 behind Waddle, with Hill missing most of the season due to injury. He was consistently on the field in 11 personnel but used only sparingly in two-receiver sets. He averaged 57.3% of snaps over the season, with his playing time ranging from 44% to 70%. There was a general trend of him playing less in wins, as Miami was more likely to go with two receivers late with a lead. He caught at least one pass in every game but never had more than five receptions or 50 receiving yards in one.

    The Dolphins offense has undergone significant changes this offseason. Bobby Slowik was promoted to offensive coordinator, and Malik Willis was signed to be the new quarterback. Washington was the one notable holdover at wide receiver, joined by free agent additions Jalen Tolbert and Tutu Atwell and rookies Caleb Douglas, Chris Bell and Kevin Coleman.

    He will likely play in the slot, but he needs a larger role to be a fantasy starter. It is rare for a receiver his size to become fantasy-relevant, with Wan'Dale Robinson, Cole Beasley and Tank Dell the primary others over the past decade to gain at least 650 yards in a season. Robinson's 2025 was the best fantasy season by a receiver that size in that span.

    Ideally, we find out at some point during the preseason whether the Dolphins are considering using him out wide more often. Without that, he remains a late-round target, as he has a chance to lead the team in targets even in an 11 personnel-only role.

    189. WR Caleb Douglas, Miami Dolphins

    Douglas was the 75th overall pick of the 2026 draft, going to the Miami Dolphins. He may have the best chance among Day 2 wide receivers to play in two-receiver sets as a rookie, outside of Denzel Boston, who landed with the Cleveland Browns after falling out of the first round. He was not especially productive in college but posted excellent measurables, including a 6-foot-4 frame, a 4.39-second 40-yard dash, a 10-foot-6 broad jump and 10.13-inch hands.

    He projects as an X receiver, and Miami does not have a clear option there. The three most notable receivers on the roster, Tutu Atwell, Jalen Tolbert and Malik Washington, all profile more as Z or slot types. New offensive coordinator Bobby Slowik also has a track record of developing productive X receivers, including Brandon Aiyuk with the San Francisco 49ers and Nico Collins with the Houston Texans.

    Miami drafted Chris Bell 19 picks later, but Bell is spending training camp on the non-football injury list after an injury late last season, which could limit him early. Douglas, meanwhile, already appears to be ahead of Atwell and Tolbert in the target pecking order this early in camp.

    He is a fine late-round option in deeper leagues, with a real chance to lead Miami in targets this season.

    190. WR Darnell Mooney, New York Giants

    Mooney has played six seasons in the NFL, including four with the Chicago Bears and two with the Atlanta Falcons. He's been a starter throughout that time, but rarely a fantasy starter. He finished 38th in fantasy points per game in 2024, while his best season from a fantasy perspective was 2021, when he averaged 12.9 PPR points per game, 31st among wide receivers that season.

    He signed a one-year, $3 million contract with the New York Giants, joining a very crowded wide receiver room. New York kept Darius Slayton and Malik Nabers, drafted Malachi Fields in the third round, and added Calvin Austin III in free agency. In June, the room became more crowded with the addition of Odell Beckham Jr. and JuJu Smith-Schuster.

    Slayton, Fields and Beckham are all likely competing for the X receiver role. While Mooney was the X receiver for the Falcons last season, he was a Z and slot receiver early in his career for the Bears. Giants offensive coordinator Matt Nagy was Mooney's head coach in Chicago in 2020 and 2021, including his only 1,000-yard season. He is likely competing for snaps at Z and slot in New York with Nabers, Austin and Smith-Schuster.

    Considering how much New York invested in Mooney relative to Austin and Smith-Schuster, he should be the favorite to play in the slot in 11 personnel. This won't be enough to make Mooney fantasy-relevant in most leagues, but Nabers' health will be worth monitoring. Nabers tore his ACL and meniscus last season and might not be ready for the start of this season. If that happens, Mooney could fill Nabers' role until he's ready, and potentially be the top receiving weapon in the offense.

    This makes Mooney an intriguing late-round option, particularly for a fantasy manager who selected multiple rookies or other receivers who might have a slow start.

    191. WR Jaylin Noel, Houston Texans

    Noel was a third-round pick by the Houston Texans. He spent his rookie season as a rotational slot receiver, splitting the role with Christian Kirk. Kirk had more snaps, routes, targets, receptions, yards and touchdowns, but Noel was the higher-graded of the two with more yards per route run. Noel never played more than 50% of snaps in a game, and he logged just 12 offensive snaps across Houston's two playoff games as the team leaned on Kirk's veteran experience.

    Kirk left this offseason for the San Francisco 49ers, opening the door for Noel to be the primary slot receiver. But the Texans also expect Tank Dell back after he missed all of last season, and both Dell and fellow second-year receiver Jayden Higgins have experience at Z, making it unlikely Noel plays anywhere but the slot. Houston could also use Dell inside, as he posted 1.52 yards per route run from the slot in 2024, compared to Noel's 1.36 last year, and there was talk in OTAs of Higgins seeing more slot work as well.

    There is also a risk Houston leans more on 12 personnel. The Texans had the sixth-lowest rate of 12 personnel last season, largely because of injuries to Cade Stover and Brevin Jordan. They added free agent Foster Moreau and drafted Marlin Klein in the second round, which should push those numbers up and mean fewer opportunities for Noel to be on the field.

    Noel is an okay target in deeper leagues as a talented young receiver in a good offense. But a lot is working against his snap share, which hurts his floor and caps his ceiling unless he can become the clear-cut second option in the passing game.

    192. WR Jahan Dotson, Atlanta Falcons

    Dotson was a first-round pick by the Washington Commanders in 2022. After two straight seasons of at least 500 yards, he was traded to the Philadelphia Eagles to serve as their third receiver behind A.J. Brown and DeVonta Smith. He managed just 1.1 receptions per game in each of those seasons, with Brown, Smith, Saquon Barkley, Dallas Goedert and Jalen Hurts on designed runs all ahead of him in the pecking order.

    There were a few notable games in Philadelphia, including seven catches for 94 yards in Week 18 of 2024 and 42 yards in the Eagles' Super Bowl win. Last season, he had four games of at least 40 yards and six without a catch. He was targeted on 7.8% of his routes over the last two seasons, the lowest rate among all 132 wide receivers with at least 300 routes.

    He signed a two-year, $15 million deal with the Atlanta Falcons this offseason. Atlanta turned over its coaching staff and is now led by Kevin Stefanski, with Tua Tagovailoa taking over at quarterback. The receiver room was rebuilt as well. Eight different wide receivers drew a target for the Falcons last season, and only Drake London and backup Dylan Drummond are still on the roster. Atlanta signed Olamide Zaccheaus in free agency and drafted Zachariah Branch in the third round.

    Despite those additions, Dotson has consistently worked as the second receiver behind London throughout training camp. He still projects to finish fourth in targets behind London, tight end Kyle Pitts and running back Bijan Robinson, which caps his fantasy value.

    He is worth a late-round pick in deeper leagues anyway. An injury to any of those three would likely make him a featured part of the offense.

    193. K Brandon Aubrey, Dallas Cowboys

    Aubrey has made the All-Pro team and the Pro Bowl in each of his three seasons. He has finished among the top three kickers in fantasy points all three years with at least 180 points, making him the one kicker who can generally be trusted every season.

    Part of the reason for his success is that the Cowboys offense is typically among the highest-scoring in the league while their defense has allowed plenty of points, forcing Dallas to keep pace. Most importantly, Aubrey has made the most field goals of 50 or more yards in each of the past three seasons. Dallas' willingness to attempt those kicks, unlike most teams, is a big reason he is widely considered the top kicker this year.

    His schedule is also ideal from a weather standpoint. The Cowboys play in a dome, and three of their eight road games are in domes or retractable-roof stadiums. Their trips to typical cold-weather cities come either early in the season or in Week 18, making it unlikely Aubrey deals with snow or cold during the fantasy season.

    Aubrey is the clear top kicker this season, and depending on your league's scoring, he is someone worth taking a few rounds before your final pick rather than waiting entirely or skipping the position.

    194. DEF Los Angeles Rams DST, Los Angeles Rams

    The Rams ranked fifth in fantasy points on defense last season, and they've added to an already well-rounded unit that ranked in the top 10 in points allowed, interceptions, sacks and fumble recoveries.

    Los Angeles made major additions to that defense this offseason, trading for edge rusher Myles Garrett, who posted a 93.0 grade last season, and cornerback Trent McDuffie, while signing McDuffie's former Chiefs teammate Jaylen Watson. Both corners graded in the mid-70s, and all three are clear upgrades over the departed Jared Verse, Cobie Durant and Darious Williams. There is also a rumor that Aaron Donald could come out of retirement, which would upgrade the group even further.

    The main concern is the schedule. The Rams have the third-most difficult overall schedule and the eighth-worst for their defense, according to the strength of schedule tool.

    195. DEF Seattle Seahawks DST, Seattle Seahawks

    The Seahawks were the clear top defense in fantasy football last season, averaging nearly a point more than the second-place unit and two points more than the third. Seattle led the league in points allowed, but they also scored a combined seven touchdowns on defense and special teams, which is likely to regress.

    The Seahawks lost a few key players in free agency, including safety Coby Bryant to the Chicago Bears, cornerback Riq Woolen to the Philadelphia Eagles and defensive end Boye Mafe to the Cincinnati Bengals. Seattle added some free agents and rookies for depth, but the defense could take a slight step back without those role players.

    Seattle also has one of the five worst schedules in the league this season. The touchdown regression, the departures and the tough schedule should all chip away at their point total from last season, but that still leaves them as a top-five defense.

    196. DEF Houston Texans DST, Houston Texans

    The Texans had the second-best fantasy defense last season, though there is reason to expect some statistical regression. Houston retained largely the same lineup, as everyone with more than 400 snaps returned, with safety Reed Blankenship the only notable addition.

    Some of last season's production came from volatile areas. Houston scored four defensive touchdowns, tied for the league lead, and their 10 fumble recoveries and 19 interceptions both ranked among the top five, all of which are relatively unstable year to year.

    That said, the Texans also ranked second in points allowed and first in yards allowed, and those numbers are more stable. Chances are Houston will be one of the top fantasy defenses again this season, though some luck will determine whether they finish as the No. 1 defense or a team closer to fifth.

    197. DEF Denver Broncos DST, Denver Broncos

    The Broncos ranked fourth among fantasy defenses last season, thanks to their league-leading 68 sacks and the third-fewest points allowed. They accomplished this with a well-rounded front four led by Nik Bonitto and Zach Allen.

    Denver's defense looks largely the same this year, though they lost John Franklin-Myers, who contributed 7.5 of those sacks, and Dre Greenlaw, who graded out at 86.9 in a small, injury-shortened sample. Their only notable addition was third-round defensive lineman Tyler Onyedim.

    Denver will likely see some regression in their sack total, but they should remain one of the league's best defensive units, which should keep them among the top fantasy defenses as well.

    198. DEF Philadelphia Eagles DST, Philadelphia Eagles

    The Eagles ranked fifth in points allowed last season but only seventh in fantasy points, due to a lack of big plays relative to other top defenses. Most notably, their 42 sacks ranked outside the top 10. The unit was remarkably similar in 2024, ranking seventh in fantasy points and just outside the top 10 in sacks.

    Philadelphia moved on from two defensive backs, Adoree' Jackson and Reed Blankenship, and upgraded at cornerback with Riq Woolen. The biggest reason for optimism is the addition of Jonathan Greenard, who earned a 76.4 defensive grade last season. He will likely start at edge rusher and could help lift a sack total that has been a weakness.

    The changes should help keep them among the top 10. Other units might have a little more upside, but their high floor makes them a very safe option.

    199. DEF Baltimore Ravens DST, Baltimore Ravens

    The Ravens have historically been an excellent option at team defense, ranking second as recently as 2023. Baltimore hasn't been as successful the past two seasons, which included a slow start to 2025, though they scored the eighth-most points among team defenses over the final eight weeks.

    The front four is the big reason to expect a noticeable leap in 2026. Baltimore added edge rusher Trey Hendrickson, the top player to hit free agency this offseason. Over the past three seasons, Hendrickson's 92.0 pass-rush grade ranks fifth among all edge defenders, behind Myles Garrett, Micah Parsons, Aidan Hutchinson and Nick Bosa.

    The Ravens also brought back Calais Campbell, and there is a chance Nnamdi Madubuike returns from his neck injury. They join defensive player of the year candidate Kyle Hamilton and linebacker Roquan Smith to form what should be an excellent unit. Baltimore will also be helped with a top-five schedule this season.

    200. K Cameron Dicker, Los Angeles Chargers

    Dicker has spent the past four seasons as the Chargers' kicker, and his fantasy value comes from accuracy. He has made 93.5% of his career field goal attempts, the best mark in NFL history, and has connected on at least 92% in every season of his career.

    The Chargers have consistently finished outside the top 10 in points scored, but that has not slowed him down. He has ranked in the top four in fantasy points among kickers in each of the past two seasons and is the only kicker with at least 38 made field goals in both. If anything, the concern this year is that a better Chargers offense leads to fewer field goals and more extra points.

    Dicker also benefits from a favorable weather schedule. His December road games include Las Vegas and two trips to Florida, and the only visit to a traditionally cold-weather city is Buffalo, which comes in September.

    He is the clear No. 2 kicker, a tier below Brandon Aubrey because he does not attempt as many long kicks, but a tier above everyone else thanks to a consistency no one else has matched.

    201. DEF Pittsburgh Steelers DST, Pittsburgh Steelers

    The Steelers ranked ninth among team defenses last year. They made plenty of big plays, ranking second in fumble recoveries, third in defensive touchdowns and inside the top 10 in both sacks and interceptions. However, they were slightly below league average in points allowed.

    Pittsburgh's front seven looks nearly identical to last season, but the secondary was overhauled. Cornerbacks Darius Slay and James Pierre, along with safeties Chuck Clark, Juan Thornhill and Jabrill Peppers, are all off the roster after each played at least 100 snaps last season. In their place are free-agent additions Jamel Dean at cornerback and Jaquan Brisker at safety. Dean notably posted an 80.7 defensive grade for the Tampa Bay Buccaneers last season.

    Those changes should help Pittsburgh remain a top-10 fantasy defense. The concern is age. T.J. Watt, Cameron Heyward and Jalen Ramsey are three key pieces of this defense, and all of them will be at least 32 years old by midseason.

    212. DEF Los Angeles Chargers DST, Los Angeles Chargers

    The Chargers ranked 14th in team defense last season, but they are in store for some positive regression. They recovered just four fumbles, tied for the second-fewest in the league, and scored no touchdowns on defense or special teams. Meanwhile, they ranked third in interceptions with 19 and finished inside the top 10 in both sacks and points allowed.

    Los Angeles returns almost the entire unit, including 10 of 11 starters. The only notable change came at defensive tackle, where Dalvin Tomlinson replaces Da'Shawn Hand, which shouldn't move the needle much. Akheem Mesidor was added to help the pass rush, but he projects as a long-term replacement for Khalil Mack rather than someone they need this season.

    The Chargers should be in fantasy starting lineups more weeks than not.

    213. DEF New England Patriots DST, New England Patriots

    New England allowed the fourth-fewest points last season but barely cracked the top 10 among fantasy defenses. Their defense and special teams combined for five touchdowns, while their 35 sacks ranked seventh-worst in the league. They also recovered as many fumbles as they forced, which is likely to regress.

    New England allowed the fourth-fewest points last season but barely cracked the top 10 among fantasy defenses. Their defense and special teams combined for five touchdowns, while their 35 sacks ranked seventh-worst in the league. They also recovered as many fumbles as they forced, which is likely to regress.

    New England should be a situational start in fantasy this season. Edge rushers Dre'Mont Jones and Harold Landry III are fine role players, but both have pass-rush grades below 70.0 over the past three seasons and are at an age where significant improvement is unlikely.

    With more losses than additions, expect a little regression.

    214. DEF Chicago Bears DST, Chicago Bears

    The Bears were an above-average fantasy defense last season, finishing below average in points allowed but making up for it with a league-leading 23 interceptions.

    Chicago's defense will look very different this season, with four players who logged more than 950 snaps no longer on the roster: linebacker Tremaine Edmunds, cornerback Nahshon Wright and safeties Jaquan Brisker and Kevin Byard III. All four posted PFF grades between 64.0 and 76.0 last season. Another five players with at least 225 snaps also departed, though each graded below 63.0.

    The Bears mostly found suitable replacements or upgrades. Linebacker Devin Bush posted an 87.6 defensive grade last season, safety Coby Bryant reached 70.3, and Chicago spent its first-round pick on safety Dillon Thieneman. Cornerbacks Jaylon Johnson and Kyler Gordon both missed time with injuries last season, and their health will be key to this unit improving.

    Chicago should be a defense to start in the right matchups, but with a wide range of outcomes given all the moving parts. This group could become a weekly must-start, or it could be one to avoid altogether.

    215. DEF Jacksonville Jaguars DST, Jacksonville Jaguars

    The Jaguars ranked third in fantasy points last season. They scored four touchdowns on defense and special teams, and their 22 interceptions ranked second in the league.

    Jacksonville lost two notable starters this offseason. Linebacker Devin Lloyd and his 87.9 grade left for the Carolina Panthers, and safety Andrew Wingard signed with the Arizona Cardinals. Lloyd accounted for five of their interceptions, which was tied for the most among linebackers. There were no notable free-agent additions, just some minor depth changes along the defensive line.

    That will likely lead to a small step back. It's worth noting, though, that Travis Hunter is expected to be healthy for the start of the season and to contribute more on defense than he did as a rookie. The Jaguars are one of several defenses to start only when the matchup is right.

    216. DEF Green Bay Packers DST, Green Bay Packers

    Green Bay's defense ranked 24th last season due to a lack of big plays. They managed just seven interceptions and seven forced fumbles, with no safeties or defensive touchdowns. They did rank 11th in points allowed, so there should be some positive regression in those big plays.

    The Packers lost several defenders, including defensive tackle Colby Wooden, edge rushers Rashan Gary and Kingsley Enagbare, linebacker Quay Walker and cornerback Nate Hobbs. Gary was the only one of that group with a PFF defensive grade above 62.0 last season.

    In their place, Green Bay added linebacker Zaire Franklin, defensive tackle Javon Hargrave and cornerback Benjamin St-Juste, then spent a second-round pick on cornerback Brandon Cisse, a third-round pick on defensive tackle Chris McClellan and a fourth-rounder on Dani Dennis-Sutton.

    The real reason for optimism is youth. Eleven of the top 15 players on the depth chart are 27 or younger, so most of this group should improve rather than decline. However, there is concern around Micah Parsons, who is expected to start the season on the PUP list. Ideally, he will be fully healthy by midseason, but there is no guarantee.

    217. TE Gunnar Helm, Tennessee Titans

    Helm was a fourth-round pick in 2025, but he immediately took over as the second tight end option in Tennessee, playing ahead of Josh Whyle throughout the preseason and eventually leading to Whyle's release. He was on the field for 100% of 12 and 13 personnel snaps throughout his rookie season. He rotated in 11 personnel early in the year, though his time there grew as the season progressed. He topped 55% of snaps just once over the first 10 weeks, then did so six times over the final eight.

    He didn't have a clear breakout game but caught at least four passes in six of them. His 69.3 receiving grade ranked 17th among tight ends with at least 50 targets, while his 3.7 yards after the catch per reception was fourth-lowest in that group.

    A lot has changed in Tennessee over the offseason, starting with new offensive coordinator Brian Daboll. The Titans' other primary tight end from last season, Chig Okonkwo, left in free agency. Daniel Bellinger followed Daboll from the New York Giants to Tennessee, and he will be Helm's main competition for playing time.

    Helm is widely expected to be the team's top receiving tight end, but Bellinger should see plenty of snaps as well. Ideally, Cam Ward also takes a step forward in his second season. The bigger issue is competition for targets, as the Titans added two wide receivers to the top of their depth chart in Carnell Tate and Wan'Dale Robinson.

    He should only be considered in deeper leagues, given how unproven he and the rest of the offense are. If you are drafting purely for upside, though, he is a fine choice over a veteran tight end, as the odds of a breakout are higher here.

    218. QB Jacoby Brissett, Arizona Cardinals

    Brissett has spent his career on the borderline between NFL starter and top backup. He was the primary starter for the Indianapolis Colts in 2017 and 2019, the Cleveland Browns in 2022, and the Cardinals last season, taking over for Kyler Murray. His passing grade has generally improved over time, sitting between 53.0 and 63.0 over his first five seasons before jumping to 69.0 to 79.0 in four of the last five.

    Looking only at seasons in which he started, he finished 25th, 24th and 21st in fantasy points per game in his first three, then leapt to ninth at 19.6 last season. The big reason was volume. He attempted 484 passes from Week 6 through the end of the season, 46 more than any other quarterback, and threw at least 36 times in nine of his 12 starts. Arizona lost each of those nine games, typically by multiple scores, and he had exactly 31 attempts in his other three starts.

    The biggest reason to expect a drop in volume is an improved run game. Brissett made his first start last season in Week 6, by which point James Conner and Trey Benson were both out for the year with injuries. Arizona drafted Jeremiah Love, signed Tyler Allgeier, and should have Conner and Benson back, giving it four backs the team values more highly than any Brissett played with last season. That should push Arizona to run more often.

    The Cardinals allowed the fourth-most points and had the third-lowest PFF defense grade last season. Despite that, they focused on offense in the draft and made few free-agent additions, largely relying on their existing roster. So even if Arizona wants to run more, game scripts could still force plenty of late-game passing.

    That makes Brissett at least an option in superflex leagues in the short term. The biggest issue is that Arizona drafted Carson Beck in the third round. If the team is out of the playoff race late in the season, it will likely turn to Beck to see what it has ahead of 2027. Anyone drafting Brissett should realize they might not have him during the fantasy playoffs.

    Tier 10

    219. QB Cam Ward, Tennessee Titans

    Ward was the first overall pick by the Titans and stepped immediately into the starting lineup. He was expected to have a trio of veteran receivers at his disposal in Calvin Ridley, Tyler Lockett and Van Jefferson, but Ridley played only five full games, catching five passes for 131 yards and two touchdowns in his last healthy one. Head coach Brian Callahan was fired after the sixth game of the season, and Lockett lasted seven games before requesting his release.

    That understandably put a damper on Ward's rookie season. The good news is he threw for two touchdowns in each of his final four full games after managing just seven across his first 12. He similarly posted his three best PFF grades near the end of the season.

    Ward will have a fresh slate this season, with more options. Tennessee drafted Carnell Tate fourth overall to be its new X receiver and signed Wan'Dale Robinson to play the slot. Ridley is also expected to be healthy, shifting from the clear top option to the third read in the passing game. Brian Daboll is the new offensive coordinator.

    In single-quarterback leagues, Ward isn't worth drafting in most redraft formats, but he could easily become a top waiver-wire target with a strong start. In superflex leagues, there are a few veterans he should go ahead of, simply for his upside in a new opportunity.

    220. TE Cade Otton, Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    Otton has been one of the most dependable tight ends in terms of playing time over the last three seasons. He played 96.5% of offensive snaps in 2023, 92.4% in 2024 and 93.2% in 2025. Tampa Bay typically spends a late-round pick on a tight end and did so again this year, so we can expect him to dominate snaps once more. He also signed a three-year, $30 million contract this offseason, which points in the same direction.

    His production, however, has largely depended on the health of the wide receivers around him. Over the last two seasons, he averaged 5.9 receptions, 53.3 yards and 0.3 touchdowns in the nine games where Mike Evans and Chris Godwin both missed time or played under 50% of snaps due to injury. He averaged 3.1 receptions, 32.9 yards and 0.1 touchdowns in every other game. That is the difference between 13.2 PPR points per game and 7.0.

    The one reason for optimism is new offensive coordinator Zac Robinson, who helped Kyle Pitts reach new heights as a receiver last season. This roster is much deeper at wide receiver than his Atlanta Falcons were, though. Evans is gone, but the Buccaneers have a young core around Godwin that includes Emeka Egbuka, Jalen McMillan, Tez Johnson and Ted Hurst.

    He is best left on the waiver wire in most leagues, though he becomes a reasonable add if injuries start piling up in that receiver room.

    221. TE Mike Gesicki, Cincinnati Bengals

    Gesicki ranked 13th among tight ends in fantasy points in 2024, his first season with the Cincinnati Bengals. He had a few spectacular games, highlighted by a 100-yard, two-touchdown performance against the Kansas City Chiefs, and added four more with at least seven receptions and 65 yards. Three of those five big outings came while Tee Higgins was out due to injury. He also had four games with 2 or fewer yards and another five with three or fewer receptions.

    He was unable to replicate that success in 2025. He was held to 20 yards or fewer over the first five weeks, then a pectoral strain cost him the middle of the season. He found a little more upon his return, including six catches for 86 yards and a touchdown against the Buffalo Bills. Higgins again missed time late in the year, but Gesicki did not take advantage the way he had in 2024.

    Cincinnati rotates its tight ends more than most teams. A receiving tight end usually leads his position group in offensive snaps given how much teams throw, but Gesicki is not always the second tight end in 12 personnel and is not always on the field on third downs, where Drew Sample has at times played for his blocking. If anything, the snaps could thin further with Erick All back.

    Gesicki is best left on the waiver wire in most leagues, though he ranks ahead of a few other lead receiving tight ends. He catches passes from Joe Burrow, and he has posted the kind of big games some tight ends never reach.

    222. WR Jalen Tolbert, Miami Dolphins

    Tolbert was a third-round pick by the Cowboys in 2022. He was initially thought to be their potential third receiver, but Noah Brown instead became a top-three receiver for the first time in his career, and Tolbert caught just two passes as a rookie. He eventually worked his way up to third on the depth chart, first behind CeeDee Lamb and Brandin Cooks, then behind Lamb and George Pickens after Pickens replaced Cooks. His best season was 2024, in part because of an injury to Cooks, when he caught 49 passes for 610 yards and seven touchdowns and finished 45th in fantasy points per game.

    KaVontae Turpin had always complicated Tolbert's role as the third receiver, and in 2025, Ryan Flournoy overtook him on the depth chart. Tolbert then left for the Miami Dolphins in free agency. Miami moved on from Tyreek Hill and Jaylen Waddle, leaving Malik Washington as the only notable holdover from last season and added Tutu Atwell in free agency while drafting Caleb Douglas, Chris Bell and Kevin Coleman Jr.

    Training camp and the preseason will go a long way toward determining how much each of those six receivers plays. After OTAs, Omar Kelly noted that Tolbert had put some distance between himself and the rest of the group, and that he was playing the X role, likely also the natural spot for Douglas and Bell. If that holds, Atwell could play the Z and Washington the slot. If Tolbert isn't playing well enough early in the season, Miami could turn to a rookie, though third-round rookie receivers don't often crack the starting lineup.

    A lot could change before the season starts, but for now, Tolbert is the top Dolphins receiver to target. Generally, any team's top receiver is worth at least flex consideration.

    224. WR Germie Bernard, Pittsburgh Steelers

    Bernard is a well-rounded receiver. There is no area of his game where he would be considered the best in the 2026 draft class, but there are also no obvious weaknesses. He is capable of playing all three receiver spots, making him a versatile chess piece for an offense.

    He was the 47th overall pick by the Steelers, a team that desperately needed receiver help last season. D.K. Metcalf remains atop the depth chart, but Pittsburgh did not retain Calvin Austin III, Adam Thielen, Marquez Valdes-Scantling or Scotty Miller. The Steelers then traded for Michael Pittman Jr. to serve as their second option.

    Bernard will compete with Roman Wilson for the third receiver spot. Wilson missed nearly all of his rookie season in 2024 with an injury and managed only 12 receptions last year. Fantasy managers tend to expect Day 2 rookies to immediately leapfrog veterans, but that is not always how it goes. It took Luther Burden III half of last season to pass Olamide Zaccheaus, Jayden Higgins never fully surpassed Xavier Hutchinson, and both Tre Harris and Jack Bech spent most of last season fourth on their depth charts.

    Bernard is a speculative late-round pick in deeper leagues until he shows he has locked down the third receiver job. Even then, he is unlikely to see enough volume to matter for fantasy barring an injury to Metcalf or Pittman. His long-term value is stronger, since it is unclear whether either veteran is part of Pittsburgh's future.

    226. RB Samaje Perine, Cincinnati Bengals

    Perine is the third-down back for the Bengals. He spent time with the team from 2020 to 2022, handling third downs behind Joe Mixon, then played a year with the Denver Broncos and another with the Kansas City Chiefs before returning to Cincinnati in 2025.

    He has typically been a third-down specialist, but he saw more early-down work as last season progressed, partly because of early struggles from starter Chase Brown. He had two late-season games with 14 carries, both against the Baltimore Ravens, one a blowout win and the other a blowout loss.

    Perine is unlikely to hold much standalone value unless Cincinnati is blowing out opponents, but he could carry handcuff appeal. The Bengals' only other notable back is Tahj Brooks, a 2025 sixth-round pick who saw offensive snaps last season only when one of the other two backs was hurt. There is a chance Brooks takes a step forward in year two while Perine slips given his age, though Perine would still likely be the lead back if Brown were to get injured.

    That makes him a late-round option in deeper leagues where most backup running backs are rostered, given the quality of the Cincinnati offense and the trust the staff has shown in him.

    232. TE Oronde Gadsden, Los Angeles Chargers

    Gadsden was a fifth-round pick by the Chargers. He drew more hype from coaches and beat writers than a typical late-round pick throughout training camp, but opened the season as a healthy inactive for the first two weeks. Will Dissly and Tyler Conklin were the top two tight ends on the depth chart, with Tucker Fisk mixed in as a run blocker. An injury to Dissly opened up playing time, and in his first game Gadsden caught five passes for 46 yards on just 22 snaps.

    In Week 6, he reached 75% of snaps for the first time and caught seven passes for 68 yards. He followed with seven catches for 164 yards and a touchdown the next week, then two more strong games right after. No tight end scored more fantasy points than Gadsden from Weeks 6 to 9. He then hit a rookie wall, with just one more game over 45 receiving yards and one more touchdown the rest of the way.

    The good news is the Chargers have a new offensive coordinator in Mike McDaniel, whose offense is more pass-heavy than predecessor Greg Roman's. His attack with the Miami Dolphins was built on speed, and tracking data shows Gadsden as one of the faster tight ends in the league.

    The bad news is that Los Angeles made notable additions at the position. Early in the offseason, the team signed Charlie Kolar to a three-year, $24.3 million contract. He is known for his run blocking, but his 79.3 receiving grade over the past two seasons ranks ninth among tight ends with at least 25 targets. Kolar has exactly 25. Los Angeles also signed David Njoku, who has more than 4,000 career receiving yards with the Cleveland Browns.

    That means Gadsden is unlikely to see the playing time fantasy managers would want this year, but it doesn't end his chances of being relevant. Jonnu Smith spent one season in McDaniel's offense, playing just 55% of the snaps yet finishing as the TE4. If Kolar handles the run blocking, Njoku plays a hybrid role and Gadsden takes the receiving work, he could still break out.

    He remains an intriguing dynasty option, especially for anyone willing to prioritize the future over this season. In redraft, he is only a late-round target.
