    233. DEF Minnesota Vikings DST, Minnesota Vikings

    The Vikings' defense ranked fifth in fantasy points last season, but is in store for some regression. Minnesota led the league with 13 fumble recoveries and scored two touchdowns, both of which are volatile year to year.

    They lost four players who logged more than 500 snaps last season: defensive tackles Jonathan Allen and Javon Hargrave, edge rusher Jonathan Greenard and longtime safety Harrison Smith, although he hasn't retired yet. Their only notable free-agent addition was James Pierre, who posted a solid 75.1 defensive grade on 643 snaps over the past three seasons.

    Minnesota leaned heavily on defense in the draft, taking four defenders across the first two days, led by first-round defensive lineman Caleb Banks. That youth movement should help the Vikings long term, but there could be a slight step back early in the season, depending on how quickly the rookies develop.

    234. DEF Kansas City Chiefs DST, Kansas City Chiefs

    The Chiefs were an interesting defense from a fantasy perspective. They allowed the sixth-fewest points last season at 328, but forced a league-low six fumbles, scored no touchdowns or safeties and recorded a below-average 35 sacks. That added up to the 22nd-ranked fantasy defense.

    Kansas City suffered significant losses this offseason. Jaylen Watson, Bryan Cook and Trent McDuffie all left after posting PFF defensive grades of at least 74.0 last season. The Chiefs also lost five players in the front seven who each played at least 300 snaps.

    Kansas City will turn largely to rookies to fill those gaps, having selected three defenders inside the first 40 picks. If this group can generate more big plays, that would be enough to lift its fantasy value, but this remains a defense to start only in the right matchups.

    237. DEF Atlanta Falcons DST, Atlanta Falcons

    The Falcons had the 11th-best fantasy defense last season thanks to big plays. They finished second in sacks with 57 and sixth in interceptions with 16. However, they were below average in points allowed.

    Atlanta lost two key pieces in the front seven in Kaden Elliss and David Onyemata. Both posted defensive grades above 75.0 over the past three seasons. The Falcons added several players from other teams, but none logged more than 500 snaps last season or graded above 70.0.

    There is also concern that James Pearce Jr. will receive a significant suspension following his arrest on domestic violence-related charges. The 2025 first-round pick earned just a 56.1 defensive grade last season. If he is suspended, it mainly means Atlanta's pass rush won't have the same opportunity to improve, though the unit wouldn't necessarily take a step back without him.

    Those veteran losses will likely lead to some regression, turning Atlanta from a likely start into a situational one.

    238. DEF Cleveland Browns DST, Cleveland Browns

    Cleveland had the eighth-ranked fantasy defense last season. Their 53 sacks ranked third, while their four defensive touchdowns tied for the league lead. They were average in points allowed and most other metrics.

    The big concern is that they lost their two best players. Myles Garrett, who earned a 93.0 grade last season, was traded to the Los Angeles Rams. Linebacker Devin Bush, who posted an 87.6 grade, signed with the Chicago Bears. No one else on the defense graded above 80.0.

    Cleveland received Jared Verse in the Garrett trade, and his very respectable 82.2 grade makes him the Browns' new top defender. They also added Quincy Williams from the New York Jets, who has graded well in the past but is coming off a down 2025 season.

    Those losses will likely leave Cleveland as a below-average defense, one worth starting only in the right matchup.

    243. QB Fernando Mendoza, Las Vegas Raiders

    Typically, quarterbacks taken first overall land higher in redraft and dynasty rookie rankings, but a few factors push Mendoza below the usual No. 1 pick.

    Mendoza joins the Raiders, who posted the third-lowest team offensive grade last season. Ideally, they will have left tackle Kolton Miller back at full strength and will add reinforcements to the receiver room and offensive line this offseason. The better the Raiders look, the more he could climb these rankings.

    He also lacks the rushing upside of most recent first-round quarterbacks. Based on PFF tracking data, he ranks among the bottom three in athleticism and top speed among first-round quarterbacks over the past three seasons, alongside Bryce Young and Cam Ward. Both of them averaged fewer than 16 rushing yards per game as rookies, a sign of how little Mendoza is likely to add on the ground. His 69.3 career PFF rushing grade ranks ahead of only Young, C.J. Stroud, Michael Penix Jr. and Kenny Pickett, so all three of these low-athleticism passers grade poorly as runners.

    Mendoza joins the Raiders, who posted the third-lowest team offensive grade last season. Ideally, they will have left tackle Kolton Miller back at full strength and will add reinforcements to the receiver room and offensive line this offseason. The better the Raiders look, the more he could climb these rankings.

    Together, these factors make Mendoza riskier than a typical first-overall quarterback. The Raiders have also seemed committed to not starting him right away, with Kirk Cousins consistently taking the first-team reps in OTAs. If Cousins makes them a playoff contender, we might not see Mendoza at all this season.

    Together, these factors make Mendoza riskier than a typical first-overall quarterback. The Raiders have also seemed committed to not starting him right away, with Kirk Cousins consistently taking the first-team reps in OTAs. If Cousins makes them a playoff contender, we might not see Mendoza at all this season.

    245. RB Demond Claiborne, Minnesota Vikings

    Claiborne is a small, speedy running back with great change-of-direction ability. Our draft guide compared him to Justice Hill and mentioned his upside as a receiving running back. He is not expected to become a three-down back, but there are plenty of receiving specialists at the position who have found fantasy value. For example, Kenneth Gainwell finished last season as RB16 while ranking fourth in receptions (73) and 47th in rushing attempts (114).

    Claiborne landed with the Vikings in the sixth round, where he will likely start his career behind Aaron Jones and Jordan Mason on the depth chart. Mason has served more of an early-down role throughout his career, while Jones has played on third downs, and significantly on early downs as well, making Jones his primary competition.

    The 31-year-old running back accepted a pay cut to remain with the Vikings, and he has missed significant time in two of the last three seasons. His rushing efficiency and receiving production have both declined in recent years. There is a reasonable chance Claiborne will be the Vikings' lead receiving back at some point this season.

    In that scenario, chances are Claiborne would only take part of Jones' playing time, with Mason also taking a larger role. This makes it unlikely Claiborne will be fantasy viable, even in the scenario of a Jones injury. However, neither Jones nor Mason is under contract in 2027. The better Claiborne plays this season, the larger his role can be in 2027. He is a fine dynasty stash, but should only be drafted in deep redraft leagues.

    253. QB Bryce Young, Carolina Panthers

    Young has spent the past three seasons as the Panthers' starting quarterback after being the first overall pick of the 2023 draft. He had a rookie year to forget and was briefly benched in 2024 before returning and ending the season with a 75.9 PFF offensive grade. He regressed slightly in 2025 to a 71.0 grade, but better weapons around him led to better fantasy production.

    The Panthers drafted Tetairoa McMillan eighth overall, and he matched Adam Thielen's production from Young's rookie season. Jalen Coker emerged as the best second option Young has had in his young career, and Rico Dowdle put up the most receiving yards by a Panthers running back in three seasons.

    All of that added up to the 28th-most fantasy points per game among quarterbacks last season. The starters below him either lost their jobs, are competing for jobs, changed teams or were Cam Ward as a rookie.

    Carolina made minimal changes to the offense this offseason. Jonathan Brooks replaces Dowdle in the backfield, first-round pick Monroe Freeling may not contribute much as a rookie, and the team added a little competition for Xavier Legette at the third receiver spot.

    Carolina made minimal changes to the offense this offseason. Jonathan Brooks replaces Dowdle in the backfield, first-round pick Monroe Freeling may not contribute much as a rookie, and the team added a little competition for Xavier Legette at the third receiver spot.

    Young is worth drafting in superflex leagues, since all starting quarterbacks are, but there's little reason to take him in a single-quarterback redraft league unless the benches are very deep.

    263. QB Aaron Rodgers, Pittsburgh Steelers

    Rodgers was once a top NFL quarterback, with four MVPs and several excellent fantasy seasons. Those days are behind him, though. He averaged 14.8 points per game in his final season in Green Bay, missed nearly all of 2023, averaged 15.7 in his one full season with the New York Jets, and 14.6 in his first season with the Pittsburgh Steelers.

    Nearly every starting quarterback he beat in fantasy production last season has either changed teams, is fighting for a starting job, or is significantly younger with room to improve.

    The good news is Rodgers has reunited with Mike McCarthy, his longtime coach in Green Bay during his peak. Pittsburgh also invested in the receiver room, adding Michael Pittman Jr. and Germie Bernard.

    The good news is Rodgers has reunited with Mike McCarthy, his longtime coach in Green Bay during his peak. Pittsburgh also invested in the receiver room, adding Michael Pittman Jr. and Germie Bernard.

    Rodgers is worth considering in superflex leagues only, where there is a chance he can be a consistent second quarterback. But there is also a chance his play keeps sliding, in which case he might not be worth starting even in a typical superflex week.

    267. RB Malik Davis, Dallas Cowboys

    Davis was an undrafted rookie in 2022 and slowly but surely advanced from practice-squad player to backup running back over the second half of last season. He was effective when given an opportunity, running 52 times for 250 yards and two touchdowns, though he never started a game.

    Davis is competing with Jaydon Blue and Phil Mafah for the backup job behind Javonte Williams. Davis appeared to be the frontrunner at the beginning of June, but head coach Brian Schottenheimer said, "We're really excited about the young guys, with Jaydon Blue and Phil Mafah. We know what Malik Davis is."

    At best, Davis will be the handcuff to Williams, but if both second-year backs can live up to the coaching staff's hopes, then Davis might not make the roster. He is a fine player to stash in deeper leagues to see how the running back competition plays out.

    273. QB Geno Smith, New York Jets

    Smith spent two years as a starter for the New York Jets, followed by seven seasons as a backup. In 2022, he took over as the Seattle Seahawks' starter and held the job for three years. He followed that with one season in Las Vegas, and now he is back with the Jets as their starter.

    Smith played well during his three seasons in Seattle, posting an 88.8 offensive grade. He ranked fifth in fantasy points in 2022, although his 18.5 points per game ranked only eighth. He fell to 24th in points per game in 2023 and 15th in 2024. The 2023 dropoff was simply fewer yards per game, along with a slight decline in passing efficiency and fewer rushing attempts.

    Smith played well during his three seasons in Seattle, posting an 88.8 offensive grade. He ranked fifth in fantasy points in 2022, although his 18.5 points per game ranked only eighth. He fell to 24th in points per game in 2023 and 15th in 2024. The 2023 dropoff was simply fewer yards per game, along with a slight decline in passing efficiency and fewer rushing attempts.

    In New York, his passing weapons will be Garrett Wilson, Adonai Mitchell and rookies Omar Cooper Jr. and Kenyon Sadiq. That is better than what he had in Las Vegas, but a step down from D.K. Metcalf and Tyler Lockett at their best, unless the rookies contribute immediately.

    Smith will be 36 before the end of the season. It's unlikely his rushing will match the peak of his career, and unless the offense plays much better than expected, his passing volume will be hard-pressed to reach his Seattle levels. He is a quarterback to avoid in redraft leagues and a safe, low-end QB2 in superflex.

    283. QB Tua Tagovailoa, Atlanta Falcons

    Tagovailoa spent the first six seasons of his career as the lead quarterback of the Miami Dolphins. He earned an 81.6 passing grade over that span, which ranked 20th among quarterbacks. He has offered minimal rushing production, so his 81.3 offensive grade ranks 26th. He has averaged 6.1 rushing yards per game and hasn't scored a rushing touchdown since 2021.

    His peak fantasy season was 2022, when he tied for ninth in fantasy points per game at 18.4. He ranked 13th in 2024 at 17.1 and was a low-end QB2 at best in the other four seasons. He was elevated in Miami by having Tyreek Hill and Jaylen Waddle to throw to.

    Miami decided to move on, leaving Tagovailoa with the Atlanta Falcons. Atlanta drafted Michael Penix Jr. with the eighth overall pick in 2024, and he is recovering from a partially torn ACL. The two are competing for the starting job. The Falcons likely want Penix to win long term, given how much they invested in him, but Tagovailoa is the favorite for Week 1.

    Tagovailoa has strong weapons in Atlanta with Drake London and Kyle Pitts. However, Kirk Cousins had those same weapons over the final seven weeks last season and averaged just 14.0 fantasy points per game.

    Tagovailoa is a QB2 option in superflex leagues, but only for as long as he holds the starting job.

    288. WR Antonio Williams, Washington Commanders

    Williams was a third-round pick by the Washington Commanders. Our draft guide called him "a quick, precise slot-only receiver with strong separation metrics but a low average depth of target." His PFF receiving grade improved each season and reached a solid 80.3 in his final year at Clemson.

    Typically, a player of his profile doesn't lead to a lot of fantasy success. He measures at 5'11", 190 pounds. Zay Flowers and Jaylen Waddle have been consistent fantasy starters at smaller sizes, but most other wide receivers of a similar size, like Josh Downs, Wan'Dale Robinson and Jayden Reed, have typically been stuck in 11 personnel only. Washington was right around league average in how often they used 11 personnel last season. They have a new offensive coordinator in David Blough, but he was on the coaching staff each of the last two seasons.

    Williams joins a particularly crowded wide receiver room, where the only sure thing is that Terry McLaurin is one of the starting wide receivers. Washington has spent a middle-round draft pick on a slot receiver in each of the last three seasons. Williams appears to be the favorite over Jaylin Lane and Luke McCaffrey. Washington kept Treylon Burks from last season while adding Dyami Brown and Van Jefferson. Washington is also expected to add Brandon Aiyuk when he becomes available.

    The big question is when and if Williams will receive opportunities as an outside receiver, or if he will be stuck in the slot. When he was first drafted, head coach Dan Quinn brought up his experience and ability to play both outside and in the slot. During the start of OTAs, Washington kept him mostly in the slot.

    Williams is the only other wide receiver outside of McLaurin that fantasy managers should consider this season because of his upside. Ideally, he will be given opportunities outside of the slot in training camp and the preseason. If that happens, he will become everyone's favorite sleeper wide receiver until his ADP rises. If he doesn't get these opportunities by Week 1, it's unlikely his role will grow to include those snaps during the season.

    292. QB Shedeur Sanders, Cleveland Browns

    Sanders was a fifth-round pick by the Browns in 2025, after significant speculation he could go among the top few picks of the draft. Cleveland gave him a chance to start over the final seven weeks of the season.

    He averaged 13.2 fantasy points per game over that stretch. Part of the problem was volume, as he threw 25 times or fewer in four games, including three victories. Efficiency was an issue too. His 45.5 offensive grade was the lowest among all quarterbacks with at least 300 snaps last season.

    The good news is that Cleveland has invested heavily in the offense. The Browns used one first-round pick on wide receiver KC Concepcion and a second-rounder on Denzel Boston, and they completely reworked the offensive line. If Sanders is the starter, we can expect improved production. With Myles Garrett gone, Cleveland may also need to throw more often. All of that points to more fantasy value, potentially pushing Sanders into QB2 territory in superflex leagues.

    The problem is that the starting job isn't assured. He is currently competing with Deshaun Watson, who missed all of last season recovering from two Achilles surgeries. The Browns also have two first-round picks next year and will likely draft a quarterback unless Sanders or Watson works out in a big way. Whoever wins the job in Week 1 might not hold it into the second half if the team isn't winning. That makes Sanders harder to draft than the 30 or so quarterbacks with clearer paths to starting.

    293. DEF Detroit Lions DST, Detroit Lions

    The Lions' defense struggled as a unit last season, allowing 413 points to rank 22nd in the league. From a fantasy perspective they fared better, thanks to a pass rush led by Aidan Hutchinson. Their 49 sacks tied for the fourth-most among defenses.

    The Lions' defense struggled as a unit last season, allowing 413 points to rank 22nd in the league. From a fantasy perspective they fared better, thanks to a pass rush led by Aidan Hutchinson. Their 49 sacks tied for the fourth-most among defenses.

    The Lions added edge rusher D.J. Wonnum and drafted Derrick Moore in the second round, but they will largely rely on returning players to fill the void.

    Safeties Kerby Joseph and Brian Branch both failed to finish last season, and their health will be another factor in whether this defense bounces back.

    294. DEF Buffalo Bills DST, Buffalo Bills

    The Bills had a relatively average defense last season, finishing above average in points allowed but making fewer big plays than a typical unit.

    Nine players who logged at least 200 snaps for Buffalo last season are not currently on an NFL roster, and eight of them are 31 or older. That group most notably includes edge rusher Joey Bosa and defensive tackle DaQuan Jones, both of whom posted defensive grades above 70.0.

    Buffalo made several free-agent additions, but none of them graded above 60.0 last season. The Bills also spent both second-round picks on defense, taking edge rusher T.J. Parker and cornerback Davison Igbinosun. Even so, they will lean more heavily on their 2025 class, as cornerback Maxwell Hairston, defensive tackle T.J. Sanders and edge rusher Landon Jackson each played more than 500 snaps as rookies.

    Buffalo is a defense to avoid early in the season until we see how those young players develop. If the Bills have been drafting well, this unit could be significantly improved.

    295. DEF Tampa Bay Buccaneers DST, Tampa Bay Buccaneers

    Tampa Bay's defense was around league average in every major metric, and we should expect more of the same this season.

    Their biggest loss was cornerback Jamel Dean, who signed with the Pittsburgh Steelers after posting an 80.7 defensive grade last season. The Buccaneers spent two Day 2 picks on cornerbacks a year ago, and those two will be expected to help make up for his departure.

    Tampa Bay also lost longtime linebacker Lavonte David to retirement, along with three players from the front-four rotation. They replaced that depth with veterans and 15th overall pick Rueben Bain Jr. Expect an improved front seven but a decline in the secondary.

    296. DEF San Francisco 49ers DST, San Francisco 49ers

    San Francisco had the seventh-worst fantasy defense last season. They finished a little above average in points allowed but posted a league-low 20 sacks.

    This unit is relying heavily on how its stars recover from injury. Defensive end Nick Bosa, linebacker Fred Warner and free-agent addition Dre Greenlaw are their three highest-graded players over the past three seasons, but none played more than 400 snaps last season due to injury.

    The 49ers lost several contributors this offseason, with five players who logged at least 500 snaps for them last season no longer on the roster. They also brought in some experience, including two Miami Dolphins defensive backs in Jack Jones and Ashtyn Davis.

    San Francisco's return to the league's elite hinges not just on those injured players recovering, but on its young ones. Six defenders drafted in the first four rounds of 2024 and 2025 are expected to have large roles this season, and none has a career PFF grade above 64.0 yet. Their collective improvement will be necessary for this defense to succeed.

    301. DEF Indianapolis Colts DST, Indianapolis Colts

    The Colts' defense was the definition of average last season, so they made several changes this offseason. Nineteen different players who took a defensive snap for Indianapolis last season are no longer on the roster.

    That includes a trio of starters in defensive end Kwity Paye, linebacker Zaire Franklin and safety Nick Cross, although all three graded below 60.0 last season. Longtime nickel cornerback Kenny Moore II was also let go. He posted a 66.7 grade last season but is 32 years old.

    Indianapolis added eight defenders from other teams who played at least 250 snaps last season, and none of them graded above 64.0. The Colts also drafted linebacker CJ Allen in the second round and safety AJ Haulcy in the third, and both are contenders to start.

    The biggest reason for optimism is a full season of Sauce Gardner. Indianapolis traded for him midseason but got only two full games out of him before an injury in his third, followed by a limited role in his Week 17 return. Even so, the Colts should be an average defense again this year.

    315. WR Malachi Fields, New York Giants

    Fields is a 6'4", 222-pound X receiver who was drafted with the 74th overall pick by the Giants. Per our draft guide, he has excellent hands, with great blocking and toughness, which fit a John Harbaugh offense. His grades were consistently decent throughout college, going from a 72.7 receiving grade in 2023 to 75.8 in 2025. While most X receivers excel against man, Fields performed better against zone defenses.

    Fields joins a crowded wide receiver room in New York. Malik Nabers and Darius Slayton return to the team, while New York added Darnell Mooney, Calvin Austin III, JuJu Smith-Schuster and Odell Beckham Jr. in free agency. Three of the four free agent additions had worked with either new head coach John Harbaugh or new offensive coordinator Matt Nagy in a previous season.

    His primary competition for playing time will likely come from Slayton, who has been the Giants' X receiver in recent seasons. If Beckham makes the roster, that also would not be a good sign for Fields' playing time as a rookie.

    If Nabers is able to play early in the season, it will be difficult to trust any of the other Giants receivers, but if any one of them were to break out, Fields would have the most upside. If Nabers misses time, then Mooney or Austin would likely benefit the most, and the impact on Fields would be minimal. The most likely scenario is that Fields spends his rookie season as a rotational backup.

    317. DEF New Orleans Saints DST, New Orleans Saints

    The Saints defense ranked 12th in fantasy points last season. They were one of five teams with at least three defensive touchdowns, and their 10 fumble recoveries tied for the fourth-most. However, they were only average in points allowed against one of the most favorable schedules in the league.

    New Orleans lost longtime linebacker Demario Davis, who posted an 89.2 defensive grade over the past three seasons. They also lost starting cornerback Alontae Taylor to the Tennessee Titans.

    The Saints replaced Davis with Kaden Elliss from division rival Atlanta. His 75.4 grade over the past three seasons is respectable for a starter but not at Davis' level. New Orleans will largely rely on its 2025 mid-round picks to replace Taylor.

    Even if this defense were unchanged, some regression would be expected given how it generated its fantasy points. The offseason changes make a step back more likely than a step forward.

    333. DEF Carolina Panthers DST, Carolina Panthers

    The Panthers defense was generally average last season, ranking high in interceptions with 15 but low in sacks with 30, tied for third-worst.

    They lost three notable players in the front seven in A'Shawn Robinson, DJ Wonnum and Christian Rozeboom. Carolina notably upgraded at two positions with veterans, adding linebacker Devin Lloyd and his 87.9 grade last season, along with edge rusher Jaelan Phillips and his 78.4 grade over the past three seasons. Robinson was replaced by second-round rookie Lee Hunter.

    While the defense might be better, the schedule is more difficult. The Panthers have the sixth-worst schedule for defenses, according to our strength of schedule tool. There might only be a few weeks where it makes sense to put Carolina in a fantasy starting lineup in a standard 12-team league, even if they finish among the top 20 most weeks.

    347. DEF Cincinnati Bengals DST, Cincinnati Bengals

    The Bengals allowed the third-most points (492) and second-most yards (6,475) last season. They outperformed that from a fantasy perspective thanks to an above-average 13 interceptions.

    Cincinnati's biggest loss was pass rusher Trey Hendrickson, who missed most of last season with an injury. They lost three other players with at least 300 snaps, but all of them graded at 64.0 or worse. There is little reason to expect this defense to be worse in 2026.

    The Bengals made three notable additions, headlined by Dexter Lawrence II and his 91.9 defensive grade over the past three seasons. Edge rusher Boye Mafe and safety Bryan Cook both posted PFF grades above 70.0 last season and over the past three seasons. An argument could be made that these are the three best players on Cincinnati's defense.

    The Bengals are relatively young outside of those additions and defensive tackle B.J. Hill, so the rest of the unit could also improve with experience. Expect some improvement from the free-agent additions, and this could be an above-average unit if some of the younger players work out.

    362. DEF Dallas Cowboys DST, Dallas Cowboys

    Dallas had the second-worst defense from a fantasy perspective last season. They allowed the most points (511) and third-most yards (6,409). Ten different players who logged at least 200 snaps for the Cowboys last season are no longer on the roster, and the only ones who graded above 70.0 were older edge rushers Jadeveon Clowney and Dante Fowler Jr.

    Dallas had the second-worst defense from a fantasy perspective last season. They allowed the most points (511) and third-most yards (6,409). Ten different players who logged at least 200 snaps for the Cowboys last season are no longer on the roster, and the only ones who graded above 70.0 were older edge rushers Jadeveon Clowney and Dante Fowler Jr.

    Dallas made four solid free-agent additions as well: edge rusher Rashan Gary, linebacker Dee Winters, cornerback Cobie Durant and safety Jalen Thompson. All four are 29 or younger, played at least 650 snaps last season and graded above 60.0.

    The Cowboys defense should be anywhere from a little improved to dramatically improved, making a slightly below-average unit the most likely outcome.

    368. QB Ty Simpson, Los Angeles Rams

    Simpson was a surprise selection for Los Angeles at 13th overall, as many expected him to go late in the first round or early in the second. He started only 15 games in college, beginning that lone season strongly before struggling as it progressed. Our draft breakdown called him a solid starter who ideally would have had another year to improve his decision-making and develop a better feel in the pocket.

    He will get that year, though he'll have to make those gains in practice rather than in games. Matthew Stafford was the NFL's MVP last season and will be the Rams' starter for as long as he is healthy, so Simpson will sit for at least one year. Stafford is 38 and may not be in Los Angeles much beyond 2026, especially if Simpson develops.

    Whenever he does take over, Simpson will be in an excellent situation. Sean McVay is arguably the best coach in the NFL, and the Rams posted by far the highest offensive grade in the league last season at 92.0, even with quarterback play excluded. Simpson would be a borderline fantasy starter in single-quarterback leagues once he wins the job, and a clear one in superflex.

    Simpson has almost no redraft value, as there's a chance Stetson Bennett IV holds the backup job and Simpson focuses entirely on 2027 and beyond. In dynasty superflex leagues, he is worthy of a first-round rookie pick given his upside in the Rams' offense.

    372. TE Eli Stowers, Philadelphia Eagles

    Stowers is an athletic former quarterback who transitioned to tight end and produced at a high level over his final two seasons at Vanderbilt. That included 100-yard games against Texas, Auburn and Alabama, along with two nonconference opponents. His 91.4 career PFF receiving grade is higher than that of recent first-round picks such as Tyler Warren and Colston Loveland, and notably higher than fellow rookie Kenyon Sadiq's. From a testing standpoint, he ranked in the 95th percentile or better in the 40-yard dash, 20-yard shuttle, vertical jump and broad jump.

    The primary concern with Stowers is his size. He was listed at 235 pounds in college and measured 239 pounds at the combine. Historically, tight ends under 240 pounds have rarely made a significant fantasy impact. However, Juwan Johnson, Harold Fannin Jr. and Oronde Gadsden II are all under that threshold and just delivered three of the most productive seasons by players of that size in the past 20 years in terms of receiving yards.

    Stowers joins the Philadelphia Eagles, where he will be Dallas Goedert's backup for one season, although he will have a shot to cut into Goedert's playing time. Goedert took a pay cut to stay with Philadelphia, and he will be 32 by the end of the season. This makes Stowers the likely starter in 2027. Goedert has ranked among the top 12 tight ends in fantasy points per game in each of the last seven seasons, and Stowers could follow in those footsteps and be a fantasy starting tight end for several seasons.

    Expectations should be much lower for Stowers this season. While he should receive some playing time, it wouldn't be surprising if he sees the third-most offensive snaps at the position, with either Grant Calcaterra or Johnny Mundt used for their run blocking. It will either take an injury to Goedert or an amazing camp by Stowers for him to make an impact in fantasy leagues this year. However, if you're in a deep enough league where some backup tight ends are rostered, he should be considered one of the top tight end handcuffs.

    377. DEF Tennessee Titans DST, Tennessee Titans

    The Titans allowed the fifth-most points last season at 478. They ranked a little better from a fantasy perspective, but that was largely due to four defensive and special teams touchdowns.

    Tennessee moved on from several players this offseason. Of the 17 who logged at least 300 snaps last season, only seven remain on the roster. The only players with more than 400 snaps and a PFF grade above 55.0 are defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons and linebacker Cedric Gray.

    The Titans added eight defenders from other teams who played at least 400 snaps last season, most with PFF grades between 61.0 and 66.0. The biggest moves were hiring head coach Robert Saleh and drafting 31st overall pick Keldric Faulk at defensive end and second-round linebacker Anthony Hill Jr.

    It will take time for this defense to start producing with so many new pieces. Until then, Tennessee should be left on the waiver wire.

    383. RB Phil Mafah, Dallas Cowboys

    Mafah was a seventh-round pick in 2025. He was a favorite in dynasty leagues, given the Cowboys' unsettled depth chart at running back heading into the season. Mafah suffered a shoulder injury in the preseason finale, landing him on injured reserve. He returned only for the final week of the season, running five times for 18 yards and a touchdown while catching two passes for 11 yards.

    Mafah is competing for the backup spot behind Javonte Williams with Jaydon Blue and Malik Davis. Dallas gave Blue and Mafah opportunities with the first team, showing Mafah is legitimately part of the competition, but he is not the favorite to win the backup spot.

    He is a fine player to hold onto in dynasty leagues or to pick up in deep leagues to see how the backup battle plays out.

    392. DEF New York Giants DST, New York Giants

    The Giants had the fifth-worst defense by fantasy points last season. Like most units that ranked near the bottom, New York made several changes this offseason.

    Their biggest loss was Dexter Lawrence II, whom they traded for a first-round pick. Linebacker Bobby Okereke, cornerback Cor'Dale Flott and safety Dane Belton all played more than 700 snaps and are no longer on the roster.

    New York added 16 players who took at least one snap for another team last season, though only five are notable. The most significant is defensive tackle DJ Reader, who posted a 76.5 defensive grade over the last three seasons. The Giants also used their fifth overall pick on linebacker Arvell Reese.

    Unless Reese makes an immediate impact, there isn't much reason to expect a significantly better season from this defense. If anything, he could struggle the way most rookies do, and the swap from Lawrence to Reader may prove to be a downgrade.

    408. DEF Miami Dolphins DST, Miami Dolphins

    Miami ranked below average in every major defensive metric last season but was rarely among the bottom five.

    The Dolphins have made several changes, particularly in the secondary. Jack Jones, Rasul Douglas, Minkah Fitzpatrick, Ashtyn Davis and Ifeatu Melifonwu were their top five defensive backs in snaps, and none of them remain on the roster.

    Miami added just one player in free agency who started last season, former Tennessee Titans cornerback Darrell Baker Jr., while the rest of their free-agent additions played 350 snaps or fewer. The Dolphins also drafted cornerback Chris Johnson with the 27th pick.

    The primary reason for optimism is new head coach Jeff Hafley, but it might take time to see notable improvement. This is a defense to avoid at the start of the season.

    422. DEF Washington Commanders DST, Washington Commanders

    No defense allowed more yards than the Commanders last season at 6,533. They also ranked among the bottom six teams in points allowed and fantasy points.

    They had six players with at least 200 snaps last season who are all free agents, and all six are at least 30 years old. That list includes two future Hall of Famers in Bobby Wagner and Von Miller.

    Their biggest addition is rookie linebacker Sonny Styles, taken with the seventh overall pick. Washington also made several free-agent additions. Safety Nick Cross, cornerback Amik Robertson and defensive end K'Lavon Chaisson all played more than 800 snaps last season but graded below 60.0. Linebacker Leo Chenal and defensive tackle Tim Settle both graded in the 70s, though with fewer than 500 snaps each.

    Washington's youth movement on defense should help in the long run, but it might not translate to a better finish in the short term. They are a defense to avoid unless they play better than expected.

    437. DEF Las Vegas Raiders DST, Las Vegas Raiders

    The Raiders allowed the eighth-most points last season, but a lack of big plays dropped them to third-worst from a fantasy perspective.

    Most teams near the bottom completely overhauled their starting lineups, and Las Vegas spent heavily to do the same, though the additions don't look like difference-makers by PFF grade. Linebackers Devin White, Elandon Roberts and Jamal Adams each played more than 400 snaps last season and are now free agents. Cornerback Kyu Blu Kelly and defensive end Tyree Wilson are also gone.

    Las Vegas added four free agents with at least 300 snaps last season. The most notable is linebacker Nakobe Dean, who posted a 62.2 grade last season and a 72.9 mark over the past three. Quay Walker, Kwity Paye and Taron Johnson were also brought in to start. The Raiders then added five defenders in the first five rounds of the draft, though it's unclear whether any will start.

    Las Vegas should be slightly better with those additions, and one or two rookies could pay off immediately. Still, unlike other defenses that finished near the bottom last season, there isn't much reason to expect significant improvement right away.

    452. DEF New York Jets DST, New York Jets

    The Jets defense scored the fewest fantasy points last season at 47.0, notably 12 fewer than the second-worst and 28 fewer than the third-worst. They also traded their two best players, defensive tackle Quinnen Williams and cornerback Sauce Gardner, at midseason.

    New York let another eight veterans leave this offseason who played at least 100 snaps last year, though all eight graded below 64.0.

    The Jets made several additions on defense, bringing in eight players with 380 or more snaps last season. Defensive tackles T'Vondre Sweat and David Onyemata, linebacker Demario Davis and safety Minkah Fitzpatrick all posted grades of 79.0 or better. New York also used high draft picks on that side of the ball, including second overall pick David Bailey and second-round cornerback D'Angelo Ponds.

    This defense will be almost entirely new, so last season's numbers have little bearing on how 2026 plays out. With so many moving parts, a below-average unit is the reasonable expectation, though there is a chance it plays well if everything comes together.

    468. DEF Arizona Cardinals DST, Arizona Cardinals

    Arizona ranked among the bottom six teams in points allowed, yards allowed and fantasy points by a defense. They had no player with more than 600 snaps and a grade of 70.0 or better.

    The Cardinals retained more of their defense than most teams that finished near the bottom. Their losses include defensive tackles Calais Campbell and Dalvin Tomlinson, linebacker Akeem Davis-Gaither and safety Jalen Thompson, with Campbell and Thompson among their highest-graded defenders.

    The rest of the defenses near the bottom either spent significantly in free agency or leaned on the draft, and Arizona did neither. They added only three players with more than 500 snaps last season: safety Andrew Wingard, linebacker Jack Gibbens and defensive tackle Andrew Billings. None graded above 64.0.

    The rest of the defenses near the bottom either spent significantly in free agency or leaned on the draft, and Arizona did neither. They added only three players with more than 500 snaps last season: safety Andrew Wingard, linebacker Jack Gibbens and defensive tackle Andrew Billings. None graded above 64.0.

    498. QB Joe Milton III, Dallas Cowboys

    Milton was a 2024 sixth-round pick by the New England Patriots. He served as their third-string quarterback as a rookie and played nearly every snap in their Week 18 game against the Buffalo Bills. He received a 95.8 offensive grade in that game, which was the third-best by a quarterback in a game that season.

    He was traded to the Dallas Cowboys to be the primary backup and attempted 24 passes over four games, most of them coming in Week 18. This season, Dallas traded for Sam Howell to compete with Milton for the backup spot.

    If Milton wins the job, he is one of the better backup options, given the Cowboys' willingness to throw the ball and the talent surrounding him.
